<p> Ottawa Citizen - RSS Feedhttps : **28;3774;TOOLONG , 25 Feb 2020 14:22:00 +0000'It was like they were playing football with her ' : Hillel Lodge rebuked for refusing resident returned from hospitalhttp : **32;3804;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;3838;TOOLONG ... title= " Yuriy Hillyer poses for a photo in Ottawa Thursday Feb 6 , 2020 . Yuriy Hillyer 's mother , Maia Giller , was a resident of Hillel Lodge but the lodge refused to admit her back after she was released from an overnight hospital stay last September . She has since died and Yuriy has lodged complaints about the Lodge 's actions . " alt= " Yuriy Hillyer poses for a photo in Ottawa Thursday Feb 6 , 2020 . Yuriy Hillyer 's mother , Maia Giller , was a resident of Hillel Lodge but the lodge refused to admit her back after she was released from an overnight hospital stay last September . She has since died and Yuriy has lodged complaints about the Lodge 's actions . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;3874;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1555380 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s Ministry of Long-Term Care has issued a &amp;ldquo ; written notification&amp;rdquo ; against Hillel Lodge for &lt;a href= " https : **33;3904;TOOLONG ... to accept a resident who was returned home from hospital early one morning&lt;/a&gt; last September but did not require the lodge to take any corrective action as a result of the incident . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A written notification is the mildest form of rebuke the ministry has for long-term care homes that fail to comply with ministry policies . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The person is only identified as &amp;ldquo ; resident #001&amp;rdquo ; in the report , but the facts coincide with the story of Maia Giller , whose story was told in this newspaper earlier this month . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Giller , 88 , had advanced dementia and had lived at Hillel since 2011 . On the night of Sept. 17 , Giller was sent by ambulance to The Ottawa Hospital where she spent about nine hours and was seen by medical staff but not admitted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The hospital returned her to Hillel at 4 a.m. the next day by a patient transfer service , but the lodge refused to let her back inside . Giller&amp;rsquo ; s son , Yuriy Hillyer , who was with his mother at the time , said the nurse on duty locked the lodge door , leaving Giller on her gurney outside on the sidewalk with her attendants . She was taken back to the hospital&amp;rsquo ; s Civic Campus where she died 10 days later . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;3939;TOOLONG ... title= " Yuriy Hillyer poses holding photos of his mother Maia. " alt= " Yuriy Hillyer poses holding photos of his mother Maia. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;3975;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1555470 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This story was so terrible , &amp;rdquo ; Hillyer said Monday . &amp;ldquo ; It was hell for her in the hospital and in the lodge . It&amp;rsquo ; s like they were playing football with her , kicking her back and forth . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hillyer said he was told last week by the ministry that the report on his mother was available . The publicity around the case seemed to help his complaints be taken seriously , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They started to pay a little bit more attention , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; For three or four months it was like no one even looked at the file . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In her finding , the ministry&amp;rsquo ; s inspector found Hillel Lodge &amp;ldquo ; failed to ensure that staff and others involved in the different aspects of care collaborated with each other in the assessment of resident #001 so that their assessments were integrated , consistent with and complimented each other . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; On a specified date , resident #001 was readmitted to the home from a medical leave . On the same day , the resident returned to acute care , and according to documentation , the resident&amp;rsquo ; s substitute decision-makers ( SDMs ) agreed to this transfer . Several hours later , the resident was returned to the home . The resident was not readmitted to the home and was sent back to the hospital . Documentation from an Emergency Room Physician indicated that they had been trying to contact the home&amp;rsquo ; s management with no results . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The investigator found Hillel&amp;rsquo ; s staff &amp;ldquo ; did not collaborate with the staff at the hospital when the resident returned from hospital&amp;rdquo ; and that the registered nurse on duty &amp;ldquo ; did not attempt to communicate with the hospital . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The incident was one of four incidents of non-compliance the ministry found concerning the care of Giller and several other patients , and the only one that didn&amp;rsquo ; t involve a request for a &amp;ldquo ; voluntary plan of correction&amp;rdquo ; the next highest level of censure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The other written notifications cited Hillel staff for failing to notice when &amp;ldquo ; &lt;span style= " font-family : -apple-system , BlinkMacSystemFont , ' Segoe UI ' , Roboto , Oxygen-Sans , Ubuntu , Cantarell , ' Helvetica Neue ' , sans-serif ; " &gt;resident #001 ? had stopped eating and drinking , and again when she had begun to lose weight . Aides at the lodge also failed to properly record nutrition intake for several residents , the investigator found . In the case of two residents , including " resident #001 " , medication was n't administered as their doctors had prescribed . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The report ordered the lodge to prepare written plans to improve nutrition monitoring , track residents&amp;rsquo ; weight changes and to correct the way drugs are administered to one of the residents . The investigation took place over seven days in January . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In an emailed statement , Hillel Lodge executive director Ted Cohen said he could not comment on specific incidents for privacy reasons . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Hillel Lodge has a proud tradition of quality care . In particular , Hillel Lodge is known for excellence in palliative and end-of-life care , &amp;rdquo ; Cohen said . &amp;ldquo ; As part of our ongoing quality improvement activities , we ensure that any issues that arise related to the care of our residents or the services we provide are quickly addressed 24 hours a day , seven days a week by our highly trained staff . In all instances , Hillel Lodge immediately addresses any issues identified in written notifications and/or voluntary plans of correction through its quality improvement activities . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The incident is still being investigated by Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s Patient Ombudsman . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **30;4005;TOOLONG " **37;4037;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Twitter.com/getBAC &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4076;TOOLONG ... being told to prepare for a possible novel coronavirus pandemic&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4111;TOOLONG ... ombudsman report highlights need for better communication in health care&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4146;TOOLONG ... police arrest Mohawk protesters at CN blockade&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Blair CrawfordTue , 25 Feb 2020 14:22:00 **45;4181;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsSimpson : Ontario hospitals are not prepared for coronavirushttp : **29;4228;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;4259;TOOLONG ... title= " Workers wearing protective suits walk away from the quarantine cruise ship Diamond Princess south of Tokyo , Feb. 10. " alt= " Workers wearing protective suits walk away from the quarantine cruise ship Diamond Princess south of Tokyo , Feb. 10. " data-portal-copyright= " Kim Kyung Hoon " **28;4295;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1558277 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Recently , there have been several statements concerning how well prepared we are in Ontario for the novel coronavirus . A statement from Christine Elliott , deputy premier and minister of Health , notes that &amp;ldquo ; efforts by our dedicated public health professionals are keeping us safe . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While there is no doubt we are much more prepared for this novel coronavirus than we were for SARS , there is a critical weakness in our health care system that is not being addressed . The reality is that if we start seeing more cases of coronavirus in Ontario , it is likely that our health care system will fail . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This critical weakness is our hospital overcrowding. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; I am a front-line emergency room physician working in Perth , and in no way are we prepared for a surge in sick patients . Two weeks ago , our hospital was so overcrowded that eight out of the 10 beds in our emergency department were taken up by patients admitted to hospital who could not be admitted to the ward because there were no beds . We had two total beds available to treat our patients , of whom there are usually 70 or 80 per day . We were treating people in hallways , in closets and in unused offices . If several sick patients with a highly contagious illness came to our department , there would be absolutely nowhere to put them . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is no capacity in the system right now to manage a crisis . In fact , most hospitals currently have negative capacity as many are running with over 100 per cent of their beds being filled . If we had a sudden surge of ill patients , we would be unable to manage them appropriately or safely . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; I can say with confidence that this issue is not due to the hard work of our nurses , physicians and the allied health professionals including hospital administrators who are working overtime to try to manage the overburdened system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This issue is entirely due to the lack of long-term beds available . During the time we had so many beds taken up in our emergency department , we had 30 patients ( out of 85 total beds ) admitted to hospital who are designated &amp;ldquo ; ALC&amp;rdquo ; ( alternative level of care ) &amp;ndash ; patients who have no acute medical issues but can not be discharged home safely and are awaiting placement in nursing homes or similar facilities . Because of this , we are over-capacity . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Furthermore , in the event of an outbreak of coronavirus , these &amp;ldquo ; ALC&amp;rdquo ; patients are sitting ducks . They often have significant health concerns and would be highly vulnerable to a serious viral illness . It does not make sense to keep these vulnerable patients in hospital and surrounded by other sick , contagious people . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; China has responded to the crisis by building emergency hospitals . We could likewise increase our ability to handle a crisis &amp;ndash ; by building emergency nursing homes . Or by changing the rules around patients designated ALC to be able to discharge them from hospital faster . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Our system is highly vulnerable right now and without creative changes we may find ourselves overwhelmed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **26;4325;TOOLONG . James Simpson&lt;/strong&gt; is a rural family , emergency and hospitalist physician practising in Perth . &lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN CITIZEN OPINIONS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4353;TOOLONG ... Fixing transit in Ottawa -- it starts with governance&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4388;TOOLONG ... On climate change -- or any other issue -- let 's listen to all generations&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4423;TOOLONG ... letters : On coronavirus and on Ottawa 's ' ByWash ' &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Dr. James SimpsonThu , 13 Feb 2020 19:47:05 **45;4458;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildSeniors'Respected , included and empowered : ' Ottawa Public Health releases Aging Well Planhttp : **36;4505;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;4543;TOOLONG ... title= " Ottawa Public Health and Ottawa Search and Rescue were checking on seniors as they were going through a building on Viewmount Drive in Ottawa Monday Sept 24 , 2018. " alt= " Ottawa Public Health and Ottawa Search and Rescue were checking on seniors as they were going through a building on Viewmount Drive in Ottawa Monday Sept 24 , 2018. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;4579;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1557555 " /&gt; &lt;div id= " details-body " class= " details-body clear clearfix " &gt; &lt;p&gt;Ottawa 's growing ranks of residents 55 and older want the city to be a compassionate and friendly place for aging people . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;They want support for caregivers and information about preventative health care in areas such as brain health and physical activity , says Ottawa Public Health 's new plan for the city 's aging population . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;And respect , too . Older adults say they want to feel heard and valued . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;In 2018 , 28 per cent of Ottawa residents were aged 55 and older . In 15 years , one in three will be 55 or older . In 2018 , two per cent of the city 's population was over the age of 85 . By 2035 , it will be four per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;OPH 's update to its aging strategy , &lt;a href= " https : **33;4609;TOOLONG ... Well Plan 2020-2022&lt;/a&gt; , was to be presented Monday at the Ottawa Board of Health . The plan is Ottawa Public Health 's first proposed aging strategy since 2012 . That strategy focused on fall prevention . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Older adults are interested in developing and maintaining optimal physical , mental and social well-being , said the report on the Aging Well Plan . Priorities were developed with consultations with 1,700 older people , including francophone , rural , ethnocultural , Indigenous , isolated , and LGBTQ+ residents . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;The four prongs to the proposed strategy include : &lt;/p&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; An age-friendly city . Older people said they want to stay connected to people that are important to them , stay healthy and active even at the oldest ages and have appropriate support for those who can no longer look after themselves . The built and social environment can help older people to live healthier lives , by making the healthy choice the easier choice , said the strategy . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; Aging without ageism . The strategy will continue to engage older adults , ensuring their voices to be heard and valued , said OPH . The strategy said the health unit will seek to better understand their interests , needs and issues and &amp;ldquo ; work toward a community in which older adults and their caregivers are respected , included and empowered . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; Supports for caregivers . Older adults and their caregivers are having difficulty navigating the health care system and are waiting too long for care , according to the report . OPH said it recognizes that the health system can be difficult to navigate and will apply a &amp;ldquo ; caregiver lens&amp;rdquo ; to the&amp;nbsp ; information and resources it develops . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; Better access to information , public health programs , and services . Older adults said they want programs for all segments of the aging population , including rural and non-English speaking residents . The consultations identified a need for services and education on physical activity , brain health and increased support for caregivers. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The strategy is to mesh with similar strategies at the federal , provincial and local levels , including Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s &lt;a href= " https : **33;4644;TOOLONG ... Adult Plan&lt;/a&gt; , which counted grading sidewalk curbs to eliminate tripping hazards , installing automatic door openers , ramps and grab bars to city facilities and adjusting crossing and signal times at 53 busy intersections among its accomplishments. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4679;TOOLONG ... announces new afternoon drive host&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;4714;TOOLONG ... orders city to provide clarity on LRT payments&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " http : **34;4749;TOOLONG ... at contaminated former RCMP training site urged to reach out to CSIS&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Joanne LauciusMon , 10 Feb 2020 21:36:34 **45;4785;TOOLONG ... angry after Ottawa care home locks door on aged mother , refuses to let her back from hospitalhttp : **32;4832;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;4866;TOOLONG ... title= " Yuriy Hillyer poses for a photo in Ottawa Thursday Feb 6 , 2020 . Yuriy Hillyer 's mother , Maia Giller , was a resident of Hillel Lodge but the lodge refused to admit her back after she was released from an overnight hospital stay last September . She has since died and Yuriy has lodged complaints about the Lodge 's actions . " alt= " Yuriy Hillyer poses for a photo in Ottawa Thursday Feb 6 , 2020 . Yuriy Hillyer 's mother , Maia Giller , was a resident of Hillel Lodge but the lodge refused to admit her back after she was released from an overnight hospital stay last September . She has since died and Yuriy has lodged complaints about the Lodge 's actions . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;4902;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1555380 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ministry of Long-Term Care and Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s patient ombudsman are investigating why Hillel Lodge long-term care home blocked one of its longtime residents from returning after she was sent back from the hospital at 4 a.m. one day last September . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Now her son is going public with his complaint , in the hope it prevents something similar happening to another frail and vulnerable person like his mother . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I told the Rabbi , prayer is good , but you also have to act , &amp;rdquo ; said Yuriy Hillyer , who keeps a candle burning on his table beside a photo of his mother , Maia Giller. &amp;ldquo ; I decided I would not be shy . I would show the problem . Without action , nothing will change . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Giller had advanced dementia and had been living at Hillel Lodge since 2011 , said Hillyer , who was with his mother the morning of Sept. 17 when a nurse from Hillel Lodge locked the door , leaving Giller outside on a stretcher with attendants from the patient transfer service that had delivered her . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Giller , who had survived famine as a child in Ukraine and barely escaped the massacre of Ukrainian Jews by the Nazis in the Second World War , died 10 days later at The Ottawa Hospital . She was 88. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;4932;TOOLONG ... title= " Yuriy Hillyer holds a photo of his mother , Maia , who died Sept. 28 at the Ottawa Hospital . She was 88. " alt= " Yuriy Hillyer holds a photo of his mother , Maia , who died Sept. 28 at the Ottawa Hospital . She was 88. " data-portal-copyright= " Blair Crawford " **28;4968;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1557066 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I was in shock , &amp;rdquo ; said Hillyer , who was a medical doctor in Ukraine before coming to Canada in 2013 to care for his mother . &amp;ldquo ; The nurse comes from Hillel Lodge and she talked very rudely . She says she has an order from the management of Hillel Lodge to not accept my mother back to the Hillel Lodge . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; She did not check on my mom . She did not look at her . She did not check her pulse or her vital signs , &amp;rdquo ; said Hillyer , who filed complaints with the Ministry of Health and Long-term Care and the Patient Ombudsman , both of which have said they are investigating the incident . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Giller&amp;rsquo ; s condition had deteriorated over the summer and she required repeated hospitalization for dehydration and constipation . She was admitted to The Ottawa Hospital&amp;rsquo ; s Civic campus on Aug. 30 and stayed 17 days before being sent back to the lodge at around 11 a.m. on Sept. 16 . Hillyer visited her at the lodge that day and spoon fed her a fortified soup for lunch , which she enjoyed . Giller slept that afternoon and Hillyer went home but was called by the lodge around 7:30 p.m. and told his mother was being sent back to the hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hillyer went to the lodge and rode with her in the ambulance to the Civic and stayed with her until 11 p.m . Medical staff checked her vital signs and found them normal , and found no problems with her vital organs , he said . Hillyer went home to sleep , leaving a caregiver he paid out of his own pocket to stay with her overnight . The caregiver called at 3 a.m. to say the hospital was sending Giller back to Hillel Lodge by Priority Patient Transfer service . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When Hillyer arrived at the lodge just after 4:30 a.m. , his mother and the attendants from Priority had already been waiting outside on the sidewalk for half an hour because no one had answered the doorbell . When a nurse did answer the door , she began arguing with the attendants . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; These guys from Priority said to her ( the Hillel nurse ) , &amp;lsquo ; We have to tell you that this is illegal action to not accept a resident , to not admit her to her place of residence , ' &amp;rdquo ; Hillyer said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; She answered that he&amp;rsquo ; s not her employer , he doesn&amp;rsquo ; t pay her wage and she isn&amp;rsquo ; t going to lose her job because of this situation . She said , &amp;lsquo ; I will not admit her back to the Hillel Lodge . ' &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hillyer said the nurse went back inside and locked the doors . Eventually Priority Patient Transfer took Giller back to the Civic , where she was admitted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A spokesman for Priority Patient Transfer said he could not discuss the case because of privacy concerns , but confirmed he&amp;rsquo ; d been talked to by Ottawa police about what happened . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ted Cohen , chief executive of Hillel Lodge , also declined to comment , citing privacy reasons . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The case that you are referencing is a complex one , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When a resident requires hospital care , Hillel Lodge prepares the resident for a safe transfer , &amp;rdquo ; Cohen said in an emailed statement . &amp;ldquo ; During their stay at the hospital , the Lodge receives updates on their progress . Once notified that the resident will be discharged from the hospital , appropriate arrangements are made to welcome the resident back to Hillel Lodge . In rare instances , due to the complex care requirements of a resident and/or to honour the explicit wishes of the resident/family , there may be a need for the resident to again be safely transferred to the hospital for further followup where they can receive care . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;4998;TOOLONG ... title= " Yuriy Hillyer with photos of his late mother Maia Giller. " alt= " Yuriy Hillyer with photos of his late mother Maia Giller. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;5034;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1555470 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jane Meadus , a lawyer with the Advocate Centre for the Elderly ( ACE ) in Toronto , said such a long-term care home&amp;rsquo ; s refusing to accept a patient back from hospital would be &amp;ldquo ; extremely unusual . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The home , under the law , would not have the authority to prevent a person from returning , &amp;rdquo ; Meadus said . They can&amp;rsquo ; t just say , &amp;lsquo ; You can&amp;rsquo ; t come back . &amp;rsquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s health care . You are entitled to be there . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus wasn&amp;rsquo ; t familiar with the Hillel Lodge complaint , but said ACE had been involved in another case where a long-term care home had refused to take back a morbidly obese man whom the home felt it couldn&amp;rsquo ; t care for . The Ministry of Health and Long-Term Care agreed that the home had acted improperly , but the man died before the complaint was taken further , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Long-term care homes must hold a resident&amp;rsquo ; s bed for 30 days if the person is sent to hospital for an acute illness and for 60 days if they&amp;rsquo ; re admitted for mental health reasons . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are some criteria for discharging people . If a home no longer wants someone to be there because there&amp;rsquo ; s a safety issue in the home , for example , &amp;rdquo ; Meadus said . &amp;ldquo ; But there&amp;rsquo ; s a process they have to go through . They can&amp;rsquo ; t just say , &amp;lsquo ; You can&amp;rsquo ; t come back . &amp;rsquo ; That&amp;rsquo ; s not appropriate . Just refusing to accept someone to return , in my experience , would be very unusual . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus was also critical of the hospital&amp;rsquo ; s decision to send a patient back in the middle of the night . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There is a really good question as to whether hospitals should be sending someone back at four in the morning . Homes are really short-handed at night and it would not be the safest time to return someone . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Maia Giller was born in Kyiv , Ukraine in 1931 . Her mother was a factory worker who fled the city two days before it was overrun by the Nazis during the Second World War , narrowly escaping the massacre of more than 33,000 of Kyiv&amp;rsquo ; s Jews in the ravine of Babi Yar . When they returned to the ruined city after the war , the lived in holes dug in the ground until new homes could be built from the rubble . Giller worked as an economist in Ukraine until she immigrated to Canada in 1994. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As Giller&amp;rsquo ; s condition deteriorated at Hillel Lodge , she became immobile and had to be lifted to and from her bed to a wheelchair . She lost the ability to speak . Hillyer acknowledges she was a difficult person to care for as her dementia advanced , hitting and spitting on staff at the lodge . In turn , he complained to the lodge about the care she was receiving and pleaded with staff to understand that his mother could not control her outbursts. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Once admitted to The Ottawa Hospital and unable to be nourished and hydrated except by medical intervention , Giller died on Sept. 28 with Hillyer at her bedside . He keeps the cloth she was holding at the time in his apartment in front of her photograph . He&amp;rsquo ; s bitter the hospital refused to allow him to try to feed her himself , as he had done several times before when she&amp;rsquo ; d been critically ill. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Though some may think those with advanced dementia should be left to die , he says , his mother still showed enjoyment despite the isolation of her condition . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I would take her outside and she would sit in the wheelchair and she liked to look at the birds , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; She could not talk , but I understand . I understand that these people have a different world . We don&amp;rsquo ; t know about this world because these people don&amp;rsquo ; t talk to us . We don&amp;rsquo ; t have communication . But they have their own world and we have to respect this world . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; If he could afford it , he would hire a lawyer to help his fight , he said . He decided to go public with his complaint to try to save another family from going through a similar experience . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; One day this could be you . It could be me. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5064;TOOLONG ... @postmedia.com&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5099;TOOLONG ... &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5134;TOOLONG ... skirts , disaster relief and highlighting medals as ' bling ' might bring more women into the military&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5169;TOOLONG ... paramedics to get new powers to treat palliative patients at home&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5204;TOOLONG ... of mankind ' : Coronavirus deaths top SARS as China returns to work&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Blair CrawfordMon , 10 Feb 2020 12:16:26 **45;5239;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsOttawa paramedics to get new powers to treat palliative patients at homehttp : **36;5286;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;5324;TOOLONG ... title= " Ottawa paramedics have waited more than 32,000 hours waiitng to offload patients to emergnecy rooms in he first seven months of 2019 , says a new report . " alt= " Ottawa paramedics have waited more than 32,000 hours waiitng to offload patients to emergnecy rooms in he first seven months of 2019 , says a new report . " data-portal-copyright= " Ottawa Paramedic Service " **28;5360;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1457423 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Starting in April , Ottawa paramedics will be able to treat palliative care patients in their own homes instead of bringing them to an emergency room for pain relief and symptom management , this newspaper has learned . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The one-year pilot project is part of the provincial government&amp;rsquo ; s push to keep people out of crowded hospitals and in their own homes when possible . It was to be announced in Ottawa by Ontario Health Minister Christine Elliott on Monday . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; By providing palliative care patients and their families with more options to receive treatment in their home or community , we&amp;rsquo ; re improving patients&amp;rsquo ; access to the right care in the right place , a key pillar of our comprehensive plan to end hallway health care , &amp;rdquo ; said Elliott . &amp;ldquo ; This new innovative model of care will help reduce unnecessary hospital visits to crowded emergency departments . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The pilot is one of a number aimed at reducing hallway medicine and creating new treatment options . The program also addresses a number of ongoing issues within the health system and among individual patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hospitals in Ottawa , like many across the province , operate close to or over capacity at all times . That means emergency rooms are often overcrowded because hospital beds are fully occupied and patients are waiting to be admitted . As a result , some hospitals are forced to cancel elective surgery to free up hospital beds. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At The Ottawa Hospital in recent years , crowding has meant that paramedics are often left waiting with patients who need to be admitted , which takes ambulances off the road . During the first eight months of 2019 no ambulances were available for a total of five days because paramedics were tied up on calls or waiting to off-load patients at the hospital , according to a City of Ottawa report . That situation has continued , say city emergency officials . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The pilot project would take some pressure off hospital emergency departments . Currently , under provincial law , paramedics are required to take palliative care patients in need of pain or symptom relief who call 911 to an emergency department for treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The pilot allows eligible patients to have the option of being treated at home after calling 911 . The province&amp;rsquo ; s Ambulance Act and Health Insurance Act will be amended to allow for the change . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ottawa paramedics will receive special training to assess the patient&amp;rsquo ; s condition when they arrive . They will be able to administer medication for pain relief , shortness of breath , hallucinations , agitation , nausea , vomiting and congested breathing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Paramedics would then send a referral back to the patient&amp;rsquo ; s palliative team for followup. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The pilot project will also address the fact that most palliative patients in Ontario are unable to die at home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; According to a 2018 study from the Institute for Clinical Evaluation Sciences , the majority of Ontario residents would prefer to die at home , but nearly 70 per cent died in hospitals or long-term care facilities . Lack of support to remain at home was a factor . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The province is planning other pilot projects involving paramedics that would enable them to take certain patients to mental health treatment or addictions crisis centres rather than hospital emergency rooms . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are some models in the region in which paramedics provide non-traditional services . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Since 2015 Ottawa paramedics have been able to divert vulnerable homeless patients directly to the Shepherds of Good Hope instead of a hospital . And in Renfrew County , community paramedics provide primary care to some complex patients in their own homes , including palliative patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a statement , Elliott said the new model of care will ensure palliative care patients &amp;ldquo ; get the proper assistance , dignity and respect they deserve . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The pilot project is part of the province&amp;rsquo ; s push to provide &amp;ldquo ; right care in the right place . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;5390;TOOLONG " **34;5419;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5455;TOOLONG ... Cruises confirms new Canadian coronovirus case aboard Diamond Princess&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5490;TOOLONG ... ' I do n't want to live like this , ' says woman denied assisted death&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;5525;TOOLONG ... skirts , disaster relief and highlighting medals as ' bling ' might bring more women into the military&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneMon , 10 Feb 2020 11:50:14 **45;5560;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsWhat can hold back the hands of time ? Scientists have looked at these five treatmentshttp : **30;5607;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;5639;TOOLONG ... title= " " Gene Editing . " " alt= " " Gene Editing . " " data-portal-copyright= " Mike Faille " **28;5675;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1546798 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Scientists have been researching ways to increase a human&amp;rsquo ; s healthspan &amp;mdash ; and possible lifespan &amp;mdash ; for decades . Here are things they have considered , and what they have learned . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Rapamycin &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt; What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; First identified in the 1970s in a soil sample from Easter Island , this FDA-approved drug is used to suppress the immune system in transplant patients . Counterintuitively , it also appears to boost immune function in healthy animals . Rapamycin may reduce inflammation , which increases with age . One study found that rapamycin rejuvenated the hearts of old mice . After treatment , there were significant improvements to cardiac function . Research with mice has also found that even when they where administered rapamycin late in life , the mice still had improvements to lifespan and their healthspan. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict : &lt;/strong&gt; Geroscientist Matt Kaeberlein said rapamycin shows the most promise of any drug that targets aging , in his opinion , although he doesn&amp;rsquo ; t necessarily think rapamycin will increase lifespan by 20 to 30 years on average in people . &amp;ldquo ; Although it might at the right dose . I do think there&amp;rsquo ; s reason to believe that rapamycin could improve several age-related health problems , including boosting immune function and protecting against dementia , cancers , and heart disease . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But critics say it is unclear what the downstream effects could be . &amp;ldquo ; If you take a compound like that for a few decades , there may be downstream effect&amp;nbsp ; &amp;mdash ; a small epidemic of lymphoma , perhaps , &amp;rdquo ; said geriatrician Dr. Peter Boling . While it is approved to prevent rejection in organ transplant patients , it is not approved to treat aging . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are also questions about side effects , which may include susceptibility to infection and the possibility of developing lymphoma. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We still don&amp;rsquo ; t know about all of the hidden dangers . As hopeful as we might be , we have to be aware of the limitations , &amp;rdquo ; said Boling. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Metformin &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; This drug has been prescribed to millions of people to treat diabetes . Among other effects , it decreases insulin levels . It also appears to target biological mechanisms related to aging . Its anti-aging effects may be linked to its influences on metabolic and cellular processes associated with inflammation . It has been linked to lifespan extension and protection against age-related diseases in animal models . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict : &lt;/strong&gt; The speculation in scientific circles has been that many of the drugs used to treat early-stage chronic disease may be effective , at least in part , because they target aging itself . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Metformin is an interesting case , &amp;rdquo ; said Kaeberlein . It clearly increases survival in people with diabetes and likely would do the same for pre-diabetics and others with significant metabolic disease , which represents a growing percentage of adults , he said . However , it&amp;rsquo ; s less clear whether metformin will be beneficial in people who are metabolically healthy and exercise regularly . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The studies in mice suggest that metformin is different from rapamycin , in the sense that metformin only increases lifespan by a very small amount and in some cases has been reported to have no effect or even shortens lifespan in mice . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The FDA has accepted a clinical trial , Targeting Aging with Metformin , known as TAME , which will test whether metformin can delay the onset of age-related diseases and conditions including cancer , cardiovascular disease and Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s disease . The goal of the trial , which is estimated to cost $55 million , is not to help people live to be 120 years old but &amp;ldquo ; to add health to our years , &amp;rdquo ; said the American Federation for Aging Research . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Like metformin , several promising drugs show great potential and await trials . If successful , the TAME trial would give the pharmaceutical industry impetus to advance these drugs and transform aging from a period of sickness to a time of extended vitality . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Caloric restriction &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; A dietary regimen that reduces calories while maintaining nutrients . Research in lab animals , which started almost a century ago , has shown that it is possible to delay age-related diseases in mice and rats through dietary and caloric restriction , but results have varied . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A parallel study of two groups of rhesus monkeys , one at the University of Wisconsin and the other at the U.S. National Institute on Aging , looked at the life-prolonging and health effects of a calorie-restricted diet . The researchers found that the Wisconsin monkeys on a diet with 30 per cent fewer calories than the control group survived to about 28 years for males and about 30 years for females &amp;mdash ; a couple of years above average for monkeys in captivity . Calorie-restricted monkeys in both groups showed fewer age-related health conditions compared to the control monkeys . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some &amp;ldquo ; biohackers&amp;rdquo ; &amp;mdash ; people who use biological interventions to optimize health &amp;mdash ; have embarked on caloric restriction diets , with the goal of living longer . Entrepreneur Dave Asprey , author of Bulletproof Diet , has said he plans to live to be 180 and is hacking his own biology with a number of interventions , including supplements and a strict diet . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict so far : &lt;/strong&gt; The rhesus monkey study also produced some contradictory findings . The National Institute on Aging&amp;rsquo ; s study found that while the dieting monkeys showed improved health , they didn&amp;rsquo ; t necessarily live longer than the control monkeys . It&amp;rsquo ; s not clear why there was a difference between the two groups of monkeys , although they were fed different diets and had different feeding times and access to food . The ages at which they dieted may also have come into play . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There is no compelling evidence caloric restriction will work with people , &amp;rdquo ; said Doug Gray , a molecular biologist with an interest in the role of protein homeostasis in aging and diseases of aging at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; If this sounds like a depressing route to extreme longevity , even scientists agree . Noted one researcher : &amp;ldquo ; Life might seem longer , but it wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t necessarily be longer . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Gene editing &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;Genome editing , or gene editing , is a type of genetic engineering in which DNA is inserted , deleted , modified or **34;5705;TOOLONG . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Last February , for example , the journal Nature Medicine reported on a therapy using &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;CRISPR-Cas9 genome editing technology aimed at **26;5741;TOOLONG suppressing rapid aging in mice with the rare genetic disorder progeria , which causes accelerated aging . According to the report , two months after the therapy , the progeria mice were stronger and more active and had better heart health . Their lifespan also increased by about 25 per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict so far : &lt;/strong&gt; Not ready for prime time , said Boling . Even though we have successfully mapped the human genome , and it&amp;rsquo ; s technically possible to remove a gene sequence and replace it , we have yet to successfully edit genes in a complex organism . Many people don&amp;rsquo ; t want to eat food containing genetically-modified organisms , let alone become one , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t think we have a clear handle on a special gene sequence that would essentially change the aging process . I would want to see if we could do it in a limited and focused way without undue downstream consequences . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;5769;TOOLONG ... title= " In 2017 , researchers at Stanford University medical school reported that injections of blood plasma from donors between the ages of 18 and 30 showed promise for helping human Alzheimer 's patients with mild to moderate symptoms regain some ability to perform basic daily tasks . " alt= " In 2017 , researchers at Stanford University medical school reported that injections of blood plasma from donors between the ages of 18 and 30 showed promise for helping human Alzheimer 's patients with mild to moderate symptoms regain some ability to perform basic daily tasks . " data-portal-copyright= " Colleen De Neve Colleen De Neve " **28;5805;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1546888 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Young blood &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; There have been a number of studies that looked at whether giving old mice blood from young mice could reverse some signs of aging . Even the researchers admitted it sounded creepy , but the results were intriguing . One study at the University of California , Berkeley , published in 2018 , found that &lt;span class= " jsx-671803276 " &gt;old mice showed benefits , including better muscle repair , from receiving blood from young mice . But young mice who received old blood had negative effects , including performing poorly on a strength test . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2017 , researchers at Stanford University medical school reported that injections of blood plasma from donors between the ages of 18 and 30 showed promise for helping human Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s patients with mild to moderate symptoms regain some ability to perform basic daily tasks , such as remembering to take their medications or preparing meals . The results were based on reports from the patients and their caregivers . The researchers cautioned that more research is needed , since the trial involved only nine patients who had received plasma infusions and nine who had received a placebo saline infusion. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict so far : &lt;/strong&gt; Don&amp;rsquo ; t try it . In a statement last February , the FDA said it had &amp;ldquo ; significant public health concerns&amp;rdquo ; about establishments that offer infusions of plasma from young donors to treat conditions ranging from normal aging and memory loss to Parkinson&amp;rsquo ; s disease , multiple sclerosis , Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s disease , and PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Simply put , we&amp;rsquo ; re concerned that some patients are being preyed upon by unscrupulous actors touting treatments of plasma from young donors as cures and remedies . Such treatments have no proven clinical benefits for the uses for which these clinics are advertising them and are potentially harmful , &amp;rdquo ; warned the FDA. &amp;ldquo ; There are reports of bad actors charging thousands of dollars for infusions that are unproven and not guided by evidence from adequate and well-controlled trials . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;5835;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;5877;TOOLONG ... this rare disease could help treat aspects of **26;5912;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;5940;TOOLONG ... would you do with 20 extra years ? Scientists are close to extending a human 's ' healthy lifespan ' &lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; Joanne LauciusThu , 30 Jan 2020 13:17:45 **45;5975;TOOLONG ... would you do with 20 extra years ? Scientists are close to extending a human 's ' healthy lifespan'http : **31;6022;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;6055;TOOLONG ... title= " " alt= " " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;6091;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1549927 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Twenty years from now , a 60-year-old might be able to buy a drug that will extend healthy life by a decade or two . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The treatment might be taken over a period of six weeks or so . The end result ? A boost to the immune system and protection against a host of age-related diseases , including heart disease , cancer and dementia . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This kind of intervention wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t be the fountain of youth &amp;mdash ; it won&amp;rsquo ; t turn back the clock and make that 60-year-old a 16-year-old again . It wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t offer eternal life . But it would produce an extended &amp;ldquo ; healthspan&amp;rdquo ; &amp;mdash ; extra years spent free of chronic age-related disease and disability . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At least , this is one version of the near future . Some , like gerobiologist Matt Kaeberlein , believe that an extended healthspan is a likely near-future scenario &amp;mdash ; and policy makers , governments and the medical profession should be prepared for it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It has been done with animals like worms , fruit flies and mice . There have even been some promising early results using a drug called rapamycin in a study involving pet dogs . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Whether or not it will actually happen with people is still debatable . But the idea and repercussions around it are real enough to warrant an entire issue of the journal Public Policy &amp;amp ; Aging Report , which asked the question : Is aging a disease ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;6121;TOOLONG ... title= " Chinese 104-year-old twins , Cao Daqiao ( senior , R ) and Cao Xiaoqiao talk at home in Weifang , east China 's Shandong province on November 29 , 2009 . According to the Shanghai Guinness World Records , this twin sisters , who were born in 1905 , were the oldest living twins in the world as of 2009. " alt= " Chinese 104-year-old twins , Cao Daqiao ( senior , R ) and Cao Xiaoqiao talk at home in Weifang , east China 's Shandong province on November 29 , 2009 . According to the Shanghai Guinness World Records , this twin sisters , who were born in 1905 , were the oldest living twins in the world as of 2009. " data-portal-copyright= " STR " **28;6157;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1549939 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Kaeberlein , a professor at the University of Washington who studies the biological mechanisms of aging , is asking for nothing less than a paradigm shift in the medical approach to the diseases associated with aging . He thinks it&amp;rsquo ; s reasonable to expect there will be products on the market within the next 20 years that could extend healthy life , although it will take a runway of several decades to prove it works . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is a much more effective approach toward health than the traditional medical approach of waiting until people are sick and trying to cure their disease . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As it stands , physicians treat individual diseases . The risks for a number of diseases , including many types of cancer , heart disease , diabetes , Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s and neurological disorders like Parkinson&amp;rsquo ; s disease , increase with age . The biggest risk factor in heart disease , for example , isn&amp;rsquo ; t smoking , too many cheeseburgers or some other environmental cause . It&amp;rsquo ; s age . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One estimate suggests that curing cancer or heart disease would increase life expectancy for only three or four years and curing both cancer and heart disease would increase life expectancy by less than 10 years because the risk of other kinds of diseases continue to increase exponentially . If aging could be slowed down , then life expectancy could be increased by two or three decades . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Modern medicine has gotten pretty good at keeping sick people alive , but we are not very good at curing age-related disease , &amp;rdquo ; said Kaeberlein. &amp;ldquo ; By treating one disease at a time , we haven&amp;rsquo ; t done anything to prevent all of the other age-related functional declines from happening in that person . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;6187;TOOLONG ... title= " Christian Mortensen , 114-years-old , smokes a cigar at his home in the Aldersly Retirement Community in San Rafael , CA in 1997 . Mortensen was the oldest man alive and celebrated his 115th birthday 16 August , 1997 . AFP PHOTO/JOHN G. MABANGLO " alt= " Christian Mortensen , 114-years-old , smokes a cigar at his home in the Aldersly Retirement Community in San Rafael , CA in 1997 . Mortensen was the oldest man alive and celebrated his 115th birthday 16 August , 1997 . AFP PHOTO/JOHN G. MABANGLO " data-portal-copyright= " JOHN G. MABANGLO " **28;6223;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1549935 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; By holding back aging , it is possible to hold back not just one disease , but a whole bunch of them . Age-related chronic diseases are taking an increasing toll on the world economy . One 2017 analysis estimated that half of the global health burden among adults can be attributed to age-related diseases . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I think you can make an argument that the extended period of time many older people live with multiple comorbidities currently is likely a direct result of the traditional medical approach of treating individual diseases , rather than treating biological aging . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Others are skeptical that an effective intervention to modify aging is just around the corner . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I know of no current intervention I would be willing to place a bet on , &amp;rdquo ; said Douglas Gray , a senior s &lt;span id= " mainContentlblContent " &gt;cientist at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute and professor of biochemistry , microbiology and immunology at the University of Ottawa . " The motivations are genuine and admirable . I just do n't buy it . " &lt;br&gt;&lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Researcher Jane Rylett , whose lab at Western University investigates the mechanisms regulating chemical communication in the nervous system , doesn&amp;rsquo ; t believe there is a single silver bullet for aging . What might work is a combination of approaches , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Never say never . But I don&amp;rsquo ; t see anything on the horizon that would signal something imminent that would allow us to prolong life for , say , 20 years , &amp;rdquo ; said Rylett , who is the scientific director for the Institute of Aging at the Canadian Institutes of Health Research . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Even if there was a drug targeted at aging , it would affect different people in different ways . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are few hurdles to be overcome . One is research funding that recognizes biological aging as a risk factor in a disease . While biological aging accounts for more than 90 per cent of the risk of developing Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s , for example , only a fraction of one per cent of the Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s research dollars spent in the U.S. is allocated to research on the role of aging in developing Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s . To turn the tide on the disease , more resources need to be spent on understanding how aging works , Kaeberlein argues . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;6253;TOOLONG ... title= " In this Aug. 14 , 2018 file photo , Dr. William Burke goes over a PET brain scan at Banner Alzheimers Institute in Phoenix . " alt= " In this Aug. 14 , 2018 file photo , Dr. William Burke goes over a PET brain scan at Banner Alzheimers Institute in Phoenix . " data-portal-copyright= " Matt York " **28;6289;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1549933 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The other major hurdle is regulatory . Drugs approved by the FDA are approved for an &amp;ldquo ; indication&amp;rdquo ; &amp;mdash ; that is , the drug treats a named disease . Most of the promising work on aging so far has been with animals , and not very complex animals at that . Translating these findings to people means clinical trials . Some contend that aging has to be recognized as a disease so it can be targeted by a drug aimed at treating it . It&amp;rsquo ; s worth noting , however , that some drugs have long been approved to prevent diseases . Vaccines are an example . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Kaeberlein doesn&amp;rsquo ; t think we should be trying to gain acceptance for the idea that aging is a disease . That&amp;rsquo ; s a semantic argument that distracts from the more important conversation , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; What&amp;rsquo ; s more important is to get people to understand that biological aging is a modifiable process . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For some , the idea that aging is a disease is absurd and potentially dangerous . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The thing that excludes aging from being a disease is its universality , &amp;rdquo ; said Gray . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some animals may live extraordinarily long lives . The naked mole rat , a hairless burrowing rodent native to East Africa which can live more than 30 years &amp;mdash ; at least 10 times the lifespan for a mouse &amp;mdash ; is of great interest to scientists . The hydra , a tiny freshwater polyp , may live virtually forever . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is also great scientific interest in the outer limits of longevity for humans . There have been numerous documented cases of people living over the age of 110 . But all humans age and eventually die . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;6319;TOOLONG ... title= " In this Oct. 28 , 2014 photo , neurosurgeon Dr. John Sampson places a catheter into a glioblastoma patient at Duke in Durham , N.C. The risk of cancer increases with age . " alt= " In this Oct. 28 , 2014 photo , neurosurgeon Dr. John Sampson places a catheter into a glioblastoma patient at Duke in Durham , N.C. The risk of cancer increases with age . " data-portal-copyright= " Shawn Rocco " **28;6355;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1549931 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 1825 , the English actuary Benjamin Gompertz determined that the likelihood of dying doubled every eight years from the age of about 30 . Two hundred years later , even though people live longer , his mathematical model , known as the &amp;ldquo ; Gompertz curve , &amp;rdquo ; still holds . It just distributes death over a longer arc , said Gray . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There is a genuine problem with ageism in our society , and the last thing we need is for older people to be perceived as diseased , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; The very word invokes an entirely inappropriate response &amp;mdash ; at least that is my perception . Perhaps it is because to many people the word brings to mind the Spanish Flu or the Black Death , not a universal , passive , and gradual loss of function . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Geriatrician Dr. Peter Boling agrees . If aging is a disease , simply by staying alive , we are all sick , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While we can and should improve aging by tackling the impact of age-related health problems , aging is inevitable and should be considered a fact of life , not a disease . It&amp;rsquo ; s better to work on preventative measures that will result in healthier people , and better care for those who are sick , said Boling , who is also a professor of medicine at Virginia Commonwealth University . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The presumption is that the quality of life among older individuals is not good , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s an interesting mix of things when we get older . I&amp;rsquo ; m more and more aware of some of the things I can&amp;rsquo ; t do . I have arthritis . I know changes are taking place in my cardiovascular system . But these things are predestined . I want to say I&amp;rsquo ; m glad for the things I have seen and the experiences I have had . Accumulated experience is part of the aging process . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Advocating for more basic research funding to learn about the biological causes of aging is a legitimate scientific objective . It will help us understand what makes us tick and may then lead to better health . But it will likely not ultimately uncover a fountain of youth , Boling argues . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He is cautious about the idea of taking drugs with possible side effects , or editing genes to change the aging process . You might , for example , be able to boost the immune system and increase resistance to illnesses such as influenza and shingles , he said . But there might be downstream effects . Aging is a complex process and humans are complex organisms . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When you change things in a large system , you don&amp;rsquo ; t know what else may occur , &amp;rdquo ; Boling warns . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;div class= " c-messagekitgutter " &gt; &lt;div class= " c-messagekitgutterright " &gt; &lt;div class= " c-messagekitblocks **28;6385;TOOLONG " &gt; &lt;div class= " c-messagemessageblocks **32;6415;TOOLONG " &gt; &lt;div class= " p-blockkitrenderer " &gt; &lt;div class= " **30;6449;TOOLONG **37;6481;TOOLONG " &g; ... &lt;div class= " p-richtextblock " dir= " auto " &gt; &lt;div class= " p-richtextsection " &gt;Both life expectancy ( the average number of years a person is expected to live ) and health-adjusted life expectancy ( the number of years spent in good health ) increased in Canada over a 20-year period , according to a study released by Statistics Canada in 2018 . Life expectancy at birth for Canadian men was 79.8 years in 2015 , but health-adjusted life expectancy at birth was 69.0 years , said the study , based on responses to health surveys . For Canadian women , life expectancy at birth was 83.9 years , but health-adjusted life expectancy was 70.5 years . &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; Increasing healthspans could save governments mountains of money . One 2013 study estimated that extending a healthy life by only 2.2 years would save the U.S. $7.1 trillion in health-care costs and increased productivity , for example . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But a world where 80 is the new 60 also brings up a lot of questions . Would treatments that extend life be available to everyone , or would this open a new rift between haves and have-nots ? Would two more decades of life mean postponing retirement and spending two more decades in the workforce ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Obviously , there are several policy and social implications associated with an increase in healthy lifespan of 20 to 30 years . Retirement age and social security would need to be redefined , for example , &amp;rdquo ; said Kaeberlein. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;6520;TOOLONG ... title= " A woman exercises with wooden dumbbells during an event marking Respect for the Aged Day at a temple in the Sugamo district of Tokyo , Japan , on Monday , Sept. 19 , 2016 . The proportion of Japan 's aged has been rising steadily for decades , with U.S. Census Bureau data showing that last year more than one in four Japanese were over 65. " alt= " A woman exercises with wooden dumbbells during an event marking Respect for the Aged Day at a temple in the Sugamo district of Tokyo , Japan , on Monday , Sept. 19 , 2016 . The proportion of Japan 's aged has been rising steadily for decades , with U.S. Census Bureau data showing that last year more than one in four Japanese were over 65. " data-portal-copyright= " Tomohiro Ohsumi " **28;6556;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1549932 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; He is doubtful it would happen all at once , and it will most likely be a gradual application of new discoveries . &amp;ldquo ; There are also questions about access and who will benefit first from such discoveries and how long it will take for them to become widely available &amp;mdash ; which can&amp;rsquo ; t be answered right now . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Kaeberlein would like to see Food And Drug Administration approval of an intervention to target biological aging . &amp;ldquo ; This could take many forms . For example , approval of a drug that when given to healthy people acts as a preventative for several age-related diseases or indications . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Researcher Jane Rylett recently returned from a trip to Japan , the world&amp;rsquo ; s first &amp;ldquo ; super-aged&amp;rdquo ; country , defined as one in which more than one in five &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;people are 65 or older . **26;6586;TOOLONG Canada will be a super-aged country in 10 to 15 years . The World Health Organization predicts that by 2050 , there will be several dozen countries in that category , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Let&amp;rsquo ; s say there was something on the horizon . Let&amp;rsquo ; s say there&amp;rsquo ; s a great discovery , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; There are a lot of health policy questions that would have to be answered . What does it mean for society if people live longer ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; jlaucius@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;6614;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;6656;TOOLONG ... this rare disease could help treat aspects of **26;6691;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;6719;TOOLONG ... can hold back the hands of time ? Scientists have looked at these five **31;6754;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; Joanne LauciusThu , 30 Jan 2020 12:33:43 **45;6787;TOOLONG ... **26;6834;TOOLONG is a potentially key way to combat loneliness -- but it 's difficult to get off the groundhttp : **34;6862;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;6898;TOOLONG ... title= " Front row , left to right : Valerie Tsmith , Margery Street , Mary Huang , Diana Armour , Jane Keeler , and back row left to right : Caroline Balderston Parry , Elliot Sherman , Jennifer Craven , and Jake Morrison of Concorde cohousing Saturday November 30 , 2019. " alt= " Front row , left to right : Valerie Tsmith , Margery Street , Mary Huang , Diana Armour , Jane Keeler , and back row left to right : Caroline Balderston Parry , Elliot Sherman , Jennifer Craven , and Jake Morrison of Concorde cohousing Saturday November 30 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Ashley Fraser " **28;6934;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1518292 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Mary Huang is 54 years old , unmarried with no children and a career that has taken her all over the globe . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When she returned to Ottawa to help care for her aging parents , she started to think about what she wanted for herself as she grew older . Ever the researcher , Huang , who builds complex planning and forecasting systems for large companies , set out to discover a way to &amp;ldquo ; build my own village&amp;rdquo ; and soon found one &amp;mdash ; a cohousing community . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I lived in New York for six months . I definitely saw where people didn&amp;rsquo ; t know their neighbours and it&amp;rsquo ; s pretty sad , &amp;rdquo ; said Huang , one of about a dozen people who form the nucleus of &lt;a href= " http : //Concordecohousing.ca " &gt;Concord ... , an intentional community in the planning stages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Concorde&amp;rsquo ; s members are seeking not just to share a roof over their heads , but also whatever they choose to share of their lives . You hear the world &amp;ldquo ; potluck&amp;rdquo ; often in the cohousing world . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cohousing is considered to be a model of living that can help avert loneliness and social isolation as more Canadians face aging without a partner or children , or with families that live far away . According to Statistics Canada , t &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;he number of people living alone has more than doubled between 1981 and 2016 , from 1.7 million to four million . **26;6964;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; York University anthropologist Margaret Critchlow has described cohousing as &amp;ldquo ; a grassroots model of neighbourly mutual support that can help reduce social isolation and promote positive , active aging . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cohousing encourages independence through awareness that we are all interdependent , Critchlow wrote in a 2013 article in the journal Social Science Directory . &amp;ldquo ; In a senior cohousing community , giving and receiving co-care is entirely voluntary . Members may choose to support each other through such activities as doing errands , driving , cooking , or going for a walk with a neighbour . Being good neighbours helps people age well in a community and they have fun doing it ! &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was this idea of voluntary support that resonated with Huang. &amp;ldquo ; I am pretty self-sufficient and can be an introvert at times . At other times I strike up conversations with random strangers and had some very interesting conversations , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; The idea that this type of interaction can be had just outside my door if I wanted really appealed to me. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Margery Street , 69 , worked for more than 40 years a pharmacist . She has a 32-year-old son with a disability . In the last half decade of her working life , Street&amp;rsquo ; s job took her to retirement homes to talk to residents about their medications . The experience was an eye-opener , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; One woman said she missed her garden . She was so depressed . So what do they do ? They give her anti-depressants . I thought to myself , &amp;lsquo ; There&amp;rsquo ; s no bloody way I&amp;rsquo ; m going to live in one of those , ' &amp;rdquo ; said Street , who is also a Concorde member . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; m an only child . My son is an only child . I currently rent an apartment . I&amp;rsquo ; m concerned about social isolation . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;6992;TOOLONG ... title= " Mary Huang is one of the founding members of Ottawa 's Concorde , a multi-generational cohousing community still in the planning phases . " alt= " Mary Huang is one of the founding members of Ottawa 's Concorde , a multi-generational cohousing community still in the planning phases . " data-portal-copyright= " Ashley Fraser " **28;7028;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1516040 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Could cohousing be one of the answers to helping people age well together ? Many think so . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Lynne Markell , 74 , is one of the nine core members of &lt;a href= " http : //Conviviumcohousing.ca " &gt;Conviv ... , a proposed cohousing community for older adults . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; m single . I live alone . I don&amp;rsquo ; t have any children . I wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t call myself lonely , but I realize I could live healthier and better with other people around me , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; We believe in the value of community support . Giving help and getting help back . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; People in cohousing communities have the independence of their own private units , which they usually own and can buy and sell , although some communities have low-cost rental units for those who can&amp;rsquo ; t afford to buy in . Residents share communal space that acts as a focal point for gatherings , usually a large kitchen and dining room . There may be a suite for guests or a caregiver , which opens up the possibility of sharing an in-house caregiver. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Most cohousing is set up under the same legal framework either as condos or co-ops , so the legalities and responsibilities are established . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Critchlow has suggested that building a social portfolio might be as important as building a financial portfolio . Cohousing could be &amp;ldquo ; a made-in-Canada model for ageing , not just in place , but in community , &amp;rdquo ; she wrote . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But so far , both Concorde and Convivium are still in the ideas stage . Neither has land or a design to show prospective members . And this is where cohousing appears to be stuck in many cities in Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s only completed cohousing community is Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s Terra Firma , which opened in 1997 after 12 families bought and renovated two three-door townhouses on Drummond Street near Saint Paul University . The members later added an infill building between the townhouses which includes a seventh residential unit and common space for hosting events ranging from community meals to dance parties and art classes . The yard behind the units is shared by all and contains a trampoline , treehouse , swing and sitting areas . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In a way , we &amp;lsquo ; re not doing anything different than what people with condos do . We just have a different intent , &amp;rdquo ; said Steven Fick , who has been a member in Terra Firma since the beginning . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fick was in his 40s when he bought into Terra Firma . He wasn&amp;rsquo ; t thinking about social networks and their connection to healthy aging at that point , but has since realized how much his community may help him age well . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; One of the strongest predictors of longevity is social connection , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But the real estate market in central Ottawa has changed in the past 22 years . Like most other cohousing groups across the country , Concorde and Convivium have stumbled on the hard reality of acquiring land , a developer and bridge financing to take the idea from concept to reality , all the while keeping the momentum going and the group cohesive . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Convivium started about four years ago as an effort to get a seniors cohousing community at Greystone Village &lt;span class= " st " &gt;on former Oblate lands between Main Street and the Rideau River . &lt;/span&gt; When that didn&amp;rsquo ; t work out , the group started the search for its own land . But raw land in a central location is expensive , and the group would have to self-finance the project through the planning and design process until the members were ready to swap the equity they had in their homes and move into their new units . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s all a matter of timing , &amp;rdquo ; said Convivium member Markell. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The group now wants to buy a small apartment building or perhaps a couple of adjacent buildings to retrofit them . It&amp;rsquo ; s faster than starting with the land , and at least people can look at the buildings and imagine what a retrofit would look like , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Perth , a cohousing community called &lt;a href= " http : **25;7058;TOOLONG " &gt;Tay Commons&lt;/a&gt; began more than four years ago when a group of friends held a potluck and agreed they didn&amp;rsquo ; t want to end up in long-term care . What they wanted was a sense of community , caring for each other and living in a modest and environmentally-friendly way . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In theory , you can live together more cheaply , &amp;rdquo ; said Tay Commons member Doug Burt , 74. &amp;ldquo ; A lot of cohousing people are independent thinkers . They want control over their own destinies . And privacy . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The group decided it didn&amp;rsquo ; t need a large property and acquired an option on a small plot of land that was once part of a municipal works yard a few blocks from Perth&amp;rsquo ; s historic downtown . They envisioned a three-story apartment block with units ranging from about 850 square feet to about 1,000 square feet and hired an architect . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Tay Commons is far off from being bricks and mortar . It takes a lot of moving parts to establish a cohousing community and keep it going . One member of the group had a partner who was not sold on the idea . Another was supportive , but didn&amp;rsquo ; t see herself moving in , said Louise McDiarmid , 76 , who is one of Tay Commons&amp;rsquo ; founding members . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The costs ballooned . The original quote to build came in at $3.3 million , which included about $150,000 for the land . But because the space was tight , it had to be designed to allow access for emergency vehicles . The next quote came in at $4.4 million , plus an estimated $300 a month for each unit to cover condo fees . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; That put the nail in the coffin , &amp;rdquo ; said McDiarmid&amp;rsquo ; s husband , Don , 82. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Because of the costs of buying land , designing the community and perhaps hiring a consultant to shepherd it through the process , cohousing is usually an option only for the solidly middle-class . &amp;ldquo ; We might be the last generation to be able to do this , &amp;rdquo ; notes Don McDiarmid. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Historically , most attempts to build co-housing don&amp;rsquo ; t work . At the end , they founder on cost , &amp;rdquo ; said Burt . &amp;ldquo ; The first thing you want to do it build a relationship . You want to make sure it will last . If you can&amp;rsquo ; t knit the community together , then it collapses. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cohousing originated in Denmark in 1964 , when architect Jan Gudmand-Hoyer and a group of friends came up with a plan for 12 houses with a common house and swimming pool . They bought land , but the project never got built . Still , the idea attracted attention and two communities were completed in Denmark by 1973 . Cohousing has taken root in the Netherlands , Scandinavia , the U.S. and B.C. But it&amp;rsquo ; s been slow to get off the ground in Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Typically , cohousing units are modest in size . Huang said 550 square feet would suit her just fine . The Fick family unit in Terra Firma is only about 1,200 square feet . The Ficks have raised three children in it . Their two daughters still live within a block of the community . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s another way to live lightly , &amp;rdquo ; said Steven Fick. &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t need to own things , I just need to have access to things . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Louise McDiarmid started thinking about aging after she read Betty Friedan&amp;rsquo ; s 1993 book Fountain of Age , which looked at the longevity boom and what it would mean for society . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I wanted to have control of my own aging . Betty Friedan spoke of it as a new stage of life with its own challenges and joys . You&amp;rsquo ; re not responsible for children anymore , so you&amp;rsquo ; re willing to take on new risks , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; I feel strongly about the need for community , I feel we need to belong to a group of people who value you for who you are . It was an opportunity to belong to something larger than yourself . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While many see cohousing as a seniors concept , it can work any way the members want . The age range among Tay Commons members is about 20 years . Convivium is for older adults . Concorde aims to be multi-generational. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Concorde member Valerie Thacker Smith , 38 , first experienced co-housing when she visited a friend near Ann Arbor , Michigan . The residents shared a massive kitchen and tool room . They took dance courses and barbecued together . Later , Thacker Smith lived in Haiti , where it is common for families to live in enclaves that cluster around a courtyard with an outdoor kitchen . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; People are so much better off . It&amp;rsquo ; s not just the emotional benefits .
