How I sanitized the feminist &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneFri , 06 Dec 2019 11:13:03 **45;8373;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsSeniorsInvestigate use of trespass law to limit visits in retirement , long-term care homes : Ottawa MPPhttp : **37;8420;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;8459;TOOLONG ... title= " Mary Sardelis outside of the retirement home where her 97-year-old mother Voula is a resident . " alt= " Mary Sardelis outside of the retirement home where her 97-year-old mother Voula is a resident . " data-portal-copyright= " Errol McGihon " **28;8495;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1512824 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is an increasingly common story in Ontario &amp;mdash ; family members who say they are banned from visiting elderly loved ones after complaining about their institutional care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On Tuesday , Ottawa MPP Joel Harden asked the Minister of Seniors and Accessibility , Raymond Cho , to investigate the issue , saying some operators in the retirement home industry are engaging in &amp;ldquo ; questionable tactics . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Harden said some retirement homes are misusing trespassing laws to silence people who complain about care or services in the home . The regulatory body for retirement homes , he said , works at a &amp;ldquo ; glacial pace&amp;rdquo ; to respond to concerns . He said he would like to see the investigation extended to long-term care homes as well . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This newspaper has reported on several cases of adult children who were banned from visiting their elderly parents in Ottawa long-term care homes after complaining about their care or conditions in the homes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Harden said he was concerned to hear about the case of Ottawa resident Mary Sardelis who was charged with trespassing after management of the retirement home where her mother lived called the police . Sardelis&amp;rsquo ; s case was documented on the CBC program Marketplace . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sardelis said s &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;he was prevented from seeing her 97-year-old mother for most of the past year until the charged was withdrawn last month . She was allowed in last Dec. 23 to quickly deliver a gift to her mother . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The home&amp;rsquo ; s owners , who claim police were called because Sardelis was being abusive and disruptive to staff , say she could have come back at any time if she signed an agreement . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Sardelis , who is now being sued by the retirement home for defamation , said the agreement was essentially a &amp;ldquo ; gag order&amp;rdquo ; that would have limited her visits with her mother to one hour in the evening and prevented her from ever criticizing the retirement home . She is back visiting her mother again , but said she feels like her every move is being watched . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sardelis said the investigative body for retirement homes , the Retirement Homes Regulatory Authority , investigated and found no evidence of the retirement home&amp;rsquo ; s allegations . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said it is &amp;ldquo ; about time&amp;rdquo ; there was an investigation into the use of trespass law to keep people from visiting loved ones . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is long overdue . People are saying thanks for bringing awareness ( to this issue ) . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Keith McLaren , lawyer for the retirement home where Sardelis&amp;rsquo ; s mother lives , said it is not illegal for retirement residences to issue trespass notices . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; McLaren added that the home , as part of the defamation lawsuit against her and nine other people , is asking that Sardelis be permanently banned from visiting . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sardelis&amp;rsquo ; s lawyer Mitchell Rowe said the defamation suit is aimed at Sardelis and a group of people who picketed the retirement home , for which they had a permit . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Advocates for the elderly say the issue is increasingly common and worrisome. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Living in a long-term care home can be a lonely and isolating experience , cut off from the outside world . One of the most important ways that a resident&amp;rsquo ; s day can be brightened up is by having a visitor . Unfortunately , residents may encounter issues in having visitors , and visitors may encounter problems when attempting to visit residents , &amp;rdquo ; wrote Jane Meadus , lawyer for the Advocacy Centre for the Elderly . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2017 , two local women were issued no trespass notices and told to leave the long-term care homes in Ottawa where their mothers lived , in separate incidents . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Both of the women , who are retired hospital nurses , said the homes were using no trespass notices and threats of fines to gag them after they complained about substandard care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You complain and this is the price you pay , &amp;rdquo ; said one woman whose mother is disabled . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Advocates say trespass laws are being abused to keep people who complain out of institutions that care for seniors . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A spokesperson for Seniors Minister Raymond Cho noted that the provincial government regulates but does not fund the retirement home sector . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Retirement Homes Regulatory Authority administers the Retirement Homes Act on behalf of the government . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If family members of residents are concerned about conditions in retirement homes please contact the Retirement Homes Regulatory Authority at 1-855-275-7472 or at info@rhra.ca , &amp;rdquo ; said director of communications Pooja Parekh. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8525;TOOLONG ... Ottawa take notes from other countries dealing with an opioid crisis ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8560;TOOLONG ... Five good reasons to label this city ' Ottawa the Inept ' &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8595;TOOLONG ... what you need to know about superbugs in the ' post-antibiotic era ' &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneThu , 28 Nov 2019 15:02:20 **45;8630;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsProposed Ontario drug regulation changes worry policy expertshttp : **33;8677;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;8712;TOOLONG ... title= " A 2016 file photo of Dr. Joel Lexchin. " alt= " A 2016 file photo of Dr. Joel Lexchin. " data-portal-copyright= " Tyler Anderson/Naitonal Post " **28;8748;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509861 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ontario government is proposing drug regulation changes that it says will remove red tape and barriers for pharmaceutical companies , allowing some drugs to bypass the normal approval process during a shortage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8778;TOOLONG ... shortages , including the often-used breast cancer drug tamoxifen&lt;/a&gt; , have been wide-spread and increasing in recent years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The move , the government says , would allow alternative drug products to quickly enter the market during a shortage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; However , the move is being met with resistance by some drug policy experts who say more information is needed to ensure the changes are good for consumers &amp;mdash ; and whether the changes will actually help prevent long-term drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The changes , part of the government&amp;rsquo ; s larger push to reduce red tape , would also make it easier for companies to raise or lower drug prices in other cases , depending on the market . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a document about the proposed changes , the government says they would ensure that Ontario remains &amp;ldquo ; open for business&amp;rdquo ; for the pharmacy and pharmaceutical industry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposals , called &amp;ldquo ; Reducing Administrative Burden to Drug Manufacturers and Pharmacies , &amp;rdquo ; are part of an omnibus red tape reduction bill that was introduced at the end of October while the legislature was recessed . They affect prescription drugs , mainly generics. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some drug policy experts fear the changes would remove some consumer protections , are being introduced with little publicity and will do little to address long-term drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pharmaceutical policy expert Dr. Joel Lexchin , who teaches at York University&amp;rsquo ; s School of Health Policy and Management , said he is concerned about the lack of consultation and transparency around the changes . Some of the proposals respond to requests from the pharmaceutical industry with apparently little or no input from consumers or healthcare providers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; More details are needed , he said , before the changes go ahead . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It should not be the job of the Ontario Ministry of Health and Long-Term Care to advocate on behalf of the pharmaceutical industry . The job of the Ministry of Health should be the protection of the health of Ontarians , &amp;rdquo ; wrote Lexchin in a submission to the Ministry of Health . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Before the ministry goes ahead with these changes it needs to provide much more information and show that these changes will put the health of the people of Ontario above the interests of the commercial entities that are advocating for them . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jacalyn Duffin , professor emerita and Hannah Chair of the History of Medicine at Queen&amp;rsquo ; s University , said she only learned about the proposals when a law firm blogged about them . She said she finds some of them shocking . &amp;ldquo ; They will remove some consumer protections and probably will not help the shortages . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposed changes include an amendment that would make it easier for generic drug companies to adjust their prices if the number of competitors on the market changes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Under the current model , generic drugs are priced at a percentage of the brand price , depending on how many competitors there are . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For example , if a brand label drug costs $10 , generic makers could charge $7.50 or $8 , but that price would decrease based on the number of competitors . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposed changes allow drug companies to more easily adjust their prices when competitors come in and out of the market , something the government says should reduce the price of generic drugs . However , it could also allow companies to increase the price of generics , especially at a time of consolidations of drug companies and shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Justin Bates , Chief Executive Officer of the Ontario Pharmacists Association , said the change enables Ontario pharmacies to operate on a level playing field with pharmacies in every other province , except Quebec . Ordinary commercial terms are a normal part of business , he said , and do not increase drug costs , which are regulated by a Canada-wide system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposed changes would also exempt certain drugs from drug submission requirements , allowing quicker funding of a drug that could fill in during a shortage . Funding of the alternative drug would be temporary , the government says . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A spokesman for Health Minister Christine Elliott dismissed concerns that the changes had been brought in quietly and with too little information , saying they were part of a &amp;ldquo ; high-profile&amp;rdquo ; government wide announcement of red tape reduction proposals and will help the government respond to patient needs in the event of potential drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The proposed changes would allow an alternative drug product to be temporarily funded under the Ontario Drug Benefit program during a supply interruption that may be due to a drug shortage &amp;hellip ; and is expected to lead to significant patient risk or harm , &amp;rdquo ; said Hayley Chazan , a spokesperson for Health Minister Christine Elliott . &amp;ldquo ; This proposal , if approved , would mitigate supply gaps of funded products for recipients of the Ontario Drug Benefit program . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Lexchin said the proposed changes are simply reactive and lack any proactive measures to deal with future drug shortages . New Zealand , for example , requires drug companies to guarantee there will be sufficient supply before approving the drug for the health system . If the company runs out of drugs , it is then their responsibility to source a replacement , not the government&amp;rsquo ; s. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Paul Grootendorst , associate professor in the Faculty of Pharmacy at the University of Toronto said the proposed changes allowing generic companies to change their prices up or down when the number of competitors changes make sense . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t see any problems , &amp;rdquo ; he said of the proposals . &amp;ldquo ; These are more housekeeping issues . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The government is seeking comment on the proposals until Nov. 27. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8813;TOOLONG ... bodies are piling up ' : Inside the opioid epidemic and the crisis that wo n't go away&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8848;TOOLONG ... concerned they ca n't provide proper care in crowded hospitals , says union&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;8883;TOOLONG ... was bawling ' : Injured Bronco 's mother stunned by his progress after surgery&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneFri , 22 Nov 2019 11:39:55 **45;8918;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsSeniorsWomenU.S. stem cell clinic offering unapproved therapies brings direct-to-consumer marketing to Ottawahttp : **34;8965;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;9001;TOOLONG ... title= " A doctor a private U.S. clinic injects a patient with stem cells to treat cartilage wastage joint injury and pain . " alt= " A doctor a private U.S. clinic injects a patient with stem cells to treat cartilage wastage joint injury and pain . " data-portal-copyright= " edward olive " **28;9037;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1507409 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Just a few months after Health Canada began cracking down on private clinics offering unapproved stem cell therapies , at least one U.S. clinic has moved in to fill the vacuum with direct marketing to Canadian consumers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The clinic from Burlington , Vermont , even offers shuttle buses to transport people from Ottawa to the clinic four hours away for treatment it suggests will end joint pain , among other things . Lunch and dinner are free , but each injection costs $6,880 . Two for $10,880. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The treatments , using umbilical cord-derived mesenchymal stem cells , are not approved in either Canada or the United States . Health Canada warns that Canadians who travel abroad for stem cell treatments &amp;ldquo ; may put themselves at risk . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While stem cells , which were discovered at the University of Toronto in 1961 by James Till and Ernest McCulloch , promise to revolutionize many treatments and could offer breakthroughs for diseases , almost all are still considered experimental and have yet to be proven safe or effective . Clinical trials on numerous potential stem cell therapies are under way , including in Ottawa . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While research progresses , private stem cell clinics have popped up around the world making promises for treatments not yet proven safe or effective . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A 2018 study by Leigh Turner of the University of Minnesota Center for Bioethics found 43 clinics offering stem cell treatments in Canada and 750 in the U.S. Earlier this year , Health Canada sent Canadian clinics , including some in Ottawa , cease-and-desist letters . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Clinics in Vermont , near the Canadian border , appear to have ramped up marketing to Canadians since then . One clinic has been holding back-to-back seminars . Another says it stopped marketing in Canada after receiving a warning from Health Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There have been cases of harm as a result of treatments , including two women who had permanent damage to their sight after stem cells were injected into their eyes at a Florida clinic . Other patients have been infected with unsterilized equipment and others have developed tumours at the site of stem cell injections. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A common harm , critics say , is exploitation . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;9067;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Michael Rudnicki is director of the regenerative medicine program and Sprott Centre for Stem Cell Research at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute , says of stem cell therapy claims : ' If it sounds too good to be true , it probably is too good to be true . ' " alt= " Dr. Michael Rudnicki is director of the regenerative medicine program and Sprott Centre for Stem Cell Research at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute , says of stem cell therapy claims : ' If it sounds too good to be true , it probably is too good to be true . ' " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;9103;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1507406 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health officials say the clinics are misusing the promise of stem cell therapy to exploit vulnerable patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; These patients are in pain and they are suffering and they are looking for help and they are being exploited , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Michael Rudnicki , director of the regenerative medicine program and Sprott Centre for Stem Cell Research at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If it sounds too good to be true , it probably is too good to be true . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At a recent seminar at a west-end Ottawa hotel meeting room , Roseanna Ammendolea of the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine told a packed room that her clinic and others like it had successfully treated people for pain related to arthritis , neuropathy and other ailments that affected joints using mesenchymal stem cells from umbilical cords . The stem cells , she claimed , are both effective and safe , saying there had been no issues with cell rejection . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We will not give injections if we feel that this injection will not be beneficial to our patients . This is why we are so successful . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Participants , including some who walked with canes and others who talked about being in pain and having mobility issues , were shown videos of people described as Canadian clients who claimed the treatments worked . One man said it was &amp;ldquo ; probably the best money I have spent in my life as far as my health . &amp;rdquo ; Another said she would do it again &amp;ldquo ; in a heartbeat&amp;rdquo ; and was able to do things she hadn&amp;rsquo ; t been able to do earlier . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They were also shown a slide showing long wait times for hip and knee replacements in Ontario , &amp;ldquo ; We are not a priority , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; Where does that leave us ? &amp;rdquo ; Participants weren&amp;rsquo ; t told exactly how the stem cells were supposed to work , but claimed they had successfully improved pain and mobility issues in clients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; What the seminar goers weren&amp;rsquo ; t told is that , even in the U.S. , the treatment is not covered by health insurance because it remains unproven. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The U.S. Federal Drug Administration has issued a warning to consumers not to use cell therapies that are unapproved or unproven. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Stem cells have been called everything from cure-alls to miracle treatments . But don&amp;rsquo ; t believe the hype . Some unscrupulous providers offer stem cell products that are both unapproved and unproven . So beware of potentially dangerous procedures &amp;mdash ; and confirm what&amp;rsquo ; s really being offered before you consider &lt;em&gt;any&lt;/em&gt; &amp;nbsp ; treatment , &amp;rdquo ; the FDA said in a statement . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The only stem-cell-based products that are FDA-approved for use in the United States are blood-forming stem cells derived from cord blood&amp;nbsp ; for limited use in patients with disorders affecting the body system that is involved in the production of blood . Bone marrow is also used for these treatments , but is generally not regulated by the FDA for that use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health Canada has granted market authorization for a stem cell therapy to treat graft-versus-host disease and two cell-based gene therapies to treat certain cancers . Most cell therapies are still experimental . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I totally understand the skepticism of it , &amp;rdquo ; Doug Argento , who works at the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine , said in a telephone interview , &amp;ldquo ; but the fact is that things that are approved now and medically paid for were seen as renegade 20 or 30 years ago . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The treatment employs technology developed by Neil Riordan , founder , chairman and chief science officer of the Stem Cell Institute in Panama , using human umbilical cord tissue-derived mesenchymal stem cells . There are 41 such clinics across the U.S. Riordan also played a role in the development of a nutritional product called Stem-Kine , which producers claim &amp;mdash ; without scientific backing &amp;mdash ; increases the number of stem cells circulating in a person&amp;rsquo ; s body . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The stem cells injected in the clinic , Argento said , are from umbilical cord tissue as a result of caesarean births to reduce risk of infection . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rudnicki , of The Ottawa Hospital Research Institute , says there is &amp;ldquo ; no evidence that these sorts of cells are regenerative at all . It would not pass muster in Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The public has to understand that there are people out to remove them from their money . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rudnicki says he regularly receives inquiries from people desperate to get stem cell treatments . He says he tries to connect them with clinical trials that they might be able to participate in. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rudnicki noted there were multiple clinical trials in Canada , including treatments of autoimmune diseases , trials involving treatment for Type 1 diabetes and others . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; But the use of these inappropriate cell types for treating arthritis and joints and so on is certainly not approved by Health Canada and would not be allowed in Canada under the regulations . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is some evidence that injections of some stem cell products might have a temporary positive impact on inflammation , he said , but it will not be regenerative and will not restore function to joints . &amp;ldquo ; They are being sold a bill of goods . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Leigh Turner of the University of Minnesota Center for Bioethics , meanwhile , says the explosion in clinics offering unproven stem cell therapies in the U.S. is &amp;ldquo ; a marketplace that traffics in misrepresentation . It is easy to see how people are taken advantage of and scammed. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is also difficult to find out about physical harms being done to patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are no safety studies . We don&amp;rsquo ; t have good data . But we do know there have been some serious harms. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Stem cell therapies have the potential to become standard treatment in some areas , but they are not there yet , Turner said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Businesses are tapping into genuine human suffering , desperation and also hope . &amp;ldquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Turner also noted there was an &amp;ldquo ; excellent chance&amp;rdquo ; that the vials of liquid being injected into patients did not actually contain stem cells . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Jonathan Fenton of another stem cell clinic in Burlington , the Vermont Regenerative Medicine , said he had complained about the new clinic , the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine , which has a similar name and employs hard-sell tactics , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; His clinic takes bone marrow from patients&amp;rsquo ; hips and injects it . The procedure is done the same day . He says he regularly sees Canadian patients for bone marrow aspiration therapy and platelet-rich plasma treatments , using their own blood . The treatments , he says , speed healing and are allowed in the U.S. The use of bone marrow aspiration is neither proven nor allowed in Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fenton , who is secretary-treasurer of the American Academy of Orthopedic Medicine , acknowledged many people offering stem cell treatments are &amp;ldquo ; not doing it to the highest ethical standards . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He has filed complaints with state officials over clinics selling unsafe or fraudulent treatments . &amp;ldquo ; I have asked the state and federal judiciary to close down this clinic for committing fraud . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He said his platelet and bone marrow treatments were covered by a major Vermont health insurer because they saw the cost of benefits were going down and patients were requiring fewer surgeries . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He said he was told by Health Canada that he could not market in Canada . Representatives of the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine , meanwhile , said they had discussions with Health Canada about what they could and could not say when marketing in Canada before holding seminars in Ottawa and Halifax . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We have looked at the information provided and have not identified any immediate non-compliance with advertising regulations pertaining to Canadian health products , &amp;rdquo ; a Health Canada spokesperson said , adding that the agency was continuing to assess . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Back at a west-end Ottawa hotel , some participants in the seminar , including a retired pharmacist , said they were considering getting the treatments . &amp;ldquo ; But it&amp;rsquo ; s expensive . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Another participant said he was skeptical. &amp;ldquo ; They seemed very sketchy when I went online . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;9133;TOOLONG " **34;9162;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;9198;TOOLONG ... and Kaushic : We 're losing the fight against superbugs&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;9233;TOOLONG ... who live to at least 110 , boast stronger immune systems , helping them fight off diseases&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;9268;TOOLONG ... stimulator ' really promising ' : Survivor moves legs for first time since Humboldt bus crash&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneMon , 18 Nov 2019 11:51:22 **45;9303;TOOLONG ... **27;9350;TOOLONG century of trauma : Tracing the evolution of PTSD through four soldiershttp : **32;9379;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;9413;TOOLONG ... title= " Pte . Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " alt= " Pte . Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " data-portal-copyright= " Wayne Cuddington " **28;9449;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500695 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; On the opening morning of the Battle of Vimy Ridge , Capt . Thain MacDowell ran towards enemy lines through a welter of mud and craters , sleet and machine gunfire . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He reached his objective , a German trench , only to find that he had become separated from most of his company . Joined by a pair of army couriers , MacDowell captured two machine gun placements , then climbed down a flight of steps into a deep dugout . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Around a sharp corner , he confronted a roomful of German soldiers . He turned and shouted to an imaginary group of Canadians behind him to hold their grenades . Seventy-seven Germans surrendered . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;9479;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;9521;TOOLONG ... The latest treatment **28;9556;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;9586;TOOLONG ... War army nurse enlisted out of concern for her **28;9621;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;9651;TOOLONG ... composer sets veteran 's poem to music for Remembrance Day&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;9686;TOOLONG ... Alex Kowbel typed his way into the **29;9721;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; He sent the prisoners up the stairs in groups of 12 to conceal the fact they were surrendering to just three Canadian soldiers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They had plenty of rations but we had a great time taking them prisoner , &amp;rdquo ; MacDowell wrote in a dispatch later that day , April 9 , 1917. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; His act of courage and deception would earn him the British Empire&amp;rsquo ; s highest decoration for military valour . One of four Canadians awarded the Victoria Cross at Vimy Ridge , MacDowell was the only one still alive six months later when he returned home to Brockville on sick leave . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell was not sick in the conventional sense : He was suffering from what military doctors called &amp;ldquo ; war neurasthenia , &amp;rdquo ; or shellshock . His symptoms included depression , insomnia , headaches , irritability , fever , perspiration , difficulty concentrating and decreased energy . He also had a slight speech impediment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell was one of 10,000 Canadian soldiers diagnosed with shellshock during the First World War . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The condition baffled doctors and challenged military leaders , who didn&amp;rsquo ; t know how to deal with the flood of traumatized soldiers that accompanied every major battle . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some attributed the phenomenon to emotional weakness or malingering . More than 300 British and Commonwealth soldiers were executed for cowardice or desertion during the war , including 23 Canadians . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; An unknown number of them suffered from what today we would call post-traumatic stress disorder ( PTSD ) . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; War has inflicted trauma on successive generations of Canadian soldiers whose psychiatric injuries have been variously labelled shellshock , battle exhaustion , combat stress reaction and PTSD . The evolution of that diagnosis has traced a century of conflict : It is a story of service and science and suffering . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;9752;TOOLONG ... title= " First World War Victoria Cross winner Lt.-Col. Thain MacDowell. " alt= " First World War Victoria Cross winner Lt.-Col. Thain MacDowell. " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;9788;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500700 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Capt . Thain MacDowell&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In February 1915 , British psychologist Dr. Charles Myers was the first to identify the phenomenon of shellshock . In the medical journal The Lancet , &lt;a href= " https : **33;9818;TOOLONG ... recounted the case histories of three soldiers&lt;/a&gt; traumatized by shell explosions . Curiously , he said , the soldiers&amp;rsquo ; hearing was little affected , while other functions such as sight , smell , taste and memory were damaged . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The close relation of these cases to those of &amp;lsquo ; hysteria&amp;rsquo ; appears fairly certain , &amp;rdquo ; he concluded . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hysteria was a mental disorder , attributed mostly to women at the time , which typically featured nervousness , fainting or fits . Shellshocked soldiers could be rendered mute or left with partially paralyzed limbs . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some doctors theorized that shellshock was the result of a physical brain injury caused by soldiers&amp;rsquo ; repeated exposure to blast waves from exploding shells &amp;mdash ; a new part of industrialized warfare . Others pointed to afflicted soldiers who had never been in an explosion to argue that the condition was due to a weakness of the nervous system , &amp;ldquo ; neurasthenia , &amp;rdquo ; triggered by the general stress of war . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The world&amp;rsquo ; s &lt;a href= " https : **33;9853;TOOLONG ... personality test&lt;/a&gt; was developed in an attempt to identify recruits with the &amp;ldquo ; emotional instability&amp;rdquo ; that could put them at risk for shellshock. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Treatments were mostly experimental , and sometimes , horrifying . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In December 1916 , Myers spearheaded the establishment of specialized medical units near the frontlines to assess and treat traumatized soldiers using Freudian talk therapy . Theorizing that shellshock was a stress disorder , he believed a patient had to relive his experience to reintegrate the traumatic event with his conscious mind . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A Canadian psychiatrist , Dr. Lewis Yealland , was the leading proponent of an altogether different school of thought : that shellshock was a kind of personal failure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Yealland , a clinician at London&amp;rsquo ; s National Hospital for the Paralyzed and Epileptic , was convinced he could recondition traumatized soldiers through the power of suggestion . He regularly accompanied those suggestions with powerful electric shocks , and sometimes , cigarette burns . He used electrotherapy on 196 patients during the war , and published his results in a disturbing 1918 book , &lt;a href= " https : **33;9888;TOOLONG ... Disorders of Warfare&lt;/a&gt; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; No one , including Yealland , could suggest that war hero Thain MacDowell lacked battlefield courage or personal fortitude . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Awarded the Victoria Cross in June 1917 , MacDowell was hospitalized in England and sent home to Canada in October . He spent three months at Brockville General Hospital where , &lt;a href= " http : **30;9923;TOOLONG ? op= ... to his medical history&lt;/a&gt; , he suffered a nervous breakdown . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In January 1918 , after a period of rest , he was deemed fit for service again : &amp;ldquo ; Officer shows much improvement since last examination &amp;mdash ; has regained his emotional control . No attacks of crying since Nov. 17. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell returned to England in February 1918 , and spent the year in officer training as the First World War raced towards its bloody conclusion . He returned to Canada in December , one month after the armistice , and again sought help . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A medical history taken at the time says : &amp;ldquo ; Officer states that he tires easily and can not sleep . Has not slept well since Nov. 1916 . He may sleep 3 to 4 hours a night if there has been no excitement . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; ( In November 1916 , MacDowell was thrown into the air by a shell blast at the Somme ; he earned the Distinguished Service Order for his role in capturing three machine gun posts during the same battle. ) &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In January 1919 , MacDowell was made commanding officer of a demobilization unit in Ottawa , where he was also an outpatient at the Sir Sandford Fleming Convalescent Home . He could only manage the work for a few months . By August 1919 , he was an in-patient at Montreal&amp;rsquo ; s Ste. Anne de Bellevue Hospital , complaining of depression , insomnia , restlessness and irritability. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; He tires very easily and in all work loses interest , &amp;rdquo ; reads his medical case history . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Montreal , he was prescribed massage and hydrotherapy , a popular method for treating mental illness in the early 20th century . Warm , continuous baths were used to treat agitated patients ; they were often bathed in a darkened room for hours , sometimes days , at a time . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In October 1919 , after MacDowell was deemed medically unfit for service and discharged , he returned to Ottawa , where he slowly recovered his mental health . His military file does not disclose what treatment he received . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For five years , he worked as private secretary to the minister of defence , and in 1929 , married Norah Hodgson , of Montreal . They had two sons . He later entered the mining business as an investor and executive . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell died of a heart attack in March 1960 . He was 69. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;9955;TOOLONG ... title= " Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " alt= " Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " data-portal-copyright= " Wayne Cuddington " **28;9991;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500685 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Signalman William " Ted " Patrick &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In the Second World War , signalmen maintained communication links between frontline officers and headquarter staff who managed the battlefield. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Enemy forces regularly used signalmen &amp;mdash ; they carried radios on their backs &amp;mdash ; to aim their artillery since they knew officers would be nearby . It made Signalmen William &amp;ldquo ; Ted&amp;rdquo ; Patrick a target in 1944 as he fought his way north through Italy with the Irish Regiment of Canada . He suffered perforated eardrums from shells exploding so close to him . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Patrick&amp;rsquo ; s Italian campaign had other harrowing moments . In the Moro River Valley , he saw a heavily pregnant woman ripped open by a landmine . During another advance , he had to cover the lighted dials of his radio as German soldiers walked past him into an ambush. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He did not seek help for the profound anxiety he suffered . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Infantry soldiers like Patrick were the primary victims of &amp;ldquo ; battle exhaustion&amp;rdquo ; in the Second World War . Research by Canadian military historian Terry Copp , a professor at Wilfrid Laurier University , found that 90 per cent of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s battle exhaustion cases came from ground troops . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In part , that was a reflection of the Royal Canadian Air Force&amp;rsquo ; s uncompromising attitude towards aircrew members who developed psychiatric problems and refused to fly . Such airmen were branded as &amp;ldquo ; lacking in moral fibre&amp;rdquo ; or LMF ; they were often demoted or dishonourably discharged . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Canadian army took a more pragmatic view . Its senior psychiatrist , Col. Frederick Van Nostrand , wanted battle exhausted soldiers treated quickly while close to the frontlines so they could be promptly returned to action . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It meant that as Canadian forces fought through Normandy , hundreds of soldiers were treated each week at field dressing stations for acute battle stress . Typically , they were sedated for 24 hours , given two days of rest , and counselled by a therapist . Many were returned to action only to suffer another breakdown . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Battle exhaustion cases represented one-quarter of all wounded soldiers among Allied forces . &amp;nbsp ; Those numbers caught military planners by surprise in Canada , where medical officers had tried to weed out recruits with emotional instability . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The methods that were used were no better than flipping a coin , &amp;rdquo ; Copp , the author of two books on the history of combat stress , said in an interview . &amp;ldquo ; None of it worked in terms of predicting who would break down or who would not break down under conditions of combat . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a prophetic report filed at the end of the war , Van Nostrand said he was unsure doctors would ever solve &amp;ldquo ; the vast problem of the psychiatric breakdown of soldiers during war . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It is my opinion , &amp;rdquo ; he wrote , &amp;ldquo ; that the methods now employed in the British , American , and Canadian armies will not materially lower the incidence of psychiatric casualties in a fighting force . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are various reasons for these opinions but two of them are fundamental : First , there is direct conflict between the needs of the service and the needs of the individual soldier as assessed by his physician . Secondly , the attitudes and behaviour of the successful soldier are contrary to most of his previous teaching . He must not allow death or mutilation of his comrades to prevent him from reaching his objective , and finally , he must pretend that he is glad to risk his life for that cause . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Van Nostrand pleaded with military planners to accept that normal people can not always manage the exceptional stress of war . &amp;ldquo ; Every soldier has his breaking point , &amp;rdquo ; he warned . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ted Patrick had bumped up against his breaking point . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;10021;TOOLONG ... title= " Ted Patrick . " alt= " Ted Patrick . " data-portal-copyright= " Wayne Cuddington " **28;10057;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500684 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; After the Second World War , Patrick returned home to Ottawa and buried his memories . It was a common approach . He married , took a job in the civil service , and launched a bee-keeping business to supplement his income . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; All the while , however , the tide of war washed over his subconscious . Patrick sometimes attacked his wife in the middle of the night , believing her to be an enemy soldier ; she eventually began to sleep in a separate bedroom . Other times , he&amp;rsquo ; d wake up sobbing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Patrick scrupulously avoided the legion hall and regimental reunions &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; anything that brought the war to mind . He was also extremely cautious . He didn&amp;rsquo ; t like to go to unfamiliar places ; he was , he said , like a rat that stayed close to a wall . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I would not take a chance on getting hurt or having the family hurt . I was always extremely cautious and went around danger , &amp;rdquo ; he once told an interviewer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It wasn&amp;rsquo ; t until the 1980s that Patrick was finally diagnosed with PTSD ; his psychiatrist urged him to confront his wartime trauma . Talking about his experiences &amp;mdash ; and sharing his memories &amp;mdash ; eased Patrick&amp;rsquo ; s anxiety . Late in life , he became a dedicated volunteer at the Canadian War Museum and travelled to Holland for ceremonies to commemorate the country&amp;rsquo ; s liberation . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He died in February 2015. &lt;strong&gt; &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;10087;TOOLONG ... title= " Gordon Forbes poses for a photo in his home in Ottawa Tuesday Oct 29 , 2019. " alt= " Gordon Forbes poses for a photo in his home in Ottawa Tuesday Oct 29 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;10123;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500678 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Lt . Gordon Forbes &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Gordon Forbes , 76 , of Orl&amp;eacute ; ans , was on board &lt;a href= " http : **34;10153;TOOLONG ... Kootenay&lt;/a&gt; 50 years ago during the worst peacetime disaster in the history of the Royal Canadian Navy &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; an event that would colour much of his life . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On Oct. 23 , 1969 , H.M.C.S. Kootenay was in the North Atlantic , returning to Halifax from a NATO naval exercise . At 8:21 a.m. , during a full power trial &amp;mdash ; a drill to test the destroyer&amp;rsquo ; s performance at top speed &amp;mdash ; an explosion ripped through the ship&amp;rsquo ; s engine room . A mass of flames shot from a broken gearbox , setting fire to the 10 men inside of the room . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Only three escaped alive . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Thick black smoke quickly filled the lower decks , but its source wasn&amp;rsquo ; t readily apparent to those on the bridge , including Lt. Gordon Forbes , the ship&amp;rsquo ; s weapons officer who was responsible for Kootenay&amp;rsquo ; s 50 tonnes of ammunition . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Engineering Officer Al Kennedy , one of those to escape the engine room , stumbled into the bridge , blackened and badly burned : &amp;ldquo ; Fire in the engine room , &amp;rdquo ; he announced . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That posed problems . The ship&amp;rsquo ; s firefighting equipment was stored near the engine room , and the ship&amp;rsquo ; s main ammunition magazine was immediately behind it . A sailor reported the bulkhead between the two was already hot . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Divers donned tanks and masks to descend into the smoke : They retrieved the ship&amp;rsquo ; s firefighting equipment and rescued sailors trapped by the blinding smoke . Forbes sprayed down the magazine to reduce the threat of a catastrophic explosion . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If the magazine had blown up , it would have destroyed the ship , &amp;rdquo ; he told this newspaper . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It took about three hours to bring the fire under control ; the magazine was then flooded to better stabilize the munitions . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Those on board were told not to talk about the disaster , which had killed nine of their fellow sailors . &amp;ldquo ; No one really knew how to deal with it , &amp;rdquo ; said Forbes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At the time of the Kootenay disaster , the Vietnam War was in full swing : In 1969 , the number of deployed U.S. troops peaked at 549,000. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;10189;TOOLONG ... title= " HMCS Kootenay approaching flight locks , Welland canal . " alt= " HMCS Kootenay approaching flight locks , Welland canal . " data-portal-copyright= " CF Photo Unit " **28;10225;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500686 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Curiously , few soldiers reported battle fatigue symptoms in Vietnam . Army officials attributed that development to limited battlefield exposure : Soldiers were rotated through the war on one-year tours of duty . It gave them a firm date by which their wartime ordeal would end . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Many military planners thought the problem of battlefield stress injuries had been solved . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Instead , of course , it had simply gone to ground . Tens of thousands of soldiers returned from Vietnam traumatized : afflicted by nightmares , insomnia , depression , rage , paranoia and addictions . Psychiatrists labeled the phenomenon delayed psychiatric trauma or &amp;ldquo ; post-Vietnam Syndrome&amp;rdquo ; since some thought the disorder was unique to Vietnam . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was the first time that psychiatrists recognized that stress injuries were not always immediate , but could announce themselves months , even years , later . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Psychiatrists who worked with Vietnam veterans lobbied to have the disorder formally recognized , and in 1980 , the authoritative American Psychiatric Association made PTSD part of its Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The formal recognition of PTSD had profound consequences and allowed for better diagnosis and treatment . Therapists recognized the same disorder in Holocaust survivors , sexual assault victims , first responders &amp;mdash ; and others afflicted by traumatic events such as the Kootenay explosion . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sub-Lieut . Clark Reiffenstein , one of those who donned scuba gear to plunge into the ship&amp;rsquo ; s smoke-filled lower decks , died of suicide one month after the fire . He was posthumously awarded the Star of Courage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Many other Kootenay sailors struggled with alcoholism , nightmares and anxiety . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 1990 , Gordon Forbes was diagnosed with clinical depression soon after retiring from the navy . He suffered from paranoia and had difficulty sleeping . His condition was later linked to PTSD and recognized as a service injury by Veterans Affairs Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was one measure of the advance in the military&amp;rsquo ; s approach to psychiatric infirmity . &amp;nbsp ; &amp;ldquo ; I was very pleased that they started to recognize PTSD , &amp;rdquo ; he said , &amp;ldquo ; and not just throw people out of the service on medical grounds , which is what happened to people who went to get help after the Kootenay fire . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2010 , Forbes published a book , We Are As One , about the disaster and its emotional aftermath . Researching and writing the book , he said , was a cathartic experience for everyone involved : &amp;ldquo ; I had so many men come up to me after I wrote the book and say , &amp;lsquo ; I thought I was the only one . ' &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;10255;TOOLONG ... title= " Natacha Dupuis is a former Canadian soldier and Afghan vet who suffered debilitating PTSD after her war service . " alt= " Natacha Dupuis is a former Canadian soldier and Afghan vet who suffered debilitating PTSD after her war service . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;10291;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500687 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Master Cpl . Natacha Dupuis&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ten years ago , in March 2009 , Master Cpl . Natacha Dupuis was put in charge of her first mission with the reconnaissance squadron of the Royal Canadian Dragoons : a week-long foot patrol in Kandahar Province . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Qualified as a tank gunner , she had served in Bosnia and was on her second tour of duty in Afghanistan . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Leaving the Forward Operating Base Frontenac , a military outpost near Kandahar , Dupuis led her patrol to a hilltop camp . The next morning , as the patrol departed , a massive explosion ripped into the armoured vehicle behind Dupuis&amp;rsquo ; vehicle . The 14-tonne Coyote was blown into the air and landed on its roof . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dupuis helped to collect the remains of two dead soldiers while preparing for a secondary attack that never came . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her team was quickly evacuated , but Dupuis kept reliving the incident and re-imagining the terrible scene . For days , unable to turn off her flow of adrenaline , she couldn&amp;rsquo ; t sleep . A psychiatrist prescribed her sleeping pills , but she struggled through each day and often sought out a private place to cry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She willed herself through her final two months of service in Afghanistan . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As soon as she returned home to Petawawa , however , she fell apart : Dupuis suffered powerful flashbacks and panic attacks that left her gasping for air . Diagnosed with PTSD , she transferred to Ottawa , then took a leave . She left the military for good in 2014. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her story is a familiar one . A &lt;a href= " https : **33;10321;TOOLONG ... Affairs Canada report last year&lt;/a&gt; revealed that about 16 per cent of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s Afghan veterans &amp;mdash ; more than 6,700 soldiers &amp;mdash ; have been diagnosed with PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The PTSD rate remained high in Afghanistan even though the military had tried to carefully prepare soldiers for the stress of war . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Soldiers were briefed about the nature of stress injuries and the importance of seeking early treatment . Those leaving Afghanistan were given an overseas decompression period and repeatedly screened for PTSD or related afflictions . But the &amp;ldquo ; psychological inoculation&amp;rdquo ; of soldiers did little to reduce the overall incidence of PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I personally feel I was absolutely ready to face going to Afghanistan , &amp;rdquo ; said Dupuis. &amp;ldquo ; I was given very good training and it showed . We were able to react to the IED attack . But how do you prepare people to see a horrible scene like that ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; After her diagnosis , Dupuis explored a variety of treatments , including cognitive therapy , a kind of talk therapy designed to help patients identify negative patterns in the way they perceive and deal with everyday events . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At Montfort Hospital , she tried a newly developed treatment , &lt;a href= " https : **33;10356;TOOLONG ... movement desensitization and reprocessing&lt;/a&gt; ( EMDR ) , which had been shown to help people process their traumatic memories through a series of guided , rapid eye movements . The therapy is believed to mimic the beneficial effects of rapid-eye movement ( REM ) sleep , which plays a role in the healthy storage of emotional memories . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; EMDR was really difficult , &amp;rdquo ; said Dupuis. &amp;ldquo ; It would drain me a lot because it takes you right back to the trauma : You would feel like you are still there . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dupuis worked with a massage therapist , who helped her with meditation and breathing techniques , and also became involved with Soldier On , an organization devoted to helping injured soldiers through athletics . She co-captained Team Canada at the 2017 Invictus Games in Toronto , and won three gold medals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; For me , I think the big breakthrough came with sport &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; but I had to be ready for that breakthrough , &amp;rdquo ; Dupuis said . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s a very complex recovery process . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Although she still suffers the occasional flashback &amp;mdash ; fireworks or big crowds can trigger them &amp;mdash ; Dupuis has returned to work as a peer support co-ordinator with Operational Stress Injury Social Support , a joint program of Veterans Affairs Canada and the Department of National Defence . She helps soldiers and veterans with PTSD. &amp;ldquo ; I share my experience , &amp;rdquo ; she said , &amp;ldquo ; and try to give them hope that it is possible to have a better life . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s most decorated soldiers have suffered the effects of PTSD , including &lt;a href= " https : **33;10391;TOOLONG ... William Barker&lt;/a&gt; , &lt;a href= " https : **33;10426;TOOLONG ... Prince&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href= " https : **33;10461;TOOLONG ... Dallaire&lt;/a&gt; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;10496;TOOLONG ... title= " Retired General , Senator Romeo Dallaire. " alt= " Retired General , Senator Romeo Dallaire. " data-portal-copyright= " Peter Bregg " **28;10532;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500698 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It underlines the fact that a century after shellshock was first described in a medical journal , doctors still can&amp;rsquo ; t predict who will suffer PTSD when exposed to the horrors of war , or why some soldiers are affected and not others . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Through statistical analysis , researchers know women are more likely than men to develop PTSD ; that people with pre-existing psychiatric conditions are more vulnerable ; and that risk increases with each additional overseas deployment a soldier undertakes. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Scientists are also slowly coming to understand what actually happens in the brain of those with PTSD : Research suggests the amygdala , an almond-shaped region of the brain that processes emotions , is overactive along with parts of the prefrontal cortex that promote fear . They&amp;rsquo ; re now examining PTSD-altered brains on a neuronal level , which raises the potential for targeted new treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dupuis , however , said soldiers experience PTSD so differently that it&amp;rsquo ; s hard to believe there will ever be a single cure &amp;mdash ; other than an end to war . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The brain , I think , has its limits : I think there&amp;rsquo ; s only so much a human can take . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;10562;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;10604;TOOLONG ... " data-portal-copyright= " Obencem " **28;10987;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1497392 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dire as they are , the numbers don&amp;rsquo ; t tell the whole story . But here are a few , to give you a sense of the problem : &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; Seven years ago , there were 19,615 frail Ontarians waitlisted to get into long-term care . By last year , there were 34,862 &amp;ndash ; &amp;shy ; a 78 per cent increase . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; Last year , the median wait time for these frail &amp;ndash ; usually elderly &amp;ndash ; people to enter long-term care in this area was 186 days . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; Between 2011 and 2018 , the number of long-term care beds in Ontario barely grew , whereas the population 75 years old and up swelled by 20 per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; The wait list to get into long-term care will go up over the next five years , despite the Ontario government&amp;rsquo ; s plan to quickly add 15,000 long-term spaces . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; If you have an elderly relative or friend , you care about this outrageous reality &amp;ndash ; in which frail older couples are sent to different care homes after decades of marriage ; families are given virtually no choice about the quality or location of the long-term home their relative goes to ; people are forced to make long-term care decisions on a few days&amp;rsquo ; notice ; and loved ones deteriorate as they wait months it to access this care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;11017;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;11059;TOOLONG ... health care will get worse despite massive investment in long-term care , warns fiscal **29;11094;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; If you don&amp;rsquo ; t have a loved one in this situation , there&amp;rsquo ; s another reason to care : thousands of people who are waiting for long-term care are stuck in hospital beds , a major cause of the &amp;ldquo ; hallway health care&amp;rdquo ; crisis . In late 2018 , 4,600 of Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s 34,000 hospital beds were taken by people waiting for long-term care or rehab. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; To its credit , the current Ontario government is frantically adding long-term care spaces , struggling to compensate for the last government&amp;rsquo ; s spending neglect in this area . But as numbers released by the &lt;a href= " https : **33;11125;TOOLONG ... Accountability Officer&lt;/a&gt; show , the fresh spending will merely hold waitlists where they are , not lessen the problem . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Longer-term thinking is imperative . We need real partnerships between retirement homes and long-term care facilities , both in providing care and helping seniors stay healthier for longer so they don&amp;rsquo ; t have to move to long-term care as early . Hiring , training and properly paying personal support workers must also become a priority . And yes , there is room for more private spending by those who can afford it ; government needs to permit this . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The ebb and flow of spending from government to government on long-term beds is no permanent fix &amp;ndash ; as the numbers , and the heartbreaking stories , show . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN CITIZEN OPINIONS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;11160;TOOLONG ... gyroscope ; picture-in-picture " **34;11446;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; She said her father was thrilled with the news . &amp;ldquo ; He was so happy . &amp;rdquo ; Her mother&amp;rsquo ; s physician , meanwhile , had provided her with a letter that said bringing them together would be a significant boost to her mental health . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Joan , who uses a wheelchair , will have a room on the ground floor &amp;mdash ; he&amp;rsquo ; s on the third &amp;mdash ; but it&amp;rsquo ; s expected the couple can spend a good part of the day together and share meals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Bertrand thought the prolonged separation bordered on the &amp;ldquo ; criminal , &amp;rdquo ; and had expressed frustration in dealing with the Local Health Integration Network , which oversees long-term care placement . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The LHIN says it is committed to reuniting separated couples as soon as is practical and it uses a ranking system to establish a priority list . &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt; It reunites about 120 couples annually in its stable of 60 facilities and the average wait time is 40 to 50 days . &lt;/span&gt; Scientists are working on a pill for that&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Kelly EganFri , 01 Nov 2019 21:54:55 **45;11679;TOOLONG ... **37;11726;TOOLONG : We all need to push for change in Ontario 's health care systemhttp : **34;11765;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;11801;TOOLONG ... title= " Frail and vulnerable Ontarians are waiting months , sometimes years , for needed services . " alt= " Frail and vulnerable Ontarians are waiting months , sometimes years , for needed services . " data-portal-copyright= " Chaiyaporn1144 " **28;11837;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1495329 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; I am a retired social worker . For the last 17 years of my career , I worked in hospitals , primarily as a discharge planner . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; We have known for years that the health care system was in grave danger due to our rapidly aging population . Despite this knowledge , virtually nothing significant has been done for the last 20 years to prepare for this wave of aging people . My hospital experience has taught me that most people have no interest in this issue until it affects their family ; then they are shocked at how the system has deteriorated , and angry that they can&amp;rsquo ; t get what they need or want . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Because of the failure of the system to adapt , the burden is falling on the most expensive part of the system , the hospitals , to be the failsafe for everything . Now we see different parts of the health system starting to blame each other for the problems . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;11867;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;11909;TOOLONG ... care waiting lists will grow despite the addition of new beds : FAO&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; What we need is an expanded home-care system , where the workers are licensed , properly trained and paid a reasonable wage . We need more subsidized retirement homes and convalescent beds for those who no longer need to be in hospital but can&amp;rsquo ; t afford the exorbitant rates of for-profit retirement homes . We need many more long-term care beds , as there is a portion of the population that can&amp;rsquo ; t be cared for in the community due to physical-care needs or behavioural issues due to mental health issues and/or dementia . We need more timely access to rehab beds and palliative care beds. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On any given day , at least 300 patients take up beds in acute care hospitals in Ottawa because they have nowhere else to go . This costs us , the taxpayers , more than $1,000 per day , per patient . It also means that those beds are not available to the people crammed into the ER on stretchers . Think about this the next time you read an article about a family member with a loved one in a hospital bed who will only accept one or two long-term care facilities for that loved one and expects to keep them in hospital as they wait for the LTC bed ( sometimes , that&amp;rsquo ; s a two-to-four year waiting list ) . The Ontario government has never stopped this practice , so the hospitals are held hostage . Hospital administrators don&amp;rsquo ; t speak out publicly for fear of reprisal from the Ministry of Health and Long-term Care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hospital social workers have the unique and often conflicting task of advocating for their clients&amp;rsquo ; needs while trying ensure timely discharges . I have sat with many tearful , desperate , overwhelmed , frightened patients and family members who are forced to settle for less than they need . I have made countless phone calls and fought many battles for them for resources that don&amp;rsquo ; t exist or are inadequate . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Surprisingly though , when I would strongly advise families of patients to advocate for themselves by calling their MPP , very few would actually follow up . &amp;nbsp ; This issue is rarely central at election time ; most people are only concerned about which party will lower their taxes . If you go to the media with your story , you can expect a quick resolution to get you out of the media spotlight . But this doesn&amp;rsquo ; t solve anything for everyone else who is waiting . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Should a devoted couple be kept apart for seven months , as is the case in &lt;a href= " https : **33;11944;TOOLONG ... recent instance&lt;/a&gt; cited by the Citizen ? Absolutely not . Is their situation a higher priority than the isolated frail person with advanced dementia who lives unsafely in the community , or the burned-out caregiver who had been waiting patiently in the queue for three years for a bed for their loved one ? In this time of not enough resources to go around , how does the Local Health Integration Network , and we as a larger society , make the decisions about who is the most &amp;ldquo ; deserving&amp;rdquo ; of scarce resources ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Instead of discussing who is more &amp;ldquo ; deserving , &amp;rdquo ; we can work together to demand the changes needed to our health care system . This means getting involved , not just when our family members are in hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **28;11979;TOOLONG Dawson&lt;/strong&gt; , MSW , was an Ottawa social worker for 34 years , working in child and family counselling , addictions treatment , child welfare , health care and dementia . &lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN CITIZEN OPINIONS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;12009;TOOLONG ... letters : Nursing shortage in Quebec ? OC Transpo 's reaction to LRT issues -- too slow , too vague , too defensive&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Gail DawsonWed , 30 Oct 2019 16:54:19 **45;12114;TOOLONG ... treatment an unprecedented leap forward in addressing damage after heart attackhttp : **34;12161;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;12197;TOOLONG ... title= " Veronika Sedlakova , post-doctoral fellow ( L ) , Sarah Mclaughlin , PhD(c) , and Dr. Emilio Alarcon , principal investigator and laboratory director , University of Ottawa Heart Institute , have developed a recombinant human collagen matrix for cardiac tissue repair . October 24 , 2019. " alt= " Veronika Sedlakova , post-doctoral fellow ( L ) , Sarah Mclaughlin , PhD(c) , and Dr. Emilio Alarcon , principal investigator and laboratory director , University of Ottawa Heart Institute , have developed a recombinant human collagen matrix for cardiac tissue repair . October 24 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Errol McGihon " **28;12233;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1491854 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It took years for Emilio Alarcon and his University of Ottawa Heart Institute research team to fully believe what they were seeing &amp;mdash ; that a gel they had developed containing human collagen was repairing damaged hearts in mice . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They repeated their research study several times , coming up with randomized , blinded results , just to make sure the findings were correct . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We had to complete it many times because we couldn&amp;rsquo ; t believe it , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The results of that work , five years in the making , were published in the prestigious journal Nature Communications on Friday . Alarcon and Erik Suuronen , a scientist in the division of cardiac surgery and director of its biomaterials and regeneration program , are lead authors . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The injectable material is the first in the world prepared using human collagen and is being called an unprecedented leap forward in addressing repair of cardiac muscle after a heart attack . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The work is still years away from clinical use on humans , but the findings are expected to attract attention because of the potential of the gel , which is designed to be injected into the hearts of patients with damaged heart tissue after a heart attack . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Alarcon said his team believes it performs better than any of the cell-based therapies or drug treatments currently available . The treatment works , he said , by increasing the number of cardiac muscle cells and blood capillaries in the tissue around damaged areas of the heart . The gel also helps bring more wound-healing cells to the site to promote repair . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Heart diseases are the leading cause of death around the world , and coronary artery disease , which can lead to a heart attack , is the most common . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; About 10 per cent of people who have a heart attack ( or myocardial infarction ) will develop scarring and thickening of the heart wall that can lead to heart failure , which has a high mortality rate and its treatment is costly to the health system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Alarcon noted that patients in remote areas without easy access to health care and those who don&amp;rsquo ; t seek immediate treatment or don&amp;rsquo ; t know they had a heart attack are more likely to suffer heart muscle damage . The treatment being developed by the heart institute team restored heart function in mice with scarred cardiac muscle . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The heart institute&amp;rsquo ; s BioEngineering and Therapeutic Solutions ( BEaTS ) team &amp;ldquo ; are hopeful their human collagen gel will one day lead to a recovery of heart function and prevent heart failure in humans , &amp;rdquo ; said Alarcon , &amp;ldquo ; but more testing is required . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The study published Friday is a first step toward that potential treatment , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Canada , approximately 600,000 patients live with advanced heart failure and health-care costs amount to more than $2.8 billion every year . As people live longer , both the number of patients and the cost of treatments are likely to increase . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;12263;TOOLONG ... airline fares are the best way to score super cheap plane tickets .
