His wife Joan , meanwhile , is 25 kilometres away in Saint Vincent Hospital on Cambridge Street , getting by in a wheelchair as she struggles with severe depression . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Bertrand , an only child , now spends her days ferrying between the two health-care facilities and battling &amp;ldquo ; the system&amp;rdquo ; to find a suitable west-end location to reunite them in what could be their final months . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Horrific , &amp;rdquo ; she says of her experience . &amp;ldquo ; People have no idea . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her parents lived in the same house in Nepean for 56 years , she said , and moved close to her in Richmond about six years ago as age-related problems began to crop up . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; All was well for the first four years until her father&amp;rsquo ; s memory loss became quite pronounced , she explained , but family was close by to help with food , basic care and errands. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;span style= " font-family : -apple-system , BlinkMacSystemFont , ' Segoe UI ' , Roboto , Oxygen-Sans , Ubuntu , Cantarell , ' Helvetica Neue ' , sans-serif ; " &gt;On March 1 , worried that Diffin 's sudden delirium was due to a urinary tract infection , she took him to the Queensway-Carleton Hospital . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We were 100 per cent thinking he would be home in 48 hours or be sent home with a prescription . But he never left . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Bertrand said a so-called MoCA test ( Montreal Cognitive Assessment ) determined her father could not be discharged into a living situation where his spouse was not mobile or healthy enough to care for him properly . So Diffin became a patient on the Alternate Level of Care unit ( where he paid $1,900 a month ) until he was relocated to his own room at Granite about two weeks ago . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Joan , meanwhile , was admitted to Queensway-Carleton on April 4 , also with a suspected infection , a side effect of which was an inability to move her feet . She was eventually transferred to Saint Vincent for longer-term rehabilitation . But she is doing poorly , Bertrand says , and even deliberately flung herself out of the wheelchair . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; My mother&amp;rsquo ; s clearly given up . They need to be together . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;12619;TOOLONG ... title= " Donna Bertrand and her father Charles Diffin at his healthy care facility . Charles and his wife Joan are being cared for in separate health care facilities and Donna has been trying to get them united . " alt= " Donna Bertrand and her father Charles Diffin at his healthy care facility . Charles and his wife Joan are being cared for in separate health care facilities and Donna has been trying to get them united . " data-portal-copyright= " Errol McGihon " **28;12655;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1491098 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; And this is where things get complicated , as Bertrand says she&amp;rsquo ; s been trying to get the authorities to place them together since May . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You have to fight and it&amp;rsquo ; s exhausting . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She did admit workers at the Local Health Integration Network have offered to move the couple to a handful of longterm care facilities , but at least one was in the east end and the others , in her opinion , were either badly located or had poor reputations . She has countered with a list of five facilities in either Stittsville , Kanata or Barrhaven. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Bertrand , too , says workers pressure families to put their elderly loved one in a home that isn&amp;rsquo ; t their preferred choice . Neither is there a great avenue of appeal , she added . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I liken it to a fortress , and the social workers stand guard . They protect the LHIN. &amp;rdquo ; She says her mother is now at the top of the list to be transferred to Granite but it could take several weeks , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Six weeks does not sound like a long time , but I&amp;rsquo ; m starting to panic . &amp;rdquo ; Her father , already bone thin , is hardly eating anything and her mother is having delusional episodes , she says . She produced a letter , in fact , from Joan&amp;rsquo ; s physician , Dr. Daniel Levine , that said her depression is &amp;ldquo ; increasingly worse&amp;rdquo ; and concludes : &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Due to her current state and condition , it would be imperative at this time that Mrs. Diffin be reunited with her husband , as this would provide significant assistance with her recovery . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Charlie , who was inducted in the Ottawa Sports Hall of Fame in 2003 , was in good spirits when we visited but sounded fatalistic about his future . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; ve had my fun and I&amp;rsquo ; ve had a lot of people helping me. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Bertrand says the criteria followed by the LHIN makes spousal reunification a priority in &amp;ldquo ; crisis&amp;rdquo ; cases . &amp;ldquo ; To me , this is a crisis . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On its website , the LHIN tells residents its goal is to reunite couples &amp;ldquo ; as soon as possible&amp;rdquo ; and it uses a ranking system to triage the waiting list . Those separated the longest , for instance , are to be offered beds first , though other factors &amp;mdash ; like availability , level of care and an individual&amp;rsquo ; s health condition &amp;mdash ; are also in play . It reunites about 120 couples annually in its stable of 60 facilities and the average wait time is 40 to 50 days . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t want this to happen to another family , &amp;rdquo ; said Bertrand , of her decision to go public . &amp;ldquo ; This is just so wrong . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; To contact Kelly Egan , please call 613-726-5896 or email kegan@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **27;12685;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;12714;TOOLONG ... after promising Alzheimer 's drug declared a failure , new analysis suggests it was effective&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;12749;TOOLONG ... show confusion , misinformation during LRT delays&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;12784;TOOLONG ... it take two people getting shot to bring attention to this ? ' : Short-term rental meeting draws a crowd&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Kelly EganThu , 24 Oct 2019 10:47:44 **45;12819;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsHow the elderly and frail are caught in the crosshairs of push to end hallway medicinehttp : **32;12866;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;12900;TOOLONG ... title= " Melanie Dea 's husband Richard Martin , 75 , was diagnosed with Huntington 's Disease 12 years ago . She was recently handed a bill for his care from the Montfort Hospital after she refused to take a spot for him in a facility with a woeful reputation in an Alzheimer 's unit . " alt= " Melanie Dea 's husband Richard Martin , 75 , was diagnosed with Huntington 's Disease 12 years ago . She was recently handed a bill for his care from the Montfort Hospital after she refused to take a spot for him in a facility with a woeful reputation in an Alzheimer 's unit . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;12936;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1485151 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hospitals are not the right place for them and their families can&amp;rsquo ; t care for them at home . The elderly and frail are increasingly collateral damage in the drive to end hallway medicine in Ontario , say advocates and families . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Patients who occupy hospital beds but no longer need acute care , ALC &amp;mdash ; alternative level of care &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; patients are a key factor in hospital overcrowding . But with record waiting lists for long-term care beds and shortages of home care workers , patients and their families say they are caught in the middle and feeling pressured. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is a crisis , &amp;rdquo ; said Melanie Dea of Rockland , who recently experienced that pressure first hand . Her husband Richard Martin , who has Huntington&amp;rsquo ; s disease , was treated at Montfort Hospital for pneumonia in July . By August he had improved , but was on a waiting list for long-term care and Dea could not safely care for him at home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said the hospital suggested he go to an Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s unit of a long-term care home . Rea refused because her husband does not have dementia . The hospital began charging him $62.18 a day , a co-payment she says she will not pay . He has since moved to a long-term care home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , the wife of an elderly patient in the same hospital room was in tears after being told her husband was being discharged , said Dea . The man ended up in the hospital because he was wandering the streets at night and his wife could no longer care for him . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Trevor Mertz of Chesterville , says his mother-in-law felt pressured to move into an Ottawa long-term care home by staff at Winchester Hospital when she was there in 2017. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They said , &amp;lsquo ; You have two hours to decide or the spot will be gone . ' &amp;rdquo ; Her stay at the home , with a history of health and safety violations , was a &amp;ldquo ; nightmare&amp;rdquo ; , according to Mertz . She eventually moved to another home , but died soon after . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You shouldn&amp;rsquo ; t pressure people on a Friday , saying you have two hours to make a decision . If I had seen the place , I would have said no. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jane Meadus of the Toronto-based Advocacy Centre for the Elderly said her organization hears from families on a daily basis who are distraught about having to quickly find a solution for a frail relative being discharged from hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They come to us in tears . It is our biggest thing right now and it is just heartbreaking . It has always happened , but the pressure on people is worse now . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus said some patients are being illegally prevented from applying for long-term care from hospital or forced into retirement homes to wait until a less expensive long-term care bed becomes available . &amp;ldquo ; We have got two-tier medicine on the backs of seniors , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hospital officials , meanwhile , say the hospital is not where frail and elderly patients in need of chronic care should be . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cholly Boland , CEO of Winchester Hospital , would not discuss individual cases , but said the hospital&amp;rsquo ; s philosophy is that it is not good to be in a hospital if you don&amp;rsquo ; t need to be . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If you are a person within the ALC category , by definition you do not need to be in the hospital and in general , it is not a good place to be . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Montfort Hospital spokesperson Genevi&amp;egrave ; ve Picard said patients are charged a co-payment when they are waiting in hospital for a long-term care bed , according to provincial policy . The preference , though , is for them to apply from home . &amp;ldquo ; Research has demonstrated that it is easier for people to make important decisions for the next stage while they are in their regular environment and can validate if they can safely remain in their home . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said she is aware of cases in which people have felt pressured to leave , but added patients will get better care tailored to their needs at home with service providers , in a retirement home or long-term care home . &amp;ldquo ; We know that situations such as these are stressful times for the patients and their loved ones . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Leah Levesque , head of nursing at Queensway Carleton Hospital , acknowledged that the transition from hospital to home or institutional care can be hard on families . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I think the bottom line for us is we think patients should be in the right bed getting appropriate care from the most appropriate providers . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That can be easier said than done , though . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The average wait in the Ottawa area for long-term care was 186 days in 2017 , above the provincial average of 146 days . In addition , support worker shortages and increasing demand mean home care is not always available or reliable . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Alan Forster , vice president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital , said making sure ALC patients get appropriate care is a societal issue . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If we continue to use hospitals as the place of last resort for people and don&amp;rsquo ; t figure out an alternative for people who are frail and in need of close attention , if we don&amp;rsquo ; t make places for that part of the population , then it will get worse for individuals who are in that situation and increasingly difficult for folks not in that situation . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are currently between 150 and 200 ALC patients at The Ottawa Hospital on any given day . Montfort has seen a 75 per cent increase in ALC patients in the past three years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus , meanwhile , said her organization sees daily evidence that families and patients are bearing the brunt of the push to end hallway medicine . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We see people being sent home , families being told to mortgage their house to pay for parents&amp;rsquo ; care in a retirement home , &amp;rdquo ; she said . The Advocacy Centre for the Elderly also sees seniors being discharged to homeless shelters , motels and transitional homes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Everyone talks about hallway medicine and those taking up the beds should be in long-term care . But no one ever talks about the effects on those people . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;12966;TOOLONG ... use admission at U.S. border snagging Canadian boomers , says lawyer&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13001;TOOLONG ... 10 hours or more a day can lead to greater risks of stroke , study finds&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13036;TOOLONG ... is the limit ' for heart treatment without invasive surgery&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneWed , 16 Oct 2019 10:43:45 **45;13071;TOOLONG ... NewsMenSeniorsWomen'Sky is the limit ' for heart treatment without invasive surgeryhttp : **33;13118;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;13153;TOOLONG ... title= " The team involved in pioneering a new procedure that , instead of open heart surgery , fixes a deadly heart arrhythmia with a non-invasive procedure that takes only minutes includes Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " alt= " The team involved in pioneering a new procedure that , instead of open heart surgery , fixes a deadly heart arrhythmia with a non-invasive procedure that takes only minutes includes Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;13189;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1482574 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; When the treatment was done , the patient looked confused . &amp;ldquo ; Is that it ? &amp;rdquo ; he asked Dr. Calum Redpath. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That was it . The potentially lifesaving treatment for a deadly heart ailment was completed in a painless , non-invasive session . The patient was free to go . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Performed by a team of doctors from the University of Ottawa Heart Institute in partnership with doctors at The Ottawa Hospital cancer centre , the treatment done in late September was a first in Canada . The Heart Institute is now one of the few centres in the world capable of performing it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This new technique offers patients a treatment option that does not require the body to be opened in any way , &amp;rdquo ; Redpath said . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s a real game-changing innovation , almost a fairytale . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Although it is still experimental and being offered on a compassionate basis , Redpath believes the &amp;ldquo ; sky is the limit&amp;rdquo ; for the ways the procedure could change treatment of a common and often deadly heart arrhythmia called ventricular tachycardia , including enabling it to be performed on more patients in smaller centres using equipment used to treat cancer patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;13219;TOOLONG ... title= " The team behind the breakthrough procedure in Ottawa includes , left to right , Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath , medical physicist Katie Lekx-Toniolo and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " alt= " The team behind the breakthrough procedure in Ottawa includes , left to right , Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath , medical physicist Katie Lekx-Toniolo and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;13255;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1482623 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sudden cardiac death kills around 50,000 Canadians every year . Most of those deaths are caused by ventricular arrhythmia , the condition that is the focus of the new non-invasive treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Conventional treatment for ventricular arrhythmia involves surgery : usually through a catheter inserted in an artery or vein in the patient&amp;rsquo ; s groin . Once inside , radio-frequency energy or other methods are used to destroy the damaged area of the patient&amp;rsquo ; s heart muscle that is causing rapid and irregular heartbeats . That ablation , as it is known , is done to restore a regular heartbeat . More than 150 such surgeries are performed at the Heart Institute every year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The new non-invasive procedure first performed in Ottawa last month could end the need for traditional heart surgery to treat the common and often deadly heart arrhythmia . Instead of time in an operating room , anesthesia , pain medicine and days of hospitalization and recovery , the patient who underwent the treatment last month put his shirt back on and walked home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Current drug treatment for the condition are either ineffective or have side-effects , and the invasive procedures , including catheter ablation surgery or implanting a defibrillator , require prolonged surgery with potential complications and sometimes long recoveries. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The procedure that uses two novel therapies , took place in late September . First , the patient underwent electrocardiographic imaging , in which doctors mapped the electrical activity of his heart using advanced imaging technology . During that procedure , the patient was fully awake and conscious , said Dr. Andrew Crean , co-director of the cardiac MRI service at the Heart Institute . He works with Redpath to identify damaged heart tissue causing arrhythmias. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Using that map , a radiation oncologist burned off damaged areas of the heart muscle using technology normally used to treat cancer patients . No surgery was needed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re excited to use this treatment now on portions of the heart , with the hope that , with just one 30-minute treatment , our current patients will live longer and eventually contribute to research studies so we can learn more about who can benefit the most from this and by how much , said Dr. Graham Cook , a radiation oncologist working on the treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Redpath says he expects up to six patients to receive the treatment in Ottawa in coming months . They are all patients who are unable or unwilling to pursue conventional treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; So far , 30 patients have been treated , mostly through Washington University in St. Louis , where it was developed . Among those patients , there is a 94 per cent success rate . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But , before it can be widely used , the treatment will be studied to determine whether it is worthwhile . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Redpath said the procedure is significantly less costly than conventional treatment &amp;mdash ; maybe five times less than surgery . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; If it were to become a routine treatment , he said , it could change how many patients were treated by allowing smaller hospitals to treat patients , even using it as a preventative procedure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The sky is the limit . There is really no limit to the number of patients that could be treated , &amp;rdquo ; Redpath said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13285;TOOLONG ... company that made Ottawa 's LRT trains wo n't discuss them&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13320;TOOLONG ... have Michelin stars evaded Canada 's top restaurants ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13355;TOOLONG ... with child labour ? These are in-patient beds during times when the hospital is unusually crowded , including flu season . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There&amp;rsquo ; s also a boost of funding for nurses to handle patients arriving by ambulance at hospitals . Paramedics have been widely delayed in returning to the road because they bring in patients , and the hospital can&amp;rsquo ; t deal with them right away . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; To end hallway health care , we must build capacity across the system and throughout a patient&amp;rsquo ; s journey through the system , &amp;rdquo ; Fullerton said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This means investing in community-based programs including home and community care and in integrated , innovating solutions to end hallway health care . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " http : //twitter.com/TomSpears1 " &gt;tspea ... @postmedia.com&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " http : //twitter.com/TomSpears1 " &gt;twitt ... &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS : &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13924;TOOLONG ... church treasurer handed two-year prison term&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;13959;TOOLONG ... Yes , that may be a cooling . &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Tom SpearsFri , 04 Oct 2019 19:14:14 **45;14029;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsCloutier : In this federal election , let 's talk about funding health infrastructurehttp : **26;14076;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;14104;TOOLONG ... title= " Current federal programs make it difficult to build new hospital infrastructure . " alt= " Current federal programs make it difficult to build new hospital infrastructure . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;14140;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1463291 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Former prime minister Kim Campbell is famously quoted as saying during the 1993 election campaign that &amp;ldquo ; an election is no time to discuss serious issues . &amp;rdquo ; With apologies to Ms. Campbell , election campaigns are excellent ways for Canadians to hold their politicians&amp;rsquo ; feet to the fire on the issues that matter to voters . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Case in point : Canadians will be going to the polls in just over a month and it is high time we heard , from those who seek to represent us in Parliament , how they would actually support innovation and transformation in Canada&amp;rsquo ; s health system in a meaningful way . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That Canadians actually want federal leadership in health care is a fact that has been proven in poll after poll for decades . The latest evidence is an Angus Reid poll released Aug. 26 that found 71 per cent of uncommitted voters consider improving access to health care their top election issue , a figure that jumps to 80 per cent among Canadians over age 55. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But where to begin ? As a first step , candidates could correct the longstanding oversight of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s federal government to not invest in health care infrastructure or in innovation led by health institutions . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2018 , the government of Canada set out an ambitious infrastructure plan calling for investments of more than $180 billion over the course of 12 years . These investments will fund transportation infrastructure , water and wastewater facilities , as well as education and social infrastructure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Not included on that list ? Health care infrastructure . In fact , hospitals have been explicitly excluded from accessing Infrastructure Canada&amp;rsquo ; s funding programs for more than a decade . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada&amp;rsquo ; s innovation programs &amp;ndash ; whether administered via Innovation Canada or through the Canada Foundation for Innovation ( CFI ) &amp;ndash ; select against leadership by health care providers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Innovation Canada favours industry leadership , both in its Innovative Superclusters Initiative and through its Strategic Innovation Fund and tends to relegate health care organizations to a supporting role . Meanwhile , CFI requires that any hospital application have sign-off from the president of a university , whose priorities are likely to lie elsewhere . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Why do we do this ? Why do we design these programs in ways that make it either difficult or impossible for hospitals , researchers and patients to benefit ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some may argue that federalism stands in the way ; that anything that happens in a hospital is outside the scope of the federal government , but that is incorrect . In fact , the federal Hospital Construction Grant Program that ran from 1948 to 1970 was the financial engine that built the current hospital infrastructure . Unfortunately , that engine has sputtered to a halt . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canadian voters must hold the federal government responsible for the care and maintenance of the hospitals it helped to create . Many of these institutions are now desperately in need of renewal even as demand increases due to our growing and aging population . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nearly half of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s hospitals are more than 50 years old and it is impossible to expect 21st-century medicine in a building designed for care models of a half a century ago . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Our aging hospital infrastructure also has the unfortunate side effect of consuming approximately 11 per cent of total public energy and accounting collectively for more than five per cent of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s greenhouse gas footprint . Not only is modern hospital infrastructure important for providing better care , it is also a much greener solution . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In late July , among many other similar announcements , the federal government pledged $1 million to provide much-needed upgrades to 46-year old Blackburn Arena in Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s east end . Fair enough , but during this election campaign let&amp;rsquo ; s put the puck in the net where it really counts : innovating health care for all Canadians . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **29;14170;TOOLONG ? mile Cloutier&lt;/strong&gt; is President and CEO of &lt;a href= " http : //www.healthcarecan.ca/ " &gt;Health ... the&lt;span style= " font-family : -apple-system , BlinkMacSystemFont , ' Segoe UI ' , Roboto , Oxygen-Sans , Ubuntu , Cantarell , ' Helvetica Neue ' , sans-serif ; " &gt; national voice of health care organizations and hospitals across Canada . Its goal is to improve the health of Canadians through an evidence-based and innovative healthcare system . &lt;/span&gt;&lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;14201;TOOLONG ... a weekend of fun , Ottawa 's light-rail transit system faces busy Monday rush-hour test drive&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;14236;TOOLONG ... Do n't move to the country to build your dream home&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;14271;TOOLONG ... raise questions about Kanata Conservative candidate who once appeared in video with Faith Goldy&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Paul- ? mile CloutierMon , 16 Sep 2019 12:13:25 **45;14306;TOOLONG ... 2019Family &amp;amp ; **33;14353;TOOLONG changer ' : Community health centres lead in building Ottawa health teamhttp : **36;14388;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;14426;TOOLONG ... title= " A stock file photo of a senior woman receiving assistance from a health care worker with a walking frame . " alt= " A stock file photo of a senior woman receiving assistance from a health care worker with a walking frame . " data-portal-copyright= " Daisy-Daisy " **28;14462;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1460777 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; When Ontario overhauls the health system in coming months , Ottawa will buck a provincial trend by putting community health centres , not hospitals , at the centre of the new system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ottawa Health Team , as it is known , is one of 31 groups across the province that have been given the green light to apply to become some of the province&amp;rsquo ; s first health teams . A coalition of six community health centres are at the centre of the Ottawa proposal , which also includes The Ottawa Hospital , Bruy&amp;egrave ; re , Ottawa Inner City Health , Carefor and Ottawa Public Health , along with numerous other organizations , totalling 47 partners . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Two other Ottawa-area teams have also received green lights to move forward : one focused on children&amp;rsquo ; s health that includes CHEO and more than 50 other members , including Eastern Ontario hospitals ; and one in the city&amp;rsquo ; s east end that includes the Montfort Hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The province announced last spring it would call for groups to form health teams as part of a major revamp of the provincial health system . The Ontario government is in the process of disbanding the LHIN ( local health integration ) system and has concentrated most health organizations in the province into a central agency . LHINs are to be replaced by health teams , which are meant to co-ordinate care among various health organizations . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health Minister Christine Elliott has said the system will offer &amp;ldquo ; seamless access to health services , including primary care , hospitals , home and community care , palliative care , residential long-term care and mental health and addictions service . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;14492;TOOLONG ... title= " Ontario Health Minister Christine Elliott in a file photo from August . " alt= " Ontario Health Minister Christine Elliott in a file photo from August . " data-portal-copyright= " Tyler Kula " **28;14528;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1460779 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; But critics , including former deputy minister of health Dr. Bob Bell , have raised concerns about the lack of resources and planning for the complex new system and the fact that most teams that have been green-lighted to move ahead are led by hospitals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Ontario health teams seem at risk for becoming hospital-driven organizations , where the strength of the hospital board governs decisions in the region , &amp;rdquo ; Bell wrote in a recent opinion piece in the Toronto Star . He also wondered whether hospital-driven health teams might be able to &amp;ldquo ; resist the temptation to consolidate regional budgets to gradually provide better hospital funding ? &amp;rdquo ; He noted hospital budgets have been squeezed in recent years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But those at the centre of planning for the Ottawa Health Team say there is genuine excitement about the prospect of doing things in a different , more streamlined way . Community health centres , which already work with numerous organizations , are well connected , said Simone Thibault , executive director of Centretown Community Health Centre and spokesperson for the project . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If we are really wanting care to be mainly in the community , we feel that we are well connected . We don&amp;rsquo ; t work in silos . We work with many health and addiction agencies . We also believe that not just the individual should be taken care of , but the whole community . We are about having the right care , at the right time , with the right provider . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Thibault said the team would focus on primary care and preventative care and medicine , as well as acute care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But the aim of the team to to help fill gaps in the transitions between different types of care , she said . Those transitions often become what Thibault refers to as &amp;ldquo ; pain points . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; As community health centres , if we can be a part of the solution that could address those pain points , then we can do something . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Alan Forster , who is vice-president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital , said : &amp;ldquo ; Philosophically we are supportive of the region leading this . It is really important for us that the community is leading this and they own it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ongoing issues such as the number of alternate level of care patients occupying hospital beds are about &amp;ldquo ; how our community really supports people who are frail , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;14558;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Alan Forster is vice-president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital . " alt= " Dr. Alan Forster is vice-president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital . " data-portal-copyright= " The Ottawa Hospital " **28;14594;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1460783 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Trevor Eggleton , manager of communications at Carefor , a not-for-profit charity that provides home care and community support , says his organization is &amp;ldquo ; really excited&amp;rdquo ; about the possibilities of the new system , which he believes will streamline care , inspire innovation and create an environment of collaboration . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are still more questions than answers in what the system built on health teams will eventually look like . Among the looming issues is how teams made up of institutions with various electronic health systems will communicate with each other and share patient records . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Elliott has described it as being a system that puts patients first and moves with them through levels of care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For now , the 31 groups involved in trying to set up health teams in Ottawa and around the province are rushing to meet the next deadline &amp;mdash ; Oct. 9 &amp;mdash ; by which time they must have full applications completed . Teams are expected to be announced this fall . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ottawa Team had 11 initial partners , but more are joining , Thibault said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The CHEO team &amp;mdash ; which has 59 partners &amp;mdash ; is the only health team moving forward under an &amp;ldquo ; innovation category . &amp;rdquo ; It is kicking off a social media campaign asking children and teens to help name it . Joanne Lowe , executive director of the Youth Services Bureau , said the children&amp;rsquo ; s team would focus on the unique needs of youth and children . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt; " &lt;/span&gt; I think it speaks volume about how we see kids that we want to be at the front end of this new concept we think it can resolve some issues , &amp;rdquo ; Lowe said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Thibault acknowledged the task of trying to refocus and reorganize the health system is massive , but added : &amp;ldquo ; It is a huge game changer . We thought , if we had an opportunity to make change , we need to embrace it , to pull in our partners and to move it forward . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;14624;TOOLONG ... add extra joy -- and extra wheel -- to The Ottawa Hospital 's annual Ride&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;14659;TOOLONG ... Healthy Ottawa -- Let 's make it safer to move around the city&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;14694;TOOLONG ... future of health care has arrived -- but is the Ontario government paying attention ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneMon , 09 Sep 2019 13:57:12 **45;14729;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsMenSeniorsWomenCold storage : The promise of Ottawa 's cord blood bankhttp : **36;14776;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;14814;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " alt= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;14850;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1448089 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;14880;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; Inside two high-tech tanks filled with liquid nitrogen in an industrial park in Nepean there are about 2,500 little bags of frozen stem cells . The contents of each one of these little bags has the potential to save a life . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The &amp;ldquo ; **26;14916;TOOLONG ; freezers contain blood harvested from the umbilical cords and placentas of newborn babies , many of them in Ottawa . The immature stem cells in cord blood are used to treat more than 80 diseases , most frequently blood cancers and other blood disorders such as leukemia , lymphoma , aplastic anemia and sickle cell disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are other possible treatments in the pipeline . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In the future , stem cells might be used to treat a broad range of diseases , from diabetes to Parkinson&amp;rsquo ; s , multiple sclerosis and &amp;mdash ; this is controversial &amp;mdash ; autism . But no , the science does not make it possible to take cord blood and , say , grow a new kidney , said Heidi Elmoazzen , the stem cells director at Canadian Blood Services who has a PhD in cryobiology. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;14944;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " alt= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;14980;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1448089 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Canadian Blood Services cord blood bank in Nepean is one of three such public banks in Canada ( Quebec has its own ) and preserves about 72 per cent of the agency&amp;rsquo ; s cord blood inventory , with the rest stored in Edmonton . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On any typical day , one to three donations will arrive at the bank&amp;rsquo ; s lab from the birthing unit at the General campus of The Ottawa Hospital , one of four hospitals in Canada where mothers of newborns may opt to donate the cord blood harvested from their newborn&amp;rsquo ; s umbilical cord . Mothers-to-be have to register before the birth to donate and agree again after the birth . The process doesn&amp;rsquo ; t hurt the mother or the baby &amp;mdash ; the umbilical cord is typically treated as medical waste , anyway . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Donors do it for altruistic reasons , to potentially help someone they will never meet . Still , &amp;ldquo ; it&amp;rsquo ; s quite an easy sell , &amp;rdquo ; said Elmoazzen. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are three ways to donate stem cells : through the blood , usually after the donor is given a drug that makes the stem cells exit bone arrow and enter the bloodstream , from the bone marrow itself , typically from the pelvic bone , and through cord blood . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As transplants go , stem cell transplants are relatively simple . The bag of frozen stem cells , which is stored in a box about the size of an audio cassette tape , is retrieved from the cryopreservation freezer by a robotic arm . Still frozen , tit is transported wherever it is needed in the world inside in a container that looks like a high-tech milk can . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When it gets to its destination , the unit is thawed . The patient , whose own blood production has been knocked out with chemotherapy , is transfused with the stem cells . If all goes well , within a few weeks the stem cells are repopulating the patient&amp;rsquo ; s body with healthy blood cells . The more closely the donor and the recipient are matched , the less likely the recipient will suffer graft vs. host disease , an immune condition that occurs when the donor&amp;rsquo ; s &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;immune cells attack the recipient 's tissues . **26;15010;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Only about 14 per cent of the total 23,000 donations made in Canada since 2013 have been listed for possible matching . In the lab in Nepean , cord blood is tested for it suitability for donation . There are many reasons why a donation isn&amp;rsquo ; t eligible for transplant . The volume collected or the total nucleated cell count might be too low . The mother might have a health or social history that makes the donation ineligible . Or the cord blood might test positive for a transmissible disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The cord blood that does not qualify for donation can , with the mother&amp;rsquo ; s agreement , be used for research in cell therapy and transplant medicine . As of this year , the bank supports 17 cord-blood research projects . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are a lot of moving parts to a complicated international system . Canadian Blood Services made a business case for opening its own cord blood banks . It costs the Canadian health system nothing for a cord blood unit from Canadian Blood Services . It costs about $40,000 U.S. for a unit from outside the country , said Todd Campbell , Canadian Blood Services&amp;rsquo ; business support specialist . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The international exchange of stem cells is a two-way street . About 60 per cent of Canadian donations have gone to recipients in other countries , but 80 per cent of Canadian patients receive stem cells from donors in other countries . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On the average month , the bank will get 10 to 15 inquiries from transplant physicians around the world about a donation in one of the cord blood freezers . The units are listed in both a Canadian registry and an international database . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cord blood is often used to treat pediatric patients . One of its advantages is that can be retrieved and delivered very quickly . There are about 440,000 people on Canada&amp;rsquo ; s bone marrow registry , for example , but only about half of those who are approached actually agree to donate if they are requested . The reasons can vary &amp;mdash ; it might have been months or years since a potential donor signed up . The donor might be hard to locate , might have lost interest or have health problems . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada opened its first public cord-blood bank in 2013 . The goal was to create a multi-ethnic cord blood inventory to reflect the diversity of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s population . Since then , Canadian Blood Services has collected blood at four hospitals &amp;mdash ; in Ottawa , Edmonton , Brampton and Vancouver &amp;mdash ; and stores it in two banks , one in Ottawa and the other in Edmonton . The target was to get more than 60 per cent non-Caucasian donors . The bank has already exceeded that target . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re trying to build an inventory of cord blood to be able to transplant more patients in Canada , &amp;rdquo ; said Campbell . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;15038;TOOLONG ... title= " Medical laboratory technician Milenko Drajic checks for the growth of stem cells in a sample at the Nepean lab. " alt= " Medical laboratory technician Milenko Drajic checks for the growth of stem cells in a sample at the Nepean lab. " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;15074;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1448088 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are also seven private cord blood banks in Canada where parents can opt to store their newborn&amp;rsquo ; s own cord blood . However , there&amp;rsquo ; s a low probability of using one&amp;rsquo ; s own cord blood for transplantation &amp;mdash ; between one in 20,000 and one in 250,000 , according to Health Canada . When a patient needs cord blood transplantation , cells from a healthy donor are preferable if privately banked blood cells carry the same genetic or congenital disorder that caused the disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; A lot of people see ( private cord blood banking ) as an insurance policy . But if your child had leukemia , a physician would not use the child&amp;rsquo ; s own cord blood . The genetic mutation is in the stem cells themselves , &amp;rdquo ; said Elmoazzen. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The other problem is that a patient sometimes needs more stem cells to be transplanted than those contained in a single cord blood unit . In most cases , even if parents had stored a unit for family use , it would likely not be sufficient for a successful transplantation , and additional units stored in public use banks would still be needed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s like the dosage of medication . You need a minimum number of stem cells per kilogram of body weight , &amp;rdquo ; said Elmomazzen. &amp;ldquo ; A 20-pound patient needs less than an adult . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada has a target of creating a bank of about 10,000 cord blood units . But that&amp;rsquo ; s a moving target which is re-evaluated regularly and the science evolves . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; So far , 22 cord blood units have been withdrawn the Canadian public bank so far . Why so few ? Transplant surgeons have been looking to source stem cell donations not only from cord blood , but also from adult registries and family members of the patient . Even if a family member is a less-than-perfect match , graft vs. host disease can be managed using pharmaceuticals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , the worldwide cord blood supply is growing quickly . There are 780,000 donated units available through the World Marrow Donor Association . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One of the system&amp;rsquo ; s biggest problems for the system is lack of awareness , said Elmoazzen . But that is changing . When the cord blood bank opened six years ago , about 32 per cent of new mothers consented to donate . That has since increased to 47 per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In an ideal world , it would like it to be like recycling . Everybody just knows about it and does it , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Cord blood by the numbers : &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Minus 196 C : &lt;/strong&gt; Temperature at which cord blood is stored in liquid nitrogen &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;23,000 : &lt;/strong&gt; Qualified cord blood units collected since Canada&amp;rsquo ; s public bank launched in 2013 &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;3,300 : &lt;/strong&gt; Cord blood units stored and available for transplant &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;One in four : &lt;/strong&gt; Chance a patient needing a transplant will find a bone marrow donor match in his or her own family &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;$0 : &lt;/strong&gt; Costs to the Canadian medical system when a patient gets a cord blood donation from the Canadian Blood Services&amp;rsquo ; bank &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;$40,000 U.S : &lt;/strong&gt; Approximate cost if cord blood is sourced from elsewhere in the world &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;22 : &lt;/strong&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Withdrawals&amp;rdquo ; from the Canadian Blood Services cord blood bank since it was founded in 2013 &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;15104;TOOLONG ... Death and the excruciating act of letting go&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;15139;TOOLONG ... Healthy Ottawa -- From poodles to Persians , tabbies to terriers , our pets are good for us&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;15174;TOOLONG ... telescope offers rare glimpse of Earth-sized rocky exoplanet&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Joanne LauciusTue , 20 Aug 2019 14:40:44 **45;15209;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal **31;15256;TOOLONG , vulnerable people and minimal supervision : Lax security major long-term care riskhttp : **36;15289;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;15327;TOOLONG ... title= " Elderly patient taking pills with water in hospital room . " alt= " Elderly patient taking pills with water in hospital room . " data-portal-copyright= " Ridofranz " **28;15363;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1442547 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health-care workers who divert medications from patients are a risk in long-term care , &lt;a href= " https : **33;15393;TOOLONG ... inquiry probing the murders of eight elderly people at the hands of nurse Elizabeth Wettlaufer concluded last week . &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are all kinds of ways it can happen . Medication room doors may be left unlocked or open . A drug cart may be left unlocked and unattended while a nurse is at a patient&amp;rsquo ; s bedside . Medications can be stolen from bulk containers or waste buckets &amp;mdash ; a lot of medications used in nursing homes end up in waste buckets , destined to be destroyed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Perpetrators count on lax security and lack of oversight , the inquiry heard . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Wettlaufer killed her victims using unused insulin that had not been counted and was stored in medication room refrigerators . She told investigators she might not have been able to get away with diverting insulin if the medication room was made of glass . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Using diverted medication to kill patients is the most common tactic of health-care serial killers , the inquiry heard from researcher Beatrice Crofts Yorker , a professor at California State University Los Angeles . Since 1970 , 90 health-care serial killers have been convicted of murdering at least 450 patients . But the total number of suspicious deaths attributed to health-care workers is more than 2,600 , she told the inquiry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Ottawa last year , the city auditor found &amp;ldquo ; numerous deficiencies&amp;rdquo ; in the way drugs are stored and destroyed in its four long-term care homes . The audit was conducted after the city received a complaint on its fraud and waste hotline . The review found there was a risk of possible drug diversion from the moment medications are delivered to the homes , to storage , administration and the disposal of unused doses . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The report also said that hand-initialled medication records were sometimes incomplete or illegible , making it hard to track who administered drugs to patients . Bins used to store the drugs marked for destruction were not sealed on at least two occasions , said the auditor&amp;rsquo ; s report . &amp;ldquo ; There was also no water in the bins to render the drugs inactive , even though in at least one instance , the bin was filled to capacity . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The auditors also found that medication rooms did not have cameras in them . &amp;ldquo ; Given the volume of medication stored in these areas , cameras in the medication rooms and the government pharmacy could provide added security and mitigate the risk of drug diversion . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The variety and quantity of medications in long-term care make it difficult to completely prevent diversion and misuse of medications . And that was particularly true of insulin , said the inquiry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Insulin can take hours or days to induce coma or death , Crofts Yorker told the inquiry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The symptoms of hypoglycemia are non-specific and can vary from patient to patient . If detected , it can be reversed with administration of IV Dextrose , which may revive a patient who was given a toxic dose of insulin , without suspicion of wrongdoing . A killer who overdoses a patient on insulin has often been off the premises for several hours or shifts when the patient is adversely affected by the overdose , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Insulin &amp;mdash ; that&amp;rsquo ; s where the system failure happened , &amp;rdquo ; said Suman Iqbal , a registered nurse who recently retired as the senior manager of the long-term care best practices program at the Registered Nurses Association of Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Technology commonly used in hospitals is one of the things that could prevent medication from being diverted . One of the inquiry&amp;rsquo ; s recommendations was that the government establish a three-year program to fund measures , including automated drug dispensing cabinets and barcode-assisted medical administration systems , as well as installing glass doors , windows and walls or security cameras in medication rooms . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Technology is making it more difficult to divert drugs . But they are expensive in comparison to old-fashioned lock-and-key systems , said Iqbal . Nursing homes have to commit to pay for the software and computers and for training staff . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Wettlaufer inquiry also noted that long-term care nursing staff are also stretched thin . Nurses reported having to strategize and prioritize when administering medications because of the number of medications , the number of residents and the challenges of administering medications to residents who often have cognitive impairment , dementia or difficulty swallowing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;FAQ about long-term care and medications &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is a Medication Administration Record ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Known as a MAR , this is a record of all the medications prescribed to a resident . Drugs are delivered to patients in a medication cart and all patients have their own drawer in the cart . The registered nurse or registered practical nurse carries a master key , which opens all the drawers . The narcotics drawer requires two keys to open it . Each time a medication is administered , the nurse initials the MAR . The city audit found that in its review of MARs , auditors were not able to determine who administered the drugs due to the illegibility of the initials on the MAR. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is an electronic Medication Administration System ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Known as an eMAR , this uses software to replace the paper MAR system , tracking the registered staff who administer medications. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is an automated dispensing system ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sometimes known as an automated dispensing cabinet ( ADC ) , these have been commonly used in hospitals in both Canada and the U.S. for about 20 years and are increasingly used in nursing homes in the U.S. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Typically , medications are delivered to long-term care homes weekly or monthly resulting in large stocks of drugs which go to waste and make it easier to divert medication . Using ADCs allows a home to reduce both medication stocks and waste . An ADC is stocked with the 50 to 100 medications most commonly prescribed within a home . The ADC tracks all medications it contains . A nurse accesses the ADC with a swipe card , passcode , or a fingerprint scanner . Where the ADC is tied to patient profiles , the ADC also documents the resident for whom the medication has been dispensed . Improved tracking of medications makes it easier to detect discrepancies that are currently not tracked , such as insulin , the inquiry heard . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is barcode-managed medication ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A : This system uses barcode technology to track medication . There is a barcode on the medication itself , one on the medication record and one on the recipient of the medication , often on a wristband . This system has the potential to protect against intentional harm because a nurse must scan a resident&amp;rsquo ; s wristband , making the transaction traceable . However , a nurse could choose not to scan the resident&amp;rsquo ; s barcoded wristband before administrating a drug and it would not be documented . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Has the city changed medication practices and procedures in its four long-term care homes as a result of the Wettlaufer inquiry ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A : The city did not make any changes as a direct result of the Wettlaufer case . However , the city &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;will implement any mandatory recommendations directed by the Ministry of Long-Term Care , said Dean Lett , the city 's director of long-term care . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;Long-term care managers did agree with the recommendations in the city auditor general 's April 2018 report , and have &lt;/span&gt; &lt;span style= " font-family : -apple-system , BlinkMacSystemFont , ' Segoe UI ' , Roboto , Oxygen-Sans , Ubuntu , Cantarell , ' Helvetica Neue ' , sans-serif ; " &gt;made several improvements around the delivery and disposal of medication . The four homes &lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;have recently implemented an eMAR system . This was not a specific recommendation from the auditor general , but was an improvement that had already been identified , said Lett. &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The system flags when a medication is due to be given to the resident and any assessments or tests that need to be completed before administering , he said . &amp;ldquo ; When a nurse gives the medication , they sign into the system and mark the medication as being administered . The eMAR will be updated with time of administration and name of the nurse who administered , and it will not appear on the eMAR again until it is time for the next dose . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The auditor&amp;rsquo ; s report also recommended that city-run homes consider installing cameras in medication rooms . An initial analysis has been completed , and staff are exploring the feasibility of installing cameras in the recommended locations , said Lett. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What are other nursing homes doing as a result of the inquiry ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ontario Long Term Care Association , which represents almost 70 per cent of the 630 homes in the province , is currently reviewing the recommendations made by the inquiry with its membership . Markham-based Extendicare , which has over 120 care homes across North America , said all of its homes in Ontario use eMAR systems . &amp;ldquo ; We are engaged with our various pharmacy partners to collaborate on innovation within the area of medication management to promote resident safety . We do not use barcode medication administration systems ; however , we are currently piloting an automated system in one of our homes , &amp;rdquo ; said a spokeswoman . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is the province doing ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A : The Ministry of Long-Term Care , along &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;with the Attorney General , the Minister of Health and the Solicitor General will be reviewing the recommendations in the coming weeks and developing a government-wide approach , including those pertaining to staffing , said a spokeswoman . The government &lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;will submit a progress report to the Ontario Legislature on July 31 , 2020 , and will also provide new funding to help address the report 's recommendations . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS : &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;15428;TOOLONG ... trial run hits 12 days at the end of Friday . NewsSeniorsChurch and Gerlock : Wettlaufer inquiry got it right , sort ofhttp : **38;15580;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;15620;TOOLONG ... title= " Left to Right : Stan Millard , Muriel Burnley and her husband Dan Silcox listen to Commissioner Eileen Gillese speak at the release of the final report of a two-year probe into the long-term care system sparked by the murders of eight people by former nurse Elizabeth Wettlaufer . Millard is the son of Gladys Millard and Dan Silcox is the son of James Silcox , two of Wettlaufer 's victims . " alt= " Left to Right : Stan Millard , Muriel Burnley and her husband Dan Silcox listen to Commissioner Eileen Gillese speak at the release of the final report of a two-year probe into the long-term care system sparked by the murders of eight people by former nurse Elizabeth Wettlaufer . Millard is the son of Gladys Millard and Dan Silcox is the son of James Silcox , two of Wettlaufer 's victims . " data-portal-copyright= " Derek Ruttan " **28;15656;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1440413 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Last Wednesday , the much-anticipated commissioner&amp;rsquo ; s report on the Elizabeth Wettlaufer case was released . Rather than attempting to assign blame to individuals or organizations , the commissioner chose to identify a number of systemic problems that led to the circumstances under which Elizabeth Wettlaufer was able to murder eight senior citizens between 2007 and 2016 . While choosing this approach is consistent with the growing consensus among patient safety experts , the commissioner&amp;rsquo ; s&amp;nbsp ; characterization that the system can be fixed by changing a number of reporting procedures does not go far enough . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; We recently completed &lt;a href= " https : **33;15686;TOOLONG ... review&lt;/a&gt; of 18 public inquiries of unnecessary injuries and deaths in Canada and Britain in which multiple factors were identified ( leadership , culture , governance , regulation and individual behaviour ) &amp;ndash ; a &amp;ldquo ; web of causation . &amp;rdquo ; The interaction of these factors led to multiple deaths ( sometimes in the hundreds ) . So , the commissioner got it right when she identified systemic issues . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; What she failed to address was the way in which these multiple factors interact with each other . Front and centre in this web of causation is leadership and culture . As we all know , leadership and culture set the tone for how a business will be run and how employees are expected to behave . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Over the past several decades , politicians and organizational leaders in health care have become obsessed with two things : the financial bottom line and a positive public image . Inevitably , this has led to reductions in staffing levels and the substituting of more qualified with less qualified staff . It has also led to a culture in which positive news is favoured over negative news . It has led to a culture in which political and organizational leaders engage in wilful blindness , knowing about problems but failing to address them because of the short-term financial costs of doing so . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A culture of bullying and silencing has become very common . Arguably , in health care ( including long-term care ) this culture has been around for a long time . Nurses , who are on the front lines , often suffer the negative consequences of such a culture . This is well documented . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;15721;TOOLONG ... title= " Commissioner Eileen Gillese speaks at the release of the final report of the Wettlaufer Inquiry . " alt= " Commissioner Eileen Gillese speaks at the release of the final report of the Wettlaufer Inquiry . " data-portal-copyright= " Derek Ruttan " **28;15757;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1440403 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; But wait , don&amp;rsquo ; t we have professional colleges that regulate the behaviour of nurses and doctors ? Yes , we do , but in recent years it has become increasingly apparent that these colleges are failing to effectively regulate the behaviour of what are sometimes called &amp;ldquo ; rogue professionals&amp;rdquo ; &amp;ndash ; a small segment of their members who act with the intention to cause harm . A recent review of how dentists regulate themselves in British Columbia concluded that making changes here and there would not be enough and that &amp;ldquo ; a complete overhaul of the way health professional regulation is conceived and delivered is required . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Britain , the Francis Inquiry identified the failure of professional colleges to act as one of the systemic factors contributing to large numbers of deaths . Having said this , it is important to note that the commissioner dedicated &lt;a href= " https : **33;15787;TOOLONG ... 3&lt;/a&gt; of the report to &amp;ldquo ; the many nurses and other caregivers who perform their jobs in the long-term care system with great kindness and skill . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;15822;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;15864;TOOLONG ... failures in long-term care allowed nurse Elizabeth Wettlaufer 's **27;15899;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; Our research has demonstrated that getting the right balance between financial efficiency and quality and patient safety is critical . We concur with the commissioner on the Wettlaufer Inquiry that piecemeal procedural changes will not be effective unless there is a significant cultural shift to increase the emphasis placed on the quality of life of patients and ensuring their safety regardless of cost . The changes needed and recommended in the coroner&amp;rsquo ; s office highlight the deeper problem of ageism in health policy , in which the costs of caring for the elderly are viewed as potentially bankrupting the system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While introducing greater transparency and improved reporting systems are arguably necessary , they will make little difference unless more fundamental shifts such as reducing the focus on profit margins and increasing the focus on the quality of life of patients and ensuring their safety are made . The attitudes associated with ageism must go . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This detailed analysis of factors resulting in tragic and preventable deaths&amp;nbsp ; &amp;nbsp ; is only one of many inquires in Canada and around the world&amp;nbsp ; that point to &amp;ldquo ; systemic&amp;rdquo ; problems and a web of causation that is driven by ideology rather than need or evidence . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As the commissioner said , we will see another Wettlaufer down the road if there is not serious recognition that long-term care ( including home care ) is more complex and dangerous than it might appear , and that expert care&amp;nbsp ; and leadership are needed to protect the vulnerable persons who depend upon these systems . The necessary shift will require meaningful public involvement in decision-making about how the system is run , and a sustained commitment from political and organizational leaders at all levels . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **28;15928;TOOLONG Church&lt;/strong&gt; , PhD , is an associate professor , Department of Political Science , at the University of Alberta . Since the 1980s , he has conducted research on health care policymaking . He is a co-editor and contributing author for Paradigm Freeze : &lt;a href= " https : **33;15958;TOOLONG ... it is so hard to reform health care in Canada&lt;/a&gt; . McGill-Queen 's University Press . 2013. &lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **27;15993;TOOLONG Gerlock&lt;/strong&gt; has more than 25 years ' experience working in the private , postsecondary and public sector and in her own consulting practice . She has evaluated health , education and capacity building , and strategic planning initiatives at the federal , provincial and international levels . &lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;16022;TOOLONG ... the Ch ? teau Laurier be nationalized ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;16057;TOOLONG ... donated his mother 's brain for Alzheimer 's research . The conversations , sprinkled with laughter , are as important as the testing to determine what people need to remain independent and as healthy as possible , she says . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is important to build a relationship first in order to understand what kind of support clients need , says Rose . Visiting patients in their homes makes that easier . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; A lot of time people get to doctor&amp;rsquo ; s appointments and get this white coat syndrome , they forget what is bothering them . When I get there on scene in their home , they are a little more relaxed and they actually talk about what is bothering them . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rose takes that information back to their family doctor . She is working under a provincial program called Health Links in which paramedics make routine home visits to high-needs patients , in close communications with their family doctor and other health professionals . Electronic health records allow test results and notes from paramedics to be uploaded to doctors offices . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;16379;TOOLONG ... title= " Stephanie Rose , a community care paramedic , looks after Maria Makkos , 82 , during their initial consultation at Maria 's home in Arnrprior , July 10 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Jean Levac " **28;16415;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1432713 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Renfrew County paramedics like Rose visit about 150 patients as part of the Health Links program , which is aimed at providing better coordinated care to some of the highest needs patients in the region . An additional 1,500 patients , who are not part of Health Links but also have high needs or difficulty getting to health appointments , also receive regular visits from Renfrew County community paramedics. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Community paramedics have become a key link in the chain that is the health system in Renfrew . They are helping to keep people out of hospital , reduce 911 calls and improve clients&amp;rsquo ; quality of life &amp;mdash ; and their families . Their work supports family physicians , community nurses and other health professionals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You become a family , &amp;rdquo ; says Rosemary Drziak . Her husband Ed Pinnell , an aerospace engineer with the National Research Council , is now being treated for malignant brain cancer . He is Rose&amp;rsquo ; s first client of the day . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The couple&amp;rsquo ; s Arnprior home has recently been fitted with a ramp for Pinnell , who uses a wheelchair since falling and breaking his hip . Seizures and complications from brain cancer put him at high risk of falling , something Drziak worries about even if she takes her eyes off him to take a shower . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rose sits close to Pinnell&amp;rsquo ; s wheelchair and asks him about seizures . He hasn&amp;rsquo ; t had any since she last saw him . &amp;ldquo ; That is one thing you were worried about , that they were becoming more frequent , &amp;rdquo ; she says to Pinnell , as she prepares to draw blood . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She asks about headaches , checks his blood pressure and blood sugar levels and talks to Pinnell and Drziak before heading off . Sweets are OK , she tells Pinnell with a smile , noting his blood sugar levels are not too high . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Community paramedics make regular home visits , but can also be reached 24 hours a day if there is a need . The program offers a crucial safety net for the couple as they deal with Pinnell&amp;rsquo ; s health challenges . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We can not say enough , &amp;rdquo ; says Drziak. &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t know what we would do without them . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rose says community paramedics become like family members to some of their clients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They know they are not alone . We are that family that can speak for them , on their behalf . Their advocate . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;16445;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;16481;TOOLONG ... future of health care has arrived -- but is the Ontario government paying attention ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;16516;TOOLONG ... cottages , tall trees and a barn : Canada builds first-ever village in B.C to house residents with dementia&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;16551;TOOLONG ... ticks are so common in parts of Canada , testing no longer done&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneThu , 25 Jul 2019 12:03:02 **45;16586;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal **25;16633;TOOLONG among some Ottawa Hospital doctors low amid ' chaos ' of new records system rollouthttp : **33;16660;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;16695;TOOLONG ... title= " A file photo of part of the General campus of The Ottawa Hospital .
