Barrett lives with young onset dementia . The Ottawa couple want to reframe the conversation around this disease . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;21717;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1330083 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; That&amp;rsquo ; s what we want people to understand . The moment you get diagnosed doesn&amp;rsquo ; t mean you&amp;rsquo ; re the person who needs a lot of care . The moment you get diagnosed is the moment you say , &amp;lsquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; m going to work really hard to keep everything that I have , all my skills , my abilities , for as long as I can . ' &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The campaign &amp;mdash ; &amp;ldquo ; Yes . I live with dementia . Let me help you understand . &amp;rdquo ; &amp;mdash ; aims to change attitudes the Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s Society uncovered with recent research that showed that one in four Canadians said they&amp;rsquo ; d feel ashamed or embarrassed if they had dementia . One in five admitted using derogatory or stigmatizing language about dementia . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That leads to real harm to patients and their families , said Alzheimer Society of Canada chief executive Pauline Tardif , who points to everything from jokes that forgetfulness must be dementia to the assumption that a diagnosis means the end of a meaningful life . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It might make people afraid to seek a reason for their own or a loved one&amp;rsquo ; s symptoms like memory loss , changes in judgment and reasoning or mood and behaviour and difficulty performing familiar tasks or expressing themselves . So they don&amp;rsquo ; t get the support they need and treatment . While&amp;nbsp ; Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s disease is eventually fatal , some forms of dementia can be cured . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;21747;TOOLONG ... title= " Keith Barrett lives with young onset dementia . He was diagnosed at 57. " alt= " Keith Barrett lives with young onset dementia . He was diagnosed at 57. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;21783;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1330085 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Once diagnosed , people with dementia may face impatience when it takes them a beat longer to order a coffee or remember their PIN &amp;mdash ; Barrett was yelled at during an airport security check &amp;mdash ; leading them to shy away from tasks they can still perform and activities they enjoy . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Or stigma &amp;mdash ; &amp;ldquo ; Why visit when she might not remember my kids&amp;rsquo ; names or even recognize me ? &amp;rdquo ; &amp;mdash ; can keep friends away , leaving people with dementia socially isolated , which is itself an important contributor to cognitive decline . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; People with dementia sit on the society&amp;rsquo ; s board of directors and have important roles in educating policy-makers , as Barrett did when he and Meyers attended a conference hosted by the federal health minister last spring to guide work on a new national dementia strategy . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; People in our communities need to be better educated and more patient and understanding to enable them to live their full lives and not disable them prematurely , &amp;rdquo ; Tardif said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The campaign is also needed to reveal the often-invisible scale of a &amp;ldquo ; crisis&amp;rdquo ; in health care , said Tardif , who co-chairs the health minister&amp;rsquo ; s advisory board on dementia after the federal government committed to developing the strategy the society had long advocated . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While 564,000 Canadians live with some form of dementia , that number is expected to rise to 937,000 by 2031. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That&amp;rsquo ; s mostly because our society is aging and we&amp;rsquo ; re living longer . While dementia isn&amp;rsquo ; t a normal part of growing old , age is the No. 1 risk factor for developing dementia . The risk doubles every five years after age 65. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The prevalence of dementia is growing exponentially and our care partners across Canada are ill-resourced and that is causing a lot of issues in the care of people living with dementia , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; The Alzheimer Society is only one of those players . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We try to do our bests with our donors&amp;rsquo ; support but we don&amp;rsquo ; t provide health care , so in working with our provincial partners and hopefully now with the federal government with the national dementia strategy that is being created we can increase the care and support available to people living with dementia . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Barrett&amp;rsquo ; s symptoms first appeared about four-and-a-half years ago . After decades of speaking effortlessly off-the-cuff for work , he started forgetting what he&amp;rsquo ; d already said . He was misplacing things and using the wrong word , say &amp;ldquo ; fridge&amp;rdquo ; instead of &amp;ldquo ; stove . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meyers recalls that Barrett would be angry that people had told him that they had had a conversation with him that he couldn&amp;rsquo ; t remember but he&amp;nbsp ; was reluctant to bring it up with doctors and they dismissed the changes as stress or aging . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I knew him really well , &amp;rdquo ; Meyers said . &amp;ldquo ; I could see the changes more than anyone else could . I couldn&amp;rsquo ; t continue to watch him be as frustrated as he was . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meyers kept bringing it up . Finally , Barrett was referred to a neuropsychologist who performed cognitive tests as doctors did other blood work and an MRI to exclude other causes of memory loss . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In December of 2016 , he was diagnosed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The couple started attending Alzheimer Society education sessions and sharing the news with their family but Barrett admits that it didn&amp;rsquo ; t seem real . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I still didn&amp;rsquo ; t believe it , &amp;rdquo ; Barrett said . &amp;ldquo ; I&amp;rsquo ; m 57 at that point . It couldn&amp;rsquo ; t be right . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The message he heard was to &amp;ldquo ; go home and get your affairs in order , get your will in order . There was no positive encouragement that you can live with this . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meyers , too , was left with the impression that Barrett was being advised to wind down his life and retire . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I think we need to do the exact opposite , &amp;rdquo ; thought Meyers , a director in a health care organization who works with seniors , many of them with dementia . &amp;ldquo ; Let&amp;rsquo ; s be positive . Let&amp;rsquo ; s live the best life we can . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Two years after that diagnosis , Barrett is still heading to the office every day with the help of a colleague who supervises some of his work . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He says the key to living well with dementia is constant learning : he&amp;rsquo ; s systematized his routines , starting with morning grooming and relies on technology , including an iWatch , the virtual assistant Alexa at home and GPS when driving . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;21813;TOOLONG ... title= " Keith Barrett . " alt= " Keith Barrett . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;21849;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1330279 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; He takes notes , asks colleagues to follow up work conversations with a short email summarizing what was discussed and Meyers , instead of telling her husband of their plans for Thursday night , for example , sends him a digital invite so it will be on his calendar . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; As articulate as Keith is when he&amp;rsquo ; s talking to you , it&amp;rsquo ; s really important to understand that he&amp;rsquo ; s just affected in a way that maybe isn&amp;rsquo ; t something you can totally see every day , &amp;rdquo ; Meyers said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Barrett describes losing confidence , like in his former ability to strike up a conversation with anyone , and struggling with anxiety and impatience . The noise and distraction of big social events can be overwhelming , and walks with the dog have largely replaced the movies they used to enjoy . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Why pay for something you forget the next day ? &amp;rdquo ; Barrett quips , but he&amp;rsquo ; s sombre as he says that he tries not to think of his grandchildren and how he will be as they grow . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He&amp;rsquo ; s become a passionate advocate for more services for the estimated 16,000 Canadians under 65 with young onset dementia and for research to find definitive tests for Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s disease and effective treatment in the future . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;21879;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;21921;TOOLONG ... who exercise into their 50s are five times less likely to develop dementia , according to new **29;21956;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;21987;TOOLONG ... whack-a-mole has taught researchers about **29;22022;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;22053;TOOLONG ... stages of Alzheimer 's and **29;22088;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s progressively destroys brain cells . From mild impairment , it takes a path through a middle stage , when patients will eventually need help with basic activities like bathing , through a late stage when they eventually become unable to speak and need 24-hour care and finally to the end of life . How long each stage lasts is different for everyone , according to the society . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Barrett is on medication that may be able to improve his symptoms for several years but at a lower dose after difficult side effects . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; At least it gives you some hope , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; What Meyers hears is pity when she shares that her husband lives with Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s and she&amp;rsquo ; s candid that it will likely take him from her too soon . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On&amp;nbsp ; her wrist is a tattoo she hopes will raise awareness of dementia and start a conversation that helps spark change . It&amp;rsquo ; s Dory , the forgetful fish from Finding Nemo and her now-unforgettable motto : &amp;ldquo ; Just keep swimming . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We want people to think about us in a positive way , &amp;rdquo ; Meyers said . &amp;ldquo ; Still typically the answer is , &amp;lsquo ; Oh , I&amp;rsquo ; m very sorry . &amp;rsquo ; &amp;nbsp ; I&amp;rsquo ; m not saying that I&amp;rsquo ; m not . We don&amp;rsquo ; t want this , but it&amp;rsquo ; s here so let&amp;rsquo ; s face it in the best way that we can . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For more on the campaign , visit&amp;nbsp ; ilivewithdementia.ca. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;22119;TOOLONG ... He then recovers and leaves hospital&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Megan GillisMon , 07 Jan 2019 18:30:01 **45;22224;TOOLONG StoryLocal **35;22271;TOOLONG care homes want end to mandatory inspectionshttp : **29;22308;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;22339;TOOLONG ... title= " Garry J Armstrong long-term care on Island Lodge Road in Ottawa . " alt= " Garry J Armstrong long-term care on Island Lodge Road in Ottawa . " data-portal-copyright= " Jean Levac " **28;22375;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1050259 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s long-term care homes are pressing the Ontario government to get rid of mandatory annual inspections as part of its focus on cutting red tape . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Groups that represent the province&amp;rsquo ; s long-term care homes have long asked for an end to annual inspections for all long-term care homes and reducing regulations that they say place an undue burden on an already stressed sector . The head of one of two major organizations says she is optimistic the province is listening to those concerns . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is a possibility that worries Jane Meadus , staff lawyer and the institutional advocate with the Advocacy Centre for the Elderly . Regulations covering long-term care homes are there for a reason &amp;mdash ; in some cases because of previous harm done to residents &amp;mdash ; said Meadus. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Inspecting long term care homes that provide care to vulnerable seniors is not red tape . It is something that is necessary . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Premier Doug Ford&amp;rsquo ; s Progressive Conservative government has already made some changes to the Long Term Care Homes Act as part of omnibus red tape legislation introduced in December , largely aimed at reducing the need for public consultation and making it easier to open temporary emergency beds. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Lisa Levin , chief executive of AdvantAge Ontario , which represents not-for-profit long-term care homes , said she is optimistic there will be changes to the inspection process in the upcoming budget . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Under the previous Liberal government , a new risk-based inspection system was introduced , meaning long-term care homes deemed higher risk are given more thorough annual inspections , although all homes are inspected every year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We have been hearing a lot of rumours that there is going to be fewer regulations and ( the provincial government ) is re-examining the situation . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Hayley Chazan , spokesperson for Health Minister Christine Elliott , said the province does not have any plans to reduce inspections in long-term care homes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re broadly consulting with partners in health care as part of our efforts to develop a long-term transformational health strategy that Ontarians need and deserve . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Both AdvantAge Ontario and the Ontario Long Term Care Association have asked the province to cut regulations faced by long-term care homes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Over the past 15 years , there has been an exponential increase in the administrative burden assumed by the long-term care sector , &amp;rdquo ; wrote Candace Chartier , CEO of the Ontario Long Term Care Association , to Giles Gherson , deputy minister of red tape and regulatory burden reduction . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22405;TOOLONG ... title= " Candace Chartier , CEO of Ontario Long Term Care Association . " alt= " Candace Chartier , CEO of Ontario Long Term Care Association . " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;22441;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 225922 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In long-term care , 95 per cent of the administrative burden arises from meeting legislated obligations directly related to superfluous care planning documentation and responding to inspection requirements , both of which divert staff time and resources from the provision of direct care . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Long-term care , says Levin , is the most over-regulated sector in Ontario with 600 regulations . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It is not helping care . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus , whose organization does advocacy work for long-term care patients and their families , said mandatory annual inspections might result in more work for long-term care staff , but that is part of the trade-off to ensure vulnerable residents are protected . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22471;TOOLONG ... title= " Jane Meadus , staff lawyer and the institutional advocate with the Advocacy Centre for the Elderly . " alt= " Jane Meadus , staff lawyer and the institutional advocate with the Advocacy Centre for the Elderly . " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;22507;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1329082 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Our real fear is that things are going to be missed . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Inspectors at homes in Ottawa and around the province have found evidence during inspections in recent years of neglect , abuse , mishandling of medications and failure to report incidents . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Long-term care organizations argue that many homes are well run and do not require annual inspections , and the burden of those inspections is , in fact , preventing staff from providing quality direct care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus noted that many of the regulations &amp;mdash ; including how long-term care homes manage medications , are things that patients and their families do not see and would not be able to make a complaint about in a complaints-based inspection system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus said she is concerned that the level of care and management of homes would change if they knew they were not going to be inspected . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is something we would be really concerned about . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Long-term care organizations say the current system is not effective . They want to see a more &amp;ldquo ; coaching for compliance&amp;rdquo ; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; A reduced regulatory burden on well-performing homes , more coaching and training supports for staff and administrators and sharing of best practices will result in stronger protections for seniors and better use of health resources , &amp;rdquo ; AdvantAge Ontario wrote in its 2019 provincial budget submission . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;22537;TOOLONG " **34;22566;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;22602;TOOLONG ... NewsSeniorsNew treatment could prevent deadly blood clots in cancer patientshttp : **32;22754;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;22788;TOOLONG ... title= " Cancer patient Harold Black , centre , developed a blog clot in late August in his lung that was luckily caught by his doctors . The Ottawa Hospital 's chief of medicine , Dr. Phil Wells , left , and Dr. Marc Carrier , a hematologist and senior scientist , have led a Canada-wide clinical trial on the subject and have developed a preventative treatment . " alt= " Cancer patient Harold Black , centre , developed a blog clot in late August in his lung that was luckily caught by his doctors . The Ottawa Hospital 's chief of medicine , Dr. Phil Wells , left , and Dr. Marc Carrier , a hematologist and senior scientist , have led a Canada-wide clinical trial on the subject and have developed a preventative treatment . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;22824;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1318525 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Harold Black gets lots of practice singing &amp;mdash ; he is in two choirs . So when he stood up to sing with his choir at Dominion-Chalmers Church last August and couldn&amp;rsquo ; t catch his breath , he knew something was wrong . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The 76-year-old Nepean man had recently been through surgery for bowel cancer and had been recovering well when the breathing problems began . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Black ended up back in the hospital , where he was diagnosed with a blood clot in his lungs &amp;mdash ; a pulmonary embolism . He was treated in hospital for two days and now has to inject himself in the stomach daily with an anticoagulant to insure there is no recurrence . It is something he may have to do for the rest of his life . Still , Black knows it could have been much worse . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When I look back , I know I was lucky I survived , &amp;rdquo ; he says . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22854;TOOLONG ... title= " Cancer patient Harold Black " alt= " Cancer patient Harold Black " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;22890;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1318527 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Blood clots are a common and sometimes deadly complication from cancer . In fact , they are the second leading cause of death for cancer patients after cancer itself . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Those who survive can be affected for life . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But that could change , thanks to research from two Ottawa researchers that offers a treatment to prevent blood clots in a high proportion of patients who are newly diagnosed with a solid cancer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Phil Wells , a senior scientist and chief of medicine at The Ottawa Hospital and The University of Ottawa , and Dr. Marc Carrier , a senior scientist and associate professor at The Ottawa Hospital , have published the results of their clinical trial in the New England Journal of Medicine . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;22920;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;22962;TOOLONG ... lab takes a shot at producing cancer-killing treatment dubbed the ' rarest drug on Earth ' **36;22997;TOOLONG href= " https : **33;23035;TOOLONG ... of Canadian breast cancer patients will reap the rewards of a new no-chemo study , experts **39;23070;TOOLONG href= " https : **33;23111;TOOLONG ... Exercise might help breast-cancer survivors avoid **42;23146;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; Part of their research was identifying the cancer patients who are face the highest risk of developing clots , using a scoring system based on blood tests and other factors . They found that about half of the people starting cancer chemotherapy were in the high-risk group and would benefit from preventative therapy . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The clinical trial involved 563 patients at 13 centres across Canada who had been identified as high risk for developing blood clots . Half were given a placebo and the other half were given a twice daily oral doses of the anticoagulant drug apixaban. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Just over 10 per cent of patients who took the placebo developed blood clots within six months , compared to 4.2 per cent of patients who were given apixaban . The patients in the placebo group were slightly less likely to suffer from a major bleed than the patients who were given the drug ( one per cent compared to 2.1 per cent ) , but the bleeds were all treatable. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23190;TOOLONG ... title= " Ottawa Hospital 's Chief of Medicine , Dr. Phil Wells , left , and Dr. Marc Carrier , a hematologist and senior scientist . " alt= " Ottawa Hospital 's Chief of Medicine , Dr. Phil Wells , left , and Dr. Marc Carrier , a hematologist and senior scientist . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;23226;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1318528 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The strategy could prevent blood clots in as many as 57,000 people in the Canada and the United States who are at high risk of developing clots after being diagnosed with cancer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Now the challenge will be to see the strategy become common practice , say the researchers , something that could save lives and money . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Black said he can live with the daily injections if they prevent another serious blood clot , but he is grateful to researchers for work that will protect others like him . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;LIFE AFTER CANCER : &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;23256;TOOLONG ... survivors are dealing with the emotional toll of the disease&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I feel very lucky because I was told that the first sign of a pulmonary embolism is often death , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; If this research prevents people like me from developing blood clots , that will make a big difference for a lot of people . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The blood clot program at The Ottawa Hospital and the University of Ottawa is the largest and most research-intensive in the world . The study was sponsored by the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;23291;TOOLONG " **34;23320;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;23356;TOOLONG ... passes on altered behaviour to next three generations in fish , researchers find&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;23391;TOOLONG ... censorship ' : Trump administration halts use of fetal tissue , shutting down study to find cure for HIV&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;23426;TOOLONG ... might the drugs we take affect our children or grandchildren ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneTue , 11 Dec 2018 12:55:48 **45;23461;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsMenSeniorsWomenThe other side of cancer : How survivors are dealing with the emotional toll of the diseasehttp : **33;23508;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;23543;TOOLONG ... title= " This is a cancer survivors boot camp or a fitness camp for people who loved ones have died of cancer . " alt= " This is a cancer survivors boot camp or a fitness camp for people who loved ones have died of cancer . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;23579;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1317144 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;23609;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; At times , Donna Jakowec&amp;rsquo ; s cancer journey felt more like a frantic struggle to keep her head above water . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I felt safe when I talked to the doctors , but between appointments I was having terrible feelings of panic . The doctors represented these islands . When I was finished with one doctor , I was forced to swim in the ocean to the next island , and I felt like I was drowning the whole way . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A federal public servant and married mother of two teenagers , Jakowec was struggling , as thousands of Canadians do every year , with the other side of &lt;a href= " https : **30;23645;TOOLONG " &g; ... . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; After the initial shock and treatment is over , patients are left coping with the psychological aftermath &amp;mdash ; often with little or no support . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The psychological and social &amp;mdash ; or psychosocial &amp;mdash ; aspects of a &lt;a href= " https : **33;23677;TOOLONG ... diagnosis&lt;/a&gt; get less attention than the physical symptoms , but can be equally , and sometimes even more , debilitating . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23712;TOOLONG ... title= " Mary Jo Lewis runs a cancer survivors boot camp in Ottawa . " alt= " Mary Jo Lewis runs a cancer survivors boot camp in Ottawa . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;23748;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1317142 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Psychosocial symptoms have been called the sixth vital sign of cancer , after body temperature , blood pressure , pulse , respiration and pain . And , although it is increasingly recognized , gaps remain in treatment and support for the emotional and psychological impact of cancer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Ottawa , at the Ottawa Regional Cancer Centre , and elsewhere , it is now routine to screen cancer patients for psychosocial symptoms , such as anxiety and depression while they are undergoing treatment . Patients , including Jakowec , are offered counselling , &lt;a href= " https : **33;23778;TOOLONG ... groups&lt;/a&gt; and other services in addition to chemotherapy , radiation and surgery , if needed . But the services are not consistent across the province . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For many , the full emotional impact of a cancer diagnosis doesn&amp;rsquo ; t hit them until they finish treatment and start to process what they have been through . For those patients , who can struggle for months and years with the mental-health after-effects , there is often limited or inconsistent support . Some struggle alone ; believing what they are experiencing is normal . Some develop post-traumatic stress disorder , known as PTSD . Others pay for psychological treatment to cope . Others find support from those who have been through the same thing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23813;TOOLONG ... title= " Donna Jakowec has survived two different forms of cancer . " alt= " Donna Jakowec has survived two different forms of cancer . " data-portal-copyright= " Errol McGihon " **28;23849;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1314800 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jakowec was 54 when she was diagnosed with breast cancer in 2016 . That was the same age her mother had been when she learned she had breast cancer . Jakowec&amp;rsquo ; s sister also had breast cancer ; she was in her late 40s when she was diagnosed with an aggressive form of breast cancer . She died three years later . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The news of her own cancer hit Jakowec like a lightning bolt , especially given her family history . She says she would sometimes find herself frozen , staring at a wall , wondering : &amp;ldquo ; What is going to happen to me ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A runner and avid cyclist , she returned to running as soon as she could after surgery and radiation , in part , to help deal with the anxiety and sense of panic . &amp;ldquo ; It sort of took the edge off , but it didn&amp;rsquo ; t take it away . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As she recovered from the active phase of &lt;a href= " https : **33;23879;TOOLONG ... treatment&lt;/a&gt; , surgery and radiation , her family and work , driving her two children to sports and music lessons , filled her days and kept her busy . But nights were given over to worry and fear , especially that cancer would return . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That lingering anxiety turned to shock when her fear was realized and Jakowec was diagnosed with a second , unrelated cancer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23914;TOOLONG ... title= " " alt= " " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;23950;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; She had begun experiencing radiating numbness that started under her rib when she ran . Eventually , she had to stop as the pain and discomfort worsened . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Around the same time , because she had finished treatment , she was transferred from active &lt;a href= " https : **33;23980;TOOLONG ... care&lt;/a&gt; to a program called Wellness Beyond Cancer . During her first meeting , the nurse practitioner she was assigned to asked whether anything was bothering her . Jakowec described her symptoms and was sent for a CT scan , which revealed the problem : a gastrointestinal stromal tumour inside her stomach , unrelated to her breast cancer , news that left her reeling . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In June , the tumour was successfully removed through laparoscopic surgery , which left her with just two small scars . No other treatment was required and the prognosis is excellent . The chances of it recurring , Jakowec was told , are less than three per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But there were other lasting effects . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Having a second cancer diagnosis in two years was devastating for Jakowec . Her anxiety and fear returned with a vengeance . She thought she was going to die . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I am actually confident it is not coming back , &amp;rdquo ; she says of the stomach tumour . But the experience has heightened her fear that her breast cancer will return . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Because I experienced a second cancer , I am much more worried I could have a recurrence ( of breast cancer ) . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;24015;TOOLONG ... title= " Donna Jakowec. " alt= " Donna Jakowec. " data-portal-copyright= " Errol McGihon " **28;24051;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1314801 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her fear of recurrence was , no doubt , heightened by her own experience and her family history , but Jackowec is far from alone . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She was referred to a psychologist working with The Ottawa Hospital&amp;rsquo ; s cancer program for help coping with her fears and anxiety , which she says is helping . She also volunteered to take part in a research study looking at fear of recurrence among cancer patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And she continues to rely on exercise and fitness to reduce her anxiety and help her cope . In September , just a few months after her surgery for a stomach tumour , Jakowec completed The Ottawa Hospital&amp;rsquo ; s 117-kilometre cycling fundraiser for cancer research . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Still , she believes the psychological impact of cancer may last for years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;24081;TOOLONG ... title= " " alt= " " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;24117;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fear of the disease returning is the elephant in the room for many cancer patients , especially as they try to return to their normal routines after intensive treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It sort of hovers around all discussions of how we are mutually doing , &amp;rdquo ; says Andrea Douglas , an Ottawa breast cancer survivor who volunteers to help other survivors . She helps with peer support for cancer patients , began a support group for survivors , takes part in a boot camp for cancer survivors and has organized a fundraising masters swim meet to raise money for the Ottawa Breast Health Centre . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She and other cancer survivors say they found themselves drawn to others who had been through similar experiences for support after being diagnosed . They exercise together , laugh together and learn from each other . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Even among those supportive groups , Douglas acknowledges , the deep fear of cancer returning is seldom discussed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I think a lot of people find it difficult to talk about . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;24147;TOOLONG ... title= " Cancer survivors boot camp in Ottawa . Cancer survivor Andrea Douglas works out . " alt= " Cancer survivors boot camp in Ottawa . Cancer survivor Andrea Douglas works out . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;24183;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1314837 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ottawa researchers Dr. Christina Tomei , a clinical psychologist in supervised practice who works in the faculty of medicine at the University of Ottawa , and Dr. Cheryl Harris , a clinical psychologist , professor and researcher who works with cancer patients in Ottawa , are hoping to help cancer survivors better cope with that fear . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Because there are so few published therapies to help treat patients who are gripped with fear that their cancer will recur , the researchers and others wanted to better understand the phenomenon and how to treat it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They conducted a small trial in which randomly assigned patients took part in six weeks of specialized therapy that helped participants address their fears head on . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some of the patients&amp;rsquo ; fears and thoughts could be &amp;ldquo ; fairly catastrophic , &amp;rdquo ; said Tomei . The goal of the therapy was to get them to look at their fears in another way . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It is not about positive thinking , &amp;rdquo ; added Harris , &amp;ldquo ; but what is the reality of my situation instead of going to worst-case scenarios ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **33;24213;TOOLONG : &amp;nbsp ; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24248;TOOLONG ... 37 , Shelly Abad Cruz discovered she was pregnant -- and had cancer&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Harris describes the therapy as giving patients a &amp;ldquo ; toolkit&amp;rdquo ; of alternative approaches from which patients can choose which works best . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There have been patients who have said &amp;lsquo ; I used to just panic to the point of not being able to think clearly and now I am able to redirect my anxiety and think it through . ' &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As a result of the pilot study , patients found they were better able to tolerate the unknown , she said . The research will expand and continue . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Understanding the impact of cancer on a whole person is something that is getting increasing attention , Harris said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Cancer is challenging , not only physically , but emotionally . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It puts stress on a patient&amp;rsquo ; s family , can add significant financial burden and even a spiritual burden . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There is definitely a trend toward this whole-person kind of approach , &amp;rdquo ; which recognizes issues beyond physical , said Harris . &amp;ldquo ; We know that a significant number of patients will come through their cancer experience without needing the help of a psychosocial program , but there are a number who will be in severe distress . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;24283;TOOLONG ... title= " A cancer survivors boot camp in Ottawa . " alt= " A cancer survivors boot camp in Ottawa . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;24319;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1314841 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; While there is a growing focus on the importance of psychological and other psychosocial support for cancer patients , gaps continue to exist &amp;mdash ; especially for patients who have finished treatment , but also for those still receiving treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a report released earlier this year , Cancer Care Ontario , the agency responsible for improving cancer services in the province , underscored the importance of complete , well-funded psychosocial oncology services . The report also raised flags that the services are inconsistent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Through better co-ordination , including the use of telemedicine in remote and rural parts of the province , all patients should have timely access to psychosocial interventions , Cancer Care Ontario recommended . That includes access to psychiatrists , social workers and psychologists as well as spiritual-care providers . It also includes access to rehab and physical therapy services for those experiencing physical functioning issues . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One patient adviser told Cancer Care Ontario that she struggled with emotional symptoms after surgery but it was only because her mother saw a flyer about connecting with a social worker that she received help . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Screening for psychosocial oncology issues should be treated like any other diagnostic tool , like a CT scan or blood work , with followup and referral for services . It can&amp;rsquo ; t just be left to chance . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;h3 style= " text-align:center ; " &gt;&lt;strong&gt;C ... and fear , by the **33;24349;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; Recent research from the Canadian Partnership Against Cancer found that while patients might be receiving excellent cancer treatment , many experience significant emotional and physical side effects that are not being adequately treated : &lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul&gt; &lt;li&gt; **32;24384;TOOLONG -- Patients who said they were not satisfied with the emotional support they received during outpatient cancer care&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt; **32;24418;TOOLONG -- Patients who reported having emotional challenges after their treatment ended . Among their biggest concerns was worry about cancer returning&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt; **32;24452;TOOLONG -- Cancer survivors who reported having at least one ongoing emotional challenge one to three years after completing treatment&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt; **34;24486;TOOLONG -- Female survivors who reported experiencing anxiety , stress and worry about cancer returning , compared with 60.7 per cent of male survivors&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt; **34;24522;TOOLONG -- Patients who reported high rates of anxiety&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; But for patients who have finished treatment , there are often few options . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The emotional after-effects of cancer are widespread and , in many cases , patients are not receiving the care they need , especially after they are finished active treatment , according to a report from the Canadian Partnership Against Cancer released in January . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; According to the study called Living with Cancer : A Report on the Patient Experience , seven in 10 patients reported having emotional challenges after treatment ended : &amp;ldquo ; Worry about cancer returning , depression and changes in sexual intimacy were their biggest concerns . &amp;rdquo ; Worry about cancer returning , according to the research , was the most difficult concern for which to get help . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;RECENT NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; : &amp;nbsp ; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24630;TOOLONG ... powder products may be harmful to lungs , possible cause of ovarian cancer : Feds&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; According to the research , more than one in five cancer survivors were told their emotional challenges were normal &amp;ldquo ; and thought nothing could be done about them . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fewer than one-third of cancer survivors sought help from a health-care provider for their emotional concerns . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Cancer survivors often have difficulty identifying and expressing emotional concerns , &amp;rdquo ; said the report . &amp;ldquo ; They also have a tough time seeking help , especially when they lack a clear understanding of the range of services and supports available . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Of those who sought help for emotional concerns , 60 per cent waited more than six months for help , according to the study . As many as 30 per cent of survivors surveyed said they relied on family and friends to help them through their emotional challenges or dealt with their concerns alone . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some , such as Sandra Dudych , a 59-year-old patient adviser with the Canadian Partnership Against Cancer , never really recover emotionally and have difficulty getting the right kind of help . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dudych , from Winnipeg , now suffers from PTSD after being diagnosed with and treated for aggressive breast cancer when she was 51 . Research has found that as many as 20 per cent of cancer survivors will develop PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;24665;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;24707;TOOLONG ... lab takes a shot at producing cancer-killing treatment dubbed the ' rarest drug on Earth ' **36;24742;TOOLONG href= " https : **33;24780;TOOLONG ... of Canadian breast cancer patients will reap the rewards of a new no-chemo study , experts **39;24815;TOOLONG href= " https : **33;24856;TOOLONG ... Exercise might help breast-cancer survivors avoid **42;24891;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; Her treatment was fraught with complications , including infections that required additional hospitalizations . She also suffers from chronic pain related to nerve damage from surgery and ongoing cognitive difficulties related to chemo , which can sometimes affect memory and executive brain function , often temporarily . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dudych said she had never suffered from depression or anxiety prior to cancer . Now , she struggles with PTSD , something she says is related to the cumulative effect of diagnosis , treatment , complications and lingering effects of cancer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dudych received psychosocial services , including from a social worker , when she was in active treatment . But she was not diagnosed with PTSD until six years later , because of the cost and limited insurance ; she is not receiving any psychological support . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Survivors are largely left to deal with their emotional aftermath of cancer with their primary health-care providers , she said , and if they have financial means , private practice therapists . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The medical professionals rally around you when you are in active treatment and then you are discharged and the ability to access resources is not as easy , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You just realize what hell it is to get through cancer , to survive treatment . It is not easy while you are in it and it is not easy after . For me , the longer-term effects are not going to go away . It is part of my new reality . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;24935;TOOLONG ... title= " This is a cancer survivors boot camp or a fitness camp for people who loved ones have died of cancer . " alt= " This is a cancer survivors boot camp or a fitness camp for people who loved ones have died of cancer . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;24971;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1317144 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; A group of breast cancer survivors who gathered in an Ottawa living room recently share a bond that has kept many of them volunteering , working out and laughing together for years . The mutual understanding helps their emotional health and coping skills , they said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When I went into ( cancer treatment ) , I thought I had all these incredible coping skills . I felt I was really mentally strong . I was running marathons . I knew how to get myself across the finish line feeling like absolute crap , &amp;rdquo ; said one woman . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; And I remember distinctly coming out of the anesthetic after my mastectomy and thinking &amp;lsquo ; It is all gone . I have nothing left mentally . &amp;rsquo ; It was almost a panic . It&amp;rsquo ; s hard enough physically , how do you get it back mentally ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said connecting with other women who had breast cancer for fitness &amp;ldquo ; was huge . I can exercise with these women who just get me. &amp;rdquo ; For many of the women , the mutual support and advice is as important as the exercise . Others belong to support groups , do peer counselling , volunteer and socialize with others who have also had breast cancer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **36;25001;TOOLONG : &lt;a href= " https : **33;25039;TOOLONG ... Paul Dewar is living and dying with love and community&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But mutual support is not always enough and some cancer survivors are on their own to find psychological support when they need it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; After active treatment , that is when I hit the wall and I stopped dead and thought &amp;lsquo ; What just happened here ? ' &amp;rdquo ; said one woman . She found little support through psychosocial services ( and was told the professional available through the cancer program was busy working with palliative cases. ) &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The woman , who is self-employed and had to take time off work during cancer treatment , has to pay for a therapist and struggled to &amp;ldquo ; find somebody who deals with this kind of thing . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Are there gaps in psychosocial supports ? Yes , she says . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When Donna Jakowec agreed to take part in the 117-kilometre cycle for cancer research in September while recovering from cancer surgery , she was asked to join a group that called themselves cancer survivors . Jakowec agreed to join the fundraising team &amp;mdash ; but only if they changed their name . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I said &amp;lsquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t feel like a survivor right now . ' &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They became the &amp;ldquo ; cancer crusaders&amp;rdquo ; instead . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jakowec said she looks forward to a time when cancer doesn&amp;rsquo ; t consume her thoughts , but knows that could be awhile . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I want this to fade so it doesn&amp;rsquo ; t define me and right now it consumes me . As soon as I wake up , I think about my cancer and when I go to bed I think about my cancer . I think it is going to be years before I stop thinking about my cancer . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;25074;TOOLONG " **34;25103;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;YOU MIGHT ALSO BE INTERESTED IN ... &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;25139;TOOLONG ... to live longer ? ' : Canada and the world have work to do before next pandemic , say experts&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneFri , 07 Dec 2018 16:55:19 **45;25244;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLife StoryLocal NewsMenSeniorsWomen'Are we ready ? ' : Canada and the world have work to do before next pandemic , say expertshttp : **33;25291;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;25326;TOOLONG ... title= " Flu vaccinations. " alt= " Flu vaccinations. " data-portal-copyright= " Ed Kaiser " **28;25362;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1316125 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; One hundred years after the deadly Spanish flu killed tens of millions of people around the world , the conditions are ripe for another severe flu pandemic , say experts , and Canada is not as ready as it needs to be . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada is not alone . At the biannual Canadian Immunization Conference , being held in Ottawa this week , delegates heard from a leading international expert who warned that more work needs to be done globally before the next severe pandemic. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A focus of the conference was on the Spanish influenza pandemic that killed between 50 million and 100 million people around the world , as many as 50,000 in Canada alone . Canada and other countries are preparing for the next one . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Are we ready in 2018 ? &amp;rdquo ; asked Nancy Cox after giving a brief history of the 1918 pandemic. &amp;ldquo ; The answer is we are better off than we were before 2009 but not where we need to be and vaccine readiness has a long way to go . &amp;rdquo ; Cox headed the U.S. Centres for Disease Control influenza branch and led a World Health Organization centre on influenza control and surveillance . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;25392;TOOLONG ... title= " Influenza victims crowd into an emergency hospital near Fort Riley , Kansas during the 1918 Spanish flu pandemic killed at least 20 million people worldwide . " alt= " Influenza victims crowd into an emergency hospital near Fort Riley , Kansas during the 1918 Spanish flu pandemic killed at least 20 million people worldwide . " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;25428;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1316120 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Despite medical advances since 1918 , neither vaccines nor anti-viral treatments are as effective as they should be and there are new challenges , including disseminating accurate information in the age of social media , conference delegates were told . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , the goal of developing a universal flu vaccine , to replace seasonal vaccines created annually , remains elusive , and seasonal vaccines are often not very effective . And , while there has been progress , gaps remain in global surveillance and in availability of treatments . During a severe pandemic , said Cox , there would not be enough ventilators or reusable respiratory protective devices to support and protect people around the world . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;25458;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Bonnie Henry is British Columbia 's provincial health officer and chairs the Canadian pandemic influenza task group " alt= " Dr. Bonnie Henry is British Columbia 's provincial health officer and chairs the Canadian pandemic influenza task group " data-portal-copyright= " JONATHAN HAYWARD " **28;25494;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1316117 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The 1918 pandemic was exceptionally severe . A similar pandemic today would cause tremendous illness , death and economic cost . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;25524;TOOLONG ... title= " Three Alberta farmers stand in a field wearing masks during the influenza epidemic that swept the world in the fall of 1918. " alt= " Three Alberta farmers stand in a field wearing masks during the influenza epidemic that swept the world in the fall of 1918. " data-portal-copyright= " Staff " **28;25560;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1308739 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cox said estimates range from 105 million to 110 million potential deaths . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; That is a very sobering number . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pandemic influenza is a global outbreak that occurs when a new flu virus for which humans have little or no immunity emerges and spreads easily . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canadian officials have been working to prepare the country for the next pandemic , building on lessons learned during the 2009 H1N1 pandemic , said Bonnie Henry , British Columbia&amp;rsquo ; s provincial health officer who chairs the Canadian pandemic influenza task group . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While progress has been made , gaps remain , she said . And Henry warned that even a pandemic with relatively mild to moderate impact can overwhelm services . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **33;25590;TOOLONG : &amp;nbsp ; &lt;a href= " https : **33;25625;TOOLONG ... to live longer ? There 's a gene for it&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The 2009 pandemic , for example , was considered to be relatively mild compared with other pandemics . But it severely stressed British Columbia&amp;rsquo ; s health system just months before the Winter Olympics were to be held in Vancouver . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; People think of 2009 as being very mild , but if the Olympics had been planned for the fall of 2009 , we would have had to cancel because we did not have ICU ( intensive care ) beds. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada&amp;rsquo ; s pandemic influenza task group aims to minimize serious illness and societal disruptions , to provide a safe and effective vaccine for all Canadians and to monitor the safety and effectiveness of the vaccine , said Henry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;25660;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;25702;TOOLONG ... down the rhetoric , advises former anti-vax **38;25737;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; During the 2009 , there were logistic problems getting vaccines to all parts of the country quickly . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Many of us may remember the challenges of not knowing when vaccines would come , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; A lot of things happened that we were not prepared for that led to inconsistencies . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada now has a contract for two doses of vaccine for every Canadian in the event of a pandemic , along with a backup contract , she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Planning is underway to do a better job of getting vaccine to remote communities and prioritize who gets vaccinated first . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Among areas where more work is needed are effective electronic systems to manage vaccine inventory and track vaccine uptake , among other thing , said Henry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We are still not where we need to be . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While Henry says there is work to be done , Canada is likely in better shape than many countries . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Most countries lack robust pandemic plans , said Cox , and very few countries hold emergency response exercises to ensure they are ready . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS : &lt;/strong&gt; " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;26105;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1272981 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;26135;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ottawa researchers are taking a page from Netflix and Google to help patients , their families and their doctors have informed conversations about death . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Researchers at Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s newly minted National Centre for Individualized Health have developed an algorithm that predicts how many months , or years , patients near the ends of their lives have to live . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is information some people might not be comfortable with , acknowledged Dr. Peter Tanuseputro , an investigator at the Bruy&amp;egrave ; re&amp;nbsp ; Research Institute and family doctor who offers house calls to his palliative patients . But he believes many elderly patients will want to know exactly how long they have to live , information not currently available for most . He also said everyone deserves access to the information , based on an individual&amp;rsquo ; s health information and data collected across Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Among other things the end-of-life calculator can lead to is more appropriate care and more people benefitting from palliative care . Only 15 per cent of Canadians receive palliative care , according to recent data released from the Canadian Institute for Health Information . Even those who do often get it too late , said Tanuseputro. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In Canada , we don&amp;rsquo ; t like to talk about death and dying , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &amp;ldquo ; We think patients have the right to their own information to tell them about what is going to happen to them . We still live in a paternalistic society where we don&amp;rsquo ; t think the patient can handle that information or don&amp;rsquo ; t think they should have this information . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The predictive algorithm , which goes by the acronym RESPECT ( for Risk Evaluation for Support : Predictions for Elder-life in the Community Tool for the End of Life ) , calculates how long a person has to live based on 25 questions about what diseases they have and how difficult it is to care for themselves . The algorithm was developed using data collected through the province&amp;rsquo ; s health system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;26171;TOOLONG ... title= " Heidi Sveistrup , CEO and Chief Science Officer at the Bruy ? ? re Research Institute , and Dr. Peter Tanuseputro , lead scientist at the Institute 's Centre for Individualized Health , are using health data to predict the life span of elderly patients . " alt= " Heidi Sveistrup , CEO and Chief Science Officer at the Bruy ? ? re Research Institute , and Dr. Peter Tanuseputro , lead scientist at the Institute 's Centre for Individualized Health , are using health data to predict the life span of elderly patients . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;26207;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1272982 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is one of the digital health projects being developed by Bruy&amp;egrave ; re aimed at addressing the health needs of people as they age ( more info at &lt;a href= " http : **34;26237;TOOLONG ... ) . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is currently being pilot-tested in Windsor-Essex to help people who are frail understand whether they would benefit from palliative care . It is one of a series of algorithms using big data that is aimed at helping older people better understand their health risk factors in an effort to help them get better treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When they are complete , the tools will be available to individuals and family members on the &lt;a href= " http : //projectbiglife.ca " &gt;projectbig ... website . In addition to calculating how close a person is to death , researchers are developing tools that will help calculate how long before an individual is likely to end up in a nursing home , and their risk of hospitalization. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Those tools can lead to preventative measures to help people stay in their homes longer , in some cases , or to get more appropriate care . Their development is part of the National Centre for Individualized Health , affiliated with Bruy&amp;egrave ; re&amp;nbsp ; Research Institute , which was created with the help of donations totalling $1.25 million . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Tanuseputro said the use of big data to create predictive tools has the potential to change individual treatment and health care more generally . The health system is just catching up to what has been done elsewhere . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If you watch Netflix , it will tell you what Netflix thinks you like based on your previous choices . In health care , we are just figuring this out , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; These tools are to help patients make informed decisions . Too often , you visit your doctor and your doctor has only limited amount of time and limited information . &amp;rdquo ; While they might have a patient&amp;rsquo ; s electronic medical data , that would not include what happened , for example , if the patient was in a hospital 100 kilometres away . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Among information patients are asked for in the 25 questions that feed into the predictive algorithm , are questions about activities of daily living , such as eating , bathing , personal hygiene and toiletting . Those , he said , are often a better indication that a patient does not have long to live , than a diagnosis of chronic illness . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He said physicians are sometimes hesitant to tell patients they are close to death &amp;mdash ; in part , because it is difficult to be accurate . This tool should change that , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We think patients have the right to know and seek out care that is appropriate to their needs . If a patient knows that they have a year to live , they should be receiving palliative care , supportive care , in order to stay at home . Clinicians don&amp;rsquo ; t do it enough , partly because we live in a death-denying society and partly because it is hard , especially if you don&amp;rsquo ; t have a terminal cancer diagnosis . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneFri , 12 Oct 2018 14:31:01 **45;26273;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal **33;26320;TOOLONG , cruel thing to do ' : Second Kingston retirement residence forced to vacatehttp : **34;26355;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;26391;TOOLONG ... title= " Ellen Toxopeus , a former resident at the Fairfield Manor East Retirement Residence in Kingston , is seen here in a photo taken Friday . Steph Crosier/Postmedia " data-portal-copyright= " Steph Crosier " **28;26427;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1256429 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fairfield Manor East has been vacated by Kingston Fire and Rescue , leaving its elderly residents and their families scrambling for care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We have made extraordinary efforts to work with the building&amp;rsquo ; s owner to ensure the fire safety for these vulnerable residents without success , &amp;rdquo ; Deputy Chief Kevin Donaldson said in a news release sent late Thursday . &amp;ldquo ; Compliance with the Ontario Fire Code remains the responsibility of the owner . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On Labour Day , &amp;nbsp ; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26457;TOOLONG ... fire department ordered Fairfield Manor West to evacuate for similar reasons&lt;/a&gt; . Both Fairfield Manors are owned by Muhammad Qazi of Q &amp;amp ; Sons Management . Qazi has not replied to repeated requests for comment on the situation . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;26492;TOOLONG ... title= " David Payne gathers mail for residents of the Fairfield Manor East Retirement Residence in Kingston on Friday . Steph Crosier/Postmedia " data-portal-copyright= " Steph Crosier " **28;26528;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1256428 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Misbah Haleem , a real estate broker in Mississauga , was once manager of the Fairfield Manor East . He would not speak to The Kingston Whig-Standard , stating only that he had been told to send media requests to Qazi. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When a journalist arrived at the Manor on Friday afternoon , resident David Payne was walking out to get the mail , which hadn&amp;rsquo ; t been collected in three days . It contained a large collection of mail for various residents . It&amp;rsquo ; s not his job to do this , &amp;ldquo ; but somebody has got to . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A week ago , Payne went above and beyond and spent $510 to feed fellow residents . When Qazi arrived Tuesday , he offered Payne a cheque , but Payne pointed to a TD Canada Trust branch down the street , &amp;ldquo ; No , you&amp;rsquo ; re getting me cash . &amp;rdquo ; Payne said Qazi went to the bank and obtained the cash to pay him right away . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; That cheque would have bounced from here to Africa , &amp;rdquo ; Payne said Friday . Payne was told that morning that he&amp;rsquo ; d have to be out of his unit by the end of the day . Fortunately , he has been offered a room at another residence in the city . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s just been a mix-up for everybody . They don&amp;rsquo ; t know what&amp;rsquo ; s going on . It&amp;rsquo ; s the residents that I&amp;rsquo ; m concerned about , &amp;rdquo ; Payne said . &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t care about anybody else . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Daughter Judy Bernard called the whole situation &amp;ldquo ; heartbreaking . &amp;rdquo ; She used to visit her mother , 95-year-old Etta Garnhum , daily , but now the only spot they could find for her is in Madoc . Bernard&amp;rsquo ; s daughter and granddaughter live there , so Garnhum will still have visitors , but Bernard will need to limit her own visits to maybe twice a week . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Everybody is very stressed , &amp;rdquo ; Bernard said . &amp;ldquo ; Most of these people , their loved ones are also seniors . It&amp;rsquo ; s stressful for relatives , it&amp;rsquo ; s very stressful for the patients and it&amp;rsquo ; s a brutal , cruel thing to do . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Two firefighters had been at the Fairfield Manor East location since Monday , ensuring new staff were trained in the facility&amp;rsquo ; s fire safety and evacuation plan . On Tuesday , Fire Chief Shawn Armstrong called that move &amp;ldquo ; completely out of the ordinary . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Donaldson said Friday that the final straw was Qazi&amp;rsquo ; s inability to maintain the home&amp;rsquo ; s fire safety protocols . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The owner&amp;rsquo ; s inability to demonstrate that he could provide sufficient , trained supervisory staff to provide the safety and the security to the residents , &amp;rdquo ; Donaldson said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The South East Local Health Integration Network ( LHIN ) is working to relocate 23 residents who had been living in the residence , located off Gore Road . They did the same with nine residents who had been living at the west-end location . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;26558;TOOLONG ... title= " Ron Toxopeus with all his mother 's belongings , ready to leave the Fairfield Manor East Retirement Residence in Kingston on Friday .
